On orthogonality to uniquely ergodic systems
M. Górska, M. Lemańczyk, T. de la Rue

Abstract
We solve Boshernitzan’s problem of characterization (in terms of so called Furstenberg systems) of bounded sequences that are orthogonal to all uniquely ergodic systems. As a step toward this solution, we provide a characterization of automorphisms which are disjoint from all ergodic ones as those whose a.a. ergodic components form a family of pairwise disjoint automorphisms. Some variations of Boshernitzan’s problem involving characteristic classes are considered. As an application, we characterize sequences orthogonal to all uniquely ergodic systems whose (unique) invariant measure yields a discrete spectrum automorphism as those satisfying an averaged Chowla property.



1
Introduction

1.1
General background and main notations

Throughout
{\mathbb{N}}:=\{1,2,\ldots\}
stands for the set of natural numbers

We recall that a
standard Borel space
is a measurable space whose sigma-algebra consists of the Borel sets for some Polish metric. If
X
is such a space then its sigma-algebra is denoted by
{\cal B}_{X}
.

If
X
is a standard Borel space, then
M(X)
is the space of all Borel probability measures on
X
, equipped with the sigma-algebra generated by all evaluation maps

\mu\mapsto\mu(B)\quad(B\in\mathcal{B}_{X}).

With this sigma-algebra,
M(X)
is again Borel standard. If
Y
is another measurable space, then a
probability kernel
from
Y
to
X
is a measurable map from
Y
to
M(X)
.

We consider measurable invertible transformations
T
acting on a standard Borel space
(X,{\cal B}_{X})
. We denote by
\mathcal{I}_{T}\subset{\cal B}_{X}
the sub-sigma-algebra of
T
-invariant Borel sets:

\mathcal{I}_{T}\ :=\ \{A\in{\cal B}_{X}:T^{-1}A=A\}.

We denote by
M(X,T)\subset M(X)
the subspace of
T
-invariant probability measures. A measure
\mu\in M(X,T)
is said to be
ergodic
(for
T
) if
\mu
only gives values 0 or 1 to sets in
\mathcal{I}_{T}
. Let
M^{e}(X,T)\subset M(X,T)
stand for the subspace of ergodic
T
-invariant measures. Both
M(X,T)
and
M^{e}(X,T)
are Borel subsets of
M(X)
.
When
T
preserves a probability measure
\mu
on
(X,{\cal B}_{X})
, we call
T
an
automorphism
of the standard Borel probability space
(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu)
, and
(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu,T)
is called a
measure-preserving system
. (We often omit
{\cal B}_{X}
and write only
(X,\mu,T)
.) For a given
\mu\in M(X)
, the group of automorphisms of
(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu)
(in which two transformations are identified if they agree outside a
\mu
-negligible set) is denoted by
{\rm Aut}(X,{\cal B}_{X},\mu)
. It is a Polish group when considered with the smallest topology (called
strong
) making all the maps
T\mapsto f\circ T
,
f\in L^{2}(X,\mu)
, continuous.

By a
topological system

(X,T)
, we mean the action of a homeomorphism
T
on a compact metric space
X
.
Given a topological system
(X,T)
,
M(X)
endowed with the weak
∗
-topology is compact, and both
M(X,T)
and
M^{e}(X,T)
are non-empty subspaces of
M(X)
with
M(X,T)
being closed and
M^{e}(X,T)
being
G_{\delta}
. Then, each
\mu\in M(X,T)
yields a measure-preserving system

(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu,T)
.

If for
i=1,2
,
(X_{i},\mathcal{B}_{X_{i}},\mu_{i},T_{i})
is a measure-preserving system, we can consider
J(T_{1},T_{2})
the set of
joinings
, i.e. of all
T_{1}\times T_{2}
-invariant measures on
X_{1}\times X_{2}
with marginals
\mu_{1}
and
\mu_{2}
, respectively. Then, the two automorphisms are called (Furstenberg)
disjoint
(we write
T_{1}\perp T_{2}
) if
J(T_{1},T_{2})=\{\mu_{1}\otimes\mu_{2}\}
. It is not hard to see that if
T_{1}\perp T_{2}
then at least one of the two automorphisms has to be ergodic. For a single measure-preserving system
(X,\mu,T)
, we write
J_{2}(T)
instead of
J(T,T)
.

1.2
Presentation of the main problem and motivations

In the present paper, we will study the following problem of
orthogonality
: Given a class
\mathscr{C}
of topological systems and a (bounded) sequence
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}:=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\colon|z|\leq 1\}
of zero mean, i.e.
M(\boldsymbol{u}):=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}\boldsymbol{u}(n)=0
, we say that
\boldsymbol{u}
is orthogonal to
\mathscr{C}
,
\boldsymbol{u}\perp\mathscr{C}
, if

(1)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}f(T^{n}x)\boldsymbol{u}(n)=0

for each
(X,T)\in\mathscr{C}
,
f\in C(X)
and all
x\in X
(we write then
\boldsymbol{u}\perp(X,T)
). The main motivation to study this problem is the celebrated Sarnak’s conjecture
1
1

1

Sarnak’s conjecture is in turn motivated by the Chowla conjecture from 1965 which predicts that the autocorrelations of the Liouville function vanish, i.e. the Liouville function is a generic point (see Section
2.1
) for the Bernoulli measure
B(1/2,1/2)
for the full shift
\{-1,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}}
; the Chowla conjecture implies Sarnak’s conjecture
[
33
]
,
[
36
]
,
[
9
]
.

[
33
]
in which
\boldsymbol{u}
is the Möbius (or Liouville) function and
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{\rm ZE}
the class of (topological) zero entropy systems.
We can also study the problem of orthogonality without the zero-mean assumption for
\boldsymbol{u}
. Namely, by
\boldsymbol{u}\perp(X,T)
, we mean uncorrelation:

(2)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}f(T^{n}x)\boldsymbol{u}(n)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}f(T^{n}x)\cdot\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}\boldsymbol{u}(n)+o(1)

(when
N\to\infty
) for all
f\in C(X)
and
x\in X
. Note that, if
(X,T)
is uniquely ergodic, i.e. if it has a unique
T
-invariant probability measure (by unicity, such a measure has to be ergodic), the above requirement is equivalent to
\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}f(T^{n}x)\boldsymbol{u}(n)=0
for all
f\in C(X)
of zero mean (with respect to the unique invariant measure) and
x\in X
. Moreover, if
M(\boldsymbol{u})=0
, we come back to (
1
), although we will see later that the natural (stronger than
M(\boldsymbol{u})=0
) assumption on
\boldsymbol{u}
is that the first Gowers-Host-Kra norm vanishes:
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
.

As done by Tao
[
37
]
, we can change the Cesàro averaging in (
1
), by the logarithmic averages (the logarithmic mean of
\boldsymbol{u}
is zero whenever its usual mean vanishes) and study the corresponding orthogonality problem:

(3)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{n\leq N}\frac{1}{n}f(T^{n}x)\boldsymbol{u}(n)=0

which, in particular, leads to the logarithmic Sarnak’s conjecture.
While even the logarithmic Sarnak’s conjecture stays open, a remarkable theorem by Franzikinakis-Host
[
11
]
asserts that the Liouville function
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
(
\boldsymbol{\lambda}(n)=-1
if the number of prime divisors (counted with multiplicity) is odd, and it is 1 otherwise) and many other multiplicative functions are logarithmically orthogonal to all uniquely ergodic zero entropy systems. A natural question arises
to characterize those sequences which are orthogonal (or logarithmically orthogonal) to the class UE of all uniquely ergodic systems. In
[
6
]
, this particular problem is attributed to M. Boshernitzan, and from now on we will call it
Boshernitzan’s problem
.
Conze, Downarowicz and Serafin showed in
[
6
]
that Boshernitzan’s problem is non-trivial by noticing that sequences “produced” by systems which are disjoint in the Furstenberg sense from all ergodic automorphisms are orthogonal to the UE class. This path of reasoning, relating the fundamental problem of Furstenberg disjointness of measure-preserving systems in ergodic theory
[
15
]
with the problem of orthogonality to sequences is not new and it was applied earlier, when studying Sarnak’s conjecture, in particular, it was successfully applied in
[
11
]
and many other papers (see the survey
[
9
]
). Let us quickly recall the essence of this approach.

Given
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
(
{\mathbb{D}}
stands for the unit disc; we freely treat
\boldsymbol{u}
as a two-sided sequence, e.g. by setting
\boldsymbol{u}(-n)=\boldsymbol{u}(n)
,
\boldsymbol{u}(0)=1
), we consider
X_{\boldsymbol{u}}:=\overline{\{S^{k}\boldsymbol{u}\colon k\in{\mathbb{Z}}\}}
the subshift generated by
\boldsymbol{u}
, where
S:{\mathbb{D}}^{{\mathbb{Z}}}\to{\mathbb{D}}^{{\mathbb{Z}}}
,
Sy(n)=y(n+1)
, for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, stands for the left shift. Let

V(\boldsymbol{u}):=\Big\{\kappa\in M(X_{\boldsymbol{u}})\colon\Big(\exists N_{k}\uparrow\infty\Big)\;\kappa=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\delta_{S^{n}\boldsymbol{u}}\Big\}\subset M(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},S)

denote the set of
\boldsymbol{u}
-
visible
measures. (
V^{\rm log}(\boldsymbol{u})\subset M(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},S)
is defined analogously; in general, the sets of visible and logarithmically visible measures can be disjoint, see, e.g.,
[
17
]
.)
By a
Furstenberg system
of
\boldsymbol{u}
, we mean the measure-preserving system
(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
, for each
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
.
Suppose now that we understand (in the sense of ergodic properties) all Furstenberg systems of
\boldsymbol{u}
. Then, if we take any topological system
(X,T)
for which for each
\nu\in M(X,T)
, the measure-preserving systems
(X,\nu,T)
is Furstenberg disjoint to all
(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
for all
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
, then it is not hard to see that

\boldsymbol{u}\perp(X,T).

In fact, to achieve this goal, as proved in
[
23
]
, we need a condition appealing
only
to Furstenberg systems of
\boldsymbol{u}
, namely, we need to see that the function
\pi_{0}:X_{\boldsymbol{u}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
,
\pi_{0}((x_{k})):=x_{0}
is orthogonal to the
L^{2}
-space of a certain factor
{\cal A}(\kappa)
of
(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
– the factor
{\cal A}(\kappa)
, called
characteristic
, will depend on the class
\mathscr{C}
to which
(X,T)
belongs:

(4)

\pi_{0}\perp L^{2}({\cal A}(\kappa))\text{ for all }\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u}).

This is a particular instance of the Veech condition (for the definition of the Veech condition in the general characteristic class setup, see Section
6.5
). For Sarnak’s conjecture (when
\mathscr{C}=\mathscr{C}_{\rm ZE})
the relevant conjecture
2
2

2

Veech’s conjecture was proved recently in
[
23
]
.
was formulated by Veech
[
38
]
and stated that, for each Furstenberg system
\kappa
of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
,
\pi_{0}
is orthogonal to the
L^{2}
-space of the Pinsker factor
\Pi(\kappa)
(i.e. the largest factor with zero Kolmogorov-Sinai entropy).

All the above can be repeated in the logarithmic case, and in
[
10
]
it is in fact proved that either a Furstenberg system of
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
is ergodic (then the Chowla conjecture holds), or it must be disjoint from all ergodic systems with zero entropy. The latter naturally raises a question (of independent interest in ergodic theory) how to describe the class Erg
⟂
of automorphisms disjoint from all ergodic automorphisms.
3
3

3

The reader can easily check that although the disjointness theory in ergodic theory is well developed, see e.g. the monograph
[
16
]
, but it usually considers the disjointness question between two ergodic automorphisms. Indeed, in this case, the problem is reduced to show that each ergodic joining is product measure.

We now pass to a description of the results of the paper.

1.3
Ergodic theory results

Let
T
be an automorphism of
(X,{\cal B}_{X},\mu)
. We are interested in the disjointness condition of
T
with the class of all ergodic automorphisms (which we denote by
T\in{\rm Erg}^{\perp}
or
T\perp{\rm Erg}
).
Therefore, we focus on the case where the system is
non-ergodic
, and consider its ergodic decomposition:

(5)

\mu\ =\ \int_{X}\mu_{x}d\mu(x),

that is, the disintegration of
\mu
over the sub-sigma-algebra
\mathcal{I}_{T}
of
T
-invariant subsets. Here,
x\mapsto\mu_{x}
is a measurable map from
X
to
M^{e}(X,T)
, and the sigma-algebra it generates coincides modulo
\mu
with
\mathcal{I}_{T}
.
Then (up to a
\mu
-negligible set), we can write
X
as a disjoint union

X\ =\ \bigsqcup_{n\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}\cup\{\infty\}}X_{n},

where, for
n\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}
,
X_{n}
is the subset of
x\in X
such that
\mu_{x}
is concentrated on
n
points with equal mass
1/n
(and those
n
points are cyclicly permutted by
T
), and
X_{\infty}
stands for the subset of
x\in X
such that
\mu_{x}
is non-atomic. We set

\bar{X}\ :=\ \{\mu_{x}:x\in X\}\subset M^{e}(X,T),

equipped with the pushforward image
\bar{\mu}
of
\mu
. We can view
\bar{X}
as an abstract space which is introduced to embody the “identity” part of
T
:
(X,\mu,T)
appears as a relatively ergodic extension of the identity system
(\bar{X},\bar{\mu},{\rm Id}_{\bar{X}})
. We also set, for
n\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}\cup\{\infty\}
,

\bar{X}_{n}\ :=\ \{\mu_{x}:x\in X_{n}\}\subset\bar{X},

so that

\bar{X}\ =\ \bigsqcup_{n\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}\cup\{\infty\}}\bar{X}_{n}.

For
n\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}
, we introduce the finite ergodic system
\bigl(\{1,\ldots,n\},\nu_{n},R_{n}\big)
, where
\nu_{n}
is the uniform probability distribution over
\{1,\ldots,n\}
and
R_{n}j:=j+1\mod n
. We also introduce an abstract standard Borel probability space
(Y,{\cal B}_{Y},\nu)
, where
\nu
is non-atomic.
Then the system
(X,\mu,T)
can be represented, up to a measure-theoretic isomorphism, as follows:

•

For
n\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}
,
X_{n}=\bar{X}_{n}\times\{1,\ldots,n\}
, and for
x=(\bar{x},j)\in X_{n}
,
Tx=(x,R_{n}j)
. Moreover,

\mu|_{X_{n}}\ =\ \bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{n}}\otimes\nu_{n}.

•

X_{\infty}=\bar{X}_{\infty}\times Y
, and for
x=(\bar{x},y)\in X_{\infty}
,
Tx=(\bar{x},T_{\bar{x}}y)
, where the automorphisms
T_{\bar{x}}\in{\rm Aut}(Y,\nu)
,
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}
, are ergodic. Here, the map

(6)

\bar{X}_{\infty}\ni\bar{x}\mapsto T_{\bar{x}}\in{\rm Aut}(Y,\nu)\text{ is Borel}.

Moreover,

\mu|_{X_{\infty}}\ =\ \bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}}\otimes\nu.

To standardize our notations, we will also set
T_{\bar{x}}:=R_{n}
for
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}_{n}
. This allows us to rewrite the ergodic decomposition (
5
) as

(7)

\mu=\int_{\bar{X}}\mu_{\bar{x}}\,d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x}),

where the conditional measures
\bar{\mu}_{\bar{x}}
are now either
\delta_{\bar{x}}\otimes\nu_{n}
if
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}_{n}
or
\delta_{\bar{x}}\otimes\nu
whenever
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}_{\infty}
.

Theorem A.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(i)
T\in{\rm Erg}^{\perp}
.

(ii) The extension
T\to{\rm Id}_{\bar{X}}
is confined (i.e. the only member
\lambda\in J_{2}(T)
such that
\lambda|_{\bar{X}\times\bar{X}}=\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
is equal to
\mu\otimes\mu
, see Subsection
2.7
).

(iii)
T_{\bar{x}}\perp T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}
for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
.
4
4

4

The implication (iii)
\Rightarrow
(i), using the approach from
[
23
]
, can be thought of to be more or less implicit in
[
6
]
.

(For the proof of Theorem A, see Theorem
3.1
and its proof.)

We refer to Section
2.10
for the definition of a characteristic class
{\cal F}
of measure-preserving systems, and the associated largest
{\cal F}
-factor
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)
in any system
(X,\mu,T)
. Recall that the class ZE of zero-entropy systems is the largest proper characteristic class
[
23
]
, and that the largest ZE-factor
{\cal A}_{\rm ZE}(X,\mu,T)
is the Pinsker factor
\Pi(T)
.

Theorem B.

(i) Let
{\cal F}
be a characteristic class. Then
T\perp{\cal F}\cap{\rm Erg}

if and only if the extension
T|_{{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}}\to{\rm Id}_{\bar{X}}
is confined (
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}
is the largest
{\cal F}
-factor of
T
).

(ii)
T\perp{\rm ZE}\cap{\rm Erg}
if and only if

\Pi(T_{\bar{x}})\perp\Pi(T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}})
for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
.

(For the proof of Theorem B, see Corollary
6.2
, Corollary
6.5
and their proofs.)

Let
J_{2}^{\rm RelErg}(T)
stand for the subset of those members
\lambda
of
J_{2}(T)
whose marginal on
\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
is
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
, and which is relatively ergodic over the factor
(\bar{X}\times\bar{X},\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu},{\rm Id}_{\bar{X}\times\bar{X}})
, i.e.
\mathcal{I}_{T\times T}=\mathcal{I}_{T}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{T}
mod
\lambda
.
The next theorem refers to the weakly ergodic subspace
F_{\rm we}(T)\subset L^{2}(\mu)
, whose precise definition is given in Subsection
2.4
. For
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
, being orthogonal to
F_{\rm we}(T)
means the absence of correlation of
f
with any function
g
defined on
Z
in any joining of
T
with an ergodic system
(Z,\rho,R)
. We recall that
\mathcal{I}_{T\times T}
stands for the sub-sigma-algebra of
T\times T
-invariant Borel subsets of
X\times X
.

Theorem C.

Assume that
f\in L^{2}(X,\mu)
, and
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{T}]=0
. The following conditions are equivalent:

(i)
f\perp F_{\rm we}(T)
.

(ii)
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[f\otimes\overline{f}\,|\,\bar{X}\times\bar{X}]=0
mod
\mu\otimes\mu
for all
\lambda\in J_{2}^{\rm RelErg}(T)
.

(iii)
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[f\otimes\overline{f}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{T\times T}]=0
mod
\lambda
for all
\lambda\in J_{2}(T)
,
\lambda|_{\bar{X}\times\bar{X}}=\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
.

(For the proof of Theorem C, see Theorem
4.5
and Corollary
4.10
and their proofs.)

1.4
Solution of Boshernitzan’s problem

Let
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
.

Proposition D.

\boldsymbol{u}\perp\,{\rm UE}
if and only if for each Furstenberg system
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
,
\pi_{0}\perp F_{{\rm we},0}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
.

(For the proof, see Proposition
5.1
and its proof.)

In Proposition
8.1
, we describe combinatorially all self-joinings of Furstenberg systems of
\boldsymbol{u}
. Hence, Proposition D, Theorem C together with the von Neumann theorem describe the full solution of Boshernitzan’s problem in terms of Furstenberg systems of
\boldsymbol{u}
, see Remark
4.11
. Proposition D has its characteristic class version counterpart (
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}={\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(\kappa)
below stands for the largest characteristic
{\cal F}
-factor of a Furstenberg system
(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
of
\boldsymbol{u}
):

Corollary E.

For any characteristic class
{\cal F}
, the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)
\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\cal F}\cap{\rm UE}
.

(b) For each Furstenberg system
\kappa
of
\boldsymbol{u}
, for each
\lambda\in J^{\rm RelErg}_{2}(S|_{{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}},\kappa|_{{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}})
such that
\lambda|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{S}}=\kappa|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}}\otimes\kappa|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}}
, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}\Bigl[{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}]\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}]\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{S}\Bigr]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}]\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}].

(c) For each Furstenberg system
\kappa
of
\boldsymbol{u}
, for each
\lambda\in J_{2}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
such that
\lambda|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{S}}=\kappa|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}}\otimes\kappa|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}}
, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}\Bigl[{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}]\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}]\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S\times S}\Bigr]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}]\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}].

(For the proof, see Corollary
6.10
and its proof.)

Given a characteristic class
{\cal F}
, we denote by
\mathscr{C}_{{\cal F}}
the class of topological systems for which all visible invariant measures yield automorphisms in
{\cal F}
. (An invariant measure
\mu
in
(X,T)
is
visible
if there exists
x\in X
which is quasi-generic for
\mu
, i.e.
\mu\in V(x)
.) The main result of
[
23
]
stated that the Veech condition (see Section
6.5
) is
equivalent
to the Sarnak condition:
\boldsymbol{u}\perp\mathscr{C}_{{\cal F}}
,
provided that
{\cal F}
is a so called ec-class
(see Section
2.10
). It was left as an open problem whether the equivalence of these two conditions holds for any characteristic class. It turns out that this is not the case as the following result shows:

Proposition F.

There exist a characteristic class
{\cal F}
and
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to\{-1,1\}
which is generic for a measure
\kappa
on
\{-1,1\}^{{\mathbb{Z}}}
such that
\boldsymbol{u}\perp\mathscr{C}_{{\cal F}}
but the Veech condition fails.

(For the proof, see Section
6.5
.)

1.5
Applications

We refer to Subsection
4.1
and Subsection
6.6
for the definitions of Gowers-Host-Kra (GHK) norms
\|\cdot\|_{u^{s}}
and of an averaged Chowla property
5
5

5

An averaged Chowla property (even in a quantitative version) has been proved for the Liouville function (and many other multiplicative functions) by Matomäki-Radziwiłł-Tao
[
30
]
.
. Denote by DISP the (characteristic) class of automorphisms having discrete spectrum.
Our main application is the following result:

Corollary G.
Let
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
,
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
.
6
6

6

This condition is equivalent to so called “zero mean property on typical short interval”, cf. Proposition
4.1
.
The following conditions are equivalent:

(i)
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfies an averaged Chowla property.

(ii)
\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}\cap{\rm DISP}
.

(iii)
\boldsymbol{u}\perp\mathscr{C}_{\rm DISP}
.

(For the proof, see Theorem
6.26
and its proof.)

The equivalence between (ii) and (iii) seems to be exceptional for the class DISP, we do not expect it to hold for a general characteristic class. It obviously does not hold for the class of all automorphisms but more interestingly, it does not hold for the class ZE. Indeed, for all sequences
\boldsymbol{u}
of the form (
42
), we have
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
by Remark
5.12
,
and they satisfy
\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}\cap{\rm ZE}
. However, by Remark
5.13
, they do not satisfy (iii), as their own topological entropy is zero. Denote by CE the class of topological systems whose set of ergodic invariant measures is countable. In light of what was said, the following result looks somewhat surprising.

Corollary H.
Let
{\cal F}
be an arbitrary characteristic class. Let
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
,
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
. Then
\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}\cap{\cal F}
if and only if
\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm CE}\cap{\cal F}
.

(For the proof, see Proposition
6.11
and its proof. The class
{\rm CE}\cap{\cal F}
is defined unambiguously due to Proposition
2.17
.)

It follows that in the theorem of Frantzikinakis-Host
[
11
]
, the final statement of orthogonality (in the logarithmic sense) of the Liouville function to all zero entropy systems from CE, is in fact equivalent to the orthogonality to all zero entropy uniquely ergodic systems.
Recall that
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
is called
mean slowly varying
if

(8)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}|\boldsymbol{u}(n)-\boldsymbol{u}(n+1)|=0.

Corollary I.
The mean slowly varying functions are multipliers of UE
⟂
. That is, if
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
is mean slowly varying and
\boldsymbol{v}\perp{\rm UE}
then
\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\perp{\rm UE}
, where
(\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v})(n)=\boldsymbol{u}(n)\cdot\boldsymbol{v}(n)
for
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

(For the proof, see Proposition
5.19
and its proof.)

Here, it seems that
the class of mean slowly varying function should precisely be the class of multipliers of UE
⟂
.

The next fact is for multiplicative functions (see Section
5.2
for some basic facts on such functions).

Corollary J.
The only pretentious multiplicative functions
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
orthogonal to UE are Archimedean characters
n\mapsto n^{it}
.

(For the proof, see Corollary
5.6
.)

It is reasonable to expect that the result holds in the class of all multiplicative functions.

1.6
Auxiliary results

In the last three sections we prove some new ergodic results which are used to obtain the aforementioned theorems, but which are also of an independent interest. Namely, in Section
8
, we provide a combinatorial description of all self-joinings of all Furstenberg systems of a bounded arithmetic function
\boldsymbol{u}
. In Section
9
, the existence of a so-called
relative ergodic decomposition
, with respect to a sub-sigma-algebra of
\mathcal{I}_{T}
is established (the proof has been written by Tim Austin). Finally, in Section
10
, we show that the “trace” of the Pinsker factor of an automorphism on almost all ergodic component is equal to the Pinsker factor of the ergodic component. This result seems to be a part of folklore, but we could not find its proof in the literature.

2
Preparatory material

2.1
Lifting lemma

Given a topological system
(Y,S)
and
\kappa\in M(Y)
, we say that a point
u\in Y
is
quasi-generic along a sequence

(N_{k})
for
\kappa
if

\frac{1}{N_{m}}\sum_{n\leq N_{m}}g(S^{n}u)\to\int_{Y}g\,d\nu
for all
g\in C(Y)
.
If the convergence takes place for the whole sequence of natural numbers, then
u
is called
generic
(for
\kappa
).
We will need the following lemma about lifting of quasi-generic point (along a subsequence) to quasi-generic sequences:

Theorem 2.1

(
[
23
]
)
.

Assume that
(Y,S)
and
(X,T)
are topological systems. Let
\nu\in M(X,T)
,
u\in Y
be quasi-generic along an increasing sequence
(N_{m})
for
\kappa\in M(Y,S)
and
\rho\in J(\kappa,\nu)
. Then there exist a sequence
(x_{n})\subset X
and a subsequence
(N_{m_{\ell}})
such that
(S^{n}u,x_{n})
is quasi-generic along
(N_{m_{\ell}})
for
\rho
, i.e.
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{m_{\ell}}}\sum_{n\leq N_{m_{\ell}}}G(S^{n}u,x_{n})=\int_{Y\times X}G\,d\rho
for each
G\in C(Y\times X)
, and the set
\{n\geq 1:\>x_{n}\neq Tx_{n-1}\}
is of the form
\{b_{1}<b_{2}<\cdots\}
with
b_{k+1}-b_{k}\to\infty
when
k\to\infty
.

Remark 2.2
.

As proved in
[
23
]
, this result is also true for the logarithmic averages.

Remark 2.3
.

First lifting lemmas of that kind can be found in
[
22
]
,
[
6
]
.

2.2
Orbital uniquely ergodic models

We will also need the following:

Theorem 2.4

(
[
1
]
)
.

Assume that
(X,T)
is uniquely ergodic and let
(x_{n})\subset X
satisfy
\{n\geq 0:\>x_{n+1}\neq Tx_{n}\}
is of the form
(b_{k})
with
b_{k+1}-b_{k}\to\infty
when
k\to\infty
. Consider
\underline{x}:=(x_{n})\in X^{{\mathbb{N}}}
, the latter space with the one-sided shift
S
. Set
Y:=\overline{\{S^{n}\underline{x}:\>n\geq 0\}}\subset X^{{\mathbb{N}}}
. Then the subshift
(Y,S)
is uniquely ergodic and (measure-theoretically) isomorphic to the original system.

2.3
Joinings and Markov operators

Given measure-preserving systems
(Z,{\cal B}_{Z},\nu,R)
,
(Z^{\prime},{\cal B}_{Z^{\prime}},\nu^{\prime},R^{\prime})
and a joining
\rho\in J(R^{\prime},R)
, we let
\Phi_{\rho}
denote the corresponding operator
\Phi_{\rho}:L^{2}(Z^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\to L^{2}(Z,\nu)
defined by

(9)

\int_{Z}\Phi_{\rho}(f^{\prime})\overline{f}\,d\nu=\int_{Z^{\prime}\times Z}f^{\prime}(z^{\prime})\overline{f(z)}\,d\rho(z^{\prime},z)

for all
f^{\prime}\in L^{2}(Z^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})
and
f\in L^{2}(Z,\nu)
.
Then
\Phi_{\rho}
is Markov:
\Phi_{\rho}\mathbbm{1}=\Phi_{\rho}^{\ast}\mathbbm{1}=\mathbbm{1}
and
\Phi_{\rho}(g^{\prime})\geq 0
whenever
g^{\prime}\geq 0
. Moreover,
\Phi_{\rho}
is intertwining
R
and
R^{\prime}
:
\Phi_{\rho}\circ R^{\prime}=R\circ\Phi_{\rho}
. And vice versa: if
\Phi:L^{2}(Z^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\to L^{2}(Z,\nu)
is a Markov operator intertwining
R
and
R^{\prime}
, then
\Phi=\Phi_{\rho}
for a unique joining
\rho\in J(R^{\prime},R)
, see e.g.
[
16
]
, Chapter 6.

2.4
Weakly ergodic part of a measure-preserving system

Given a measure-preserving system
(X,{\cal B}_{X},\mu,T)
we call
f\in L^{2}(X,{\cal B}_{X},\mu)

weakly ergodic
if there exists an ergodic
(X^{\prime},{\cal B}_{X^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime},T^{\prime})
and a joining
\rho\in J(T^{\prime},T)
such that
f\in{\rm Im}(\Phi_{\rho})
, where
\Phi_{\rho}:L^{2}(X^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\to L^{2}(X,\mu)
is the Markov operator corresponding to
\rho
. The closed subspace
F_{\rm we}(T)
spanned by the weakly ergodic elements is called the
weakly ergodic
part of
L^{2}(X,\mu)
for
T
. Note that

(10)

If
f\perp F_{\rm we}(T)
then
f\in L^{2}_{0}(X,\mu)
, i.e.
\int_{X}f\,d\mu=0
.

Moreover,

(11)

f\perp F_{\rm we}(T)
iff
\Phi_{\rho^{\prime}}(f)=0

for all ergodic
T^{\prime}
and
\rho^{\prime}\in J(T,T^{\prime})
.

In what follows when considering the problem of orthogonality, unless stated otherwise, we will only consider the zero mean functions.
In order to treat the non-zero mean case, we consider the space
F_{{\rm we},0}(T)
generated
by
{\rm Im}(\Phi_{\rho}|_{L^{2}_{0}(X^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})})
for all ergodic
T^{\prime}
.

Remark 2.5
.

If a system
(X,{\cal B}_{X},\mu,T)
is non-ergodic then
F_{\rm we}(T)
is never dense in it. Indeed, take any non-trivial factor
{\cal A}\subset{\cal B}_{X}
of
T
which belongs to

{\rm Erg}^{\perp}
(we can take for
{\cal A}
the sigma-algebra of invariant sets). If
g\in L^{2}_{0}({\cal A})
then
\int\Phi_{\rho}(f^{\prime})\overline{g}\,d\mu=\int f^{\prime}\otimes\overline{g}\,d\rho=0
, the latter because
T^{\prime}\perp T|_{{\cal A}}
.

2.5
Koopman operator and spectral measures

Given a measure-preserving system
(X,{\cal B}_{X},\mu,T)
, recall that we have the corresponding Koopman operator (denoted also by
T
) acting on
L^{2}(Z,\mathcal{D},\nu)
by

f\mapsto Tf:=f\circ T.

This operator is unitary. Given
f\in L^{2}(\mu)
, we denote by
\sigma_{f}
(or
\sigma_{f,T}
if an ambiguity can arise) the
spectral measure
of
f
which is a finite positive (Borel) measure on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
determined by

\widehat{\sigma}(n):=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{1}}z^{n}\,d\sigma_{f}(z)=\int_{X}T^{n}(f)\cdot\overline{f}\,d\mu\text{ for all }n\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

Then,
f
is an eigenfunction corresponding to an eigenvalue
e^{2\pi i\alpha}
of
T
if and only if its spectral measure is atomic concentrated at
e^{2\pi i\alpha}
.
T
is said to have discrete spectrum if the space
L^{2}(Z,\mathcal{D},\nu)
is spanned by the eigenfunctions of
T
. If
T
is ergodic then its eigenvalues form a (countable) subgroup of
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

2.6
Furstenberg systems of arithmetic functions

Given
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
, by
V(\boldsymbol{u})
, we denote the set of shift-invariant measures on
(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},{\cal B}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}}))
for which
\boldsymbol{u}\in X_{\boldsymbol{u}}
is quasi-generic. Recall that
(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},{\cal B}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}}),\kappa,S)
is a
Furstenberg system
of
\boldsymbol{u}
.
Let
\pi_{0}(y)=y_{0}
for
y\in X_{\boldsymbol{u}}
; then
\pi_{n}=\pi_{0}\circ S^{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, is a stationary process (once, we fix
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
).

2.7
Confined extensions

Confined extensions (see the definition below) are systematically studied in
[
4
]
(we recall some known results for completeness). In the present paper, we consider them only in the context of extensions of identity. Let
R\in{\rm Aut}(Z,\mathcal{B}_{Z},\kappa)
and
T\in{\rm Aut}(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu)
.

Lemma 2.6
.

If
\Phi_{\rho}:L^{2}(Z,\kappa)\to L^{2}(X,\mu)
and
\pi_{0}\in L^{2}(X,\mu)
then

\pi_{0}\perp{\rm Im}\,\Phi_{\rho}\text{ if and only if }\pi_{0}\perp{\rm Im}(\Phi_{\rho}\circ\Phi^{\ast}_{\rho}).

Proof.

Assume that
\pi_{0}\perp{\rm Im}(\Phi_{\rho}\circ\Phi^{\ast}_{\rho})
. Then for each
g\in L^{2}(Z,\kappa)
, we have
g=g_{1}+g_{2}
, where

g_{1}\in\overline{{\rm Im}\,\Phi^{\ast}_{\rho}}
and
g_{2}\perp{\rm Im}\,\Phi^{\ast}_{\rho}
. Hence

\int\pi_{0}\Phi_{\rho}(g)\,d\mu=\int\pi_{0}\Phi_{\rho}(g_{1})\,d\mu+\int\pi_{0}\Phi_{\rho}(g_{2})\,d\mu=

\int\pi_{0}\Phi_{\rho}(g_{1})\,d\mu+\int\Phi^{\ast}_{\rho}(\pi_{0})g_{2}\,d\kappa=\int\pi_{0}\Phi_{\rho}(g_{1})\,d\mu

and since
g_{1}
can be approximated by
\Phi_{\rho}^{\ast}(h)
with
h\in L^{2}(X,\mu)
, the claim follows.

Below, we restrict to
L^{2}_{0}
-spaces. By Lemma
2.6
, we obtain the following:

Lemma 2.7

(del Junco-Rudolph,
[
19
]
, proof of Prop. 5.3)
.

Under the above assumption,
\Phi_{\rho}=0
if and only if
\Phi_{\rho}\circ\Phi^{\ast}_{\rho}=0
.

Assume now that we have an extension
\widetilde{R}\in{\rm Aut}(\widetilde{Z},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}},\widetilde{\kappa})
of
R
. If
\widetilde{\lambda}
is any self-joining of
\widetilde{\kappa}
and
\lambda
is its restriction to
Z\times Z
then we easily check that

(12)

\Phi_{\lambda}=\mathrm{proj}_{L^{2}(Z)}\circ\Phi_{\widetilde{\lambda}}|_{L^{2}(Z)}.

Lemma 2.8
.

Assume that
\widetilde{\rho}
is a joining of
\widetilde{R}
and
T
(and
\rho
stands for its restriction to
Z\times X
). Then

\Phi_{\rho}^{\ast}\circ\Phi_{\rho}=\mathrm{proj}_{L^{2}(Z)}\circ\Phi^{\ast}_{\widetilde{\rho}}\circ\Phi_{\widetilde{\rho}}|_{L^{2}(Z)}.

Proof.

Similarly as in (
12
), we have
\Phi_{\rho}=\Phi_{\widetilde{\rho}}|_{L^{2}(Z)}
. Then, for
f,g\in L^{2}(Z)
,

\langle\Phi^{\ast}_{\rho}\circ\Phi_{\rho}f,g\rangle=\langle\Phi_{\rho}f,\Phi_{\rho}g\rangle=

\langle\Phi_{\widetilde{\rho}}f,\Phi_{\widetilde{\rho}}g\rangle=\langle\Phi^{\ast}_{\widetilde{\rho}}\circ\Phi_{\widetilde{\rho}}f,g\rangle=\langle\mathrm{proj}_{L^{2}(Z)}\circ\Phi^{\ast}_{\widetilde{\rho}}\circ\Phi_{\widetilde{\rho}}f,g\rangle.

Following
[
4
]
, an extension
\widetilde{Z}\to Z
is called
confined
if for each self-joining
\widetilde{\lambda}\in J_{2}(\widetilde{Z})
, we have

\widetilde{\lambda}|_{Z\times Z}=\kappa\otimes\kappa\;\Rightarrow\widetilde{\lambda}=\widetilde{\kappa}\otimes\widetilde{\kappa}.

We have now the following lifting disjointness result (first proved in
[
4
]
).

Proposition 2.9
.

Assume that
\widetilde{Z}\to Z
is a confined extension and let
Z\perp X
. Then
\widetilde{Z}\perp X
.

Proof.

Define
\widetilde{\lambda}\in J_{2}(\widetilde{R})
so that
\Phi_{\widetilde{\lambda}}=\Phi^{\ast}_{\widetilde{\rho}}\circ\Phi_{\widetilde{\rho}}
. Then by this and (
12
),

\Phi_{\lambda}=\mathrm{proj}_{L^{2}(Z)}\circ\Phi^{\ast}_{\widetilde{\rho}}\circ\Phi_{\widetilde{\rho}}|_{L^{2}(Z)}.

But now, by Lemma
2.8
, we obtain that
\Phi_{\lambda}=\Phi_{\rho}^{\ast}\circ\Phi_{\rho}
. Since
\Phi_{\rho}=0
, also
\Phi_{\lambda}=0
and therefore
\Phi_{\widetilde{\lambda}}=0
by our assumption. The result follows from Lemma
2.7
.

It follows easily that each confined extension of an identity is disjoint from all ergodic automorphisms. In fact, in Theorem
3.1
, we will show that an automorphism
R
is disjoint from all ergodic automorphisms if and only if it is a confined extension of an identity.

Lemma 2.10
.

Assume that
S\in{\rm Aut}(Y,\mathcal{B}_{Y},\nu)
. Then the set

S^{\not\perp}:=\{R\in{\rm Aut}(Z,\mathcal{B}_{Z},\kappa):\>R\not\perp S\}

cannot contain an uncountable family of pairwise disjoint automorphisms.

Proof.

Assume that we have
S^{\not\perp}\ni R_{i}\in{\rm Aut}(Z,\mathcal{B}_{Z},\kappa)
,
i\in I
(uncountable) are pairwise disjoint. Now, suppose that
\Phi_{i}:L^{2}(Z,\mathcal{B}_{Z},\kappa)\to L^{2}(Y,\mathcal{B}_{Y},\nu)
is a non-trivial intertwining Markov operator. Take
f,g\in L^{2}_{0}(Z,\mathcal{B}_{Z},\kappa)
, then, for each
i\neq j
, we have

\langle\Phi_{i}f,\Phi_{j}g\rangle=\langle\Phi_{j}^{\ast}\Phi_{i}f,g\rangle=0

as
\Phi^{\ast}_{j}\Phi_{i}=0
is an intertwining operator of
R_{i}
and
R_{j}
, so on
L_{0}^{2}
it is zero. So the images via
\Phi_{i}
’s are pairwise orthogonal, and we obtain a contradiction by separability.

2.8
Joinings of a non-ergodic automorphism with an ergodic system

Assume that
T\in{\rm Aut}(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu)
.
In the notation introduced at the beginning of Section
1.3
,
{T}:(\bar{x},u)\mapsto\bigl(\bar{x},T_{\bar{x}}(u)\bigr)
acts on the space
X=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}\bar{X}_{n}\times\{1,\ldots,n\}\sqcup\bar{X}_{\infty}\times Y
, where
\mu|_{\bar{X}_{n}\times\{1,\ldots,n\}}=\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{n}}\otimes\nu_{n}
and

\mu|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}\times Y}=\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}}\otimes\nu
.
Let us fix
R
an
ergodic
automorphism of
(Z,\mathcal{B}_{Z},\kappa)
.
Let
\rho\in J({T},R)
. Then we have factors:

(X\times Z,\rho)\to(X,\mu)\to({\bar{X}},\bar{\mu}),

so let us disintegrate
\rho
over
({\bar{X}},\bar{\mu})
:

(13)

\rho=\int_{{\bar{X}}}\rho_{{\bar{x}}}\,d\bar{\mu}({\bar{x}}).

Since the action of
T
on
\bar{X}
is the identity, note that

(14)

the measures
\rho_{{\bar{x}}}
are
{T}\times R
-invariant.

Moreover, for
A\in\mathcal{B}_{X}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}_{Z}
, we have

\rho(A\times B)=\int_{\bar{X}}\rho_{{\bar{x}}}(A\times B)\,d\bar{\mu}({\bar{x}}),

so

\mu(A)=\rho(A\times Z)=\int_{\bar{X}}\rho_{{\bar{x}}}(A\times Z)\,d\bar{\mu}({\bar{x}})

and

\kappa(B)=\rho(X\times B)=\int_{\bar{X}}\rho_{{\bar{x}}}(X\times B)\,d\bar{\mu}({\bar{x}}),

so by that (and in view of (
14
)):

(15)

\rho_{{\bar{x}}}\in J((T_{\bar{x}},\nu),(R,\kappa))

(for a.a.
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}_{\infty}
; replace
\nu
by
\nu_{n}
when we consider
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}_{n}
,
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
) by the uniqueness of disintegration of
\mu
(over
\bar{\mu}
) and the ergodicity of
\kappa
.

And vice versa: if (
15
) holds
and
the map

{\bar{x}}\mapsto\rho_{{\bar{x}}}
is measurable then the formula (
13
) defines a
\rho\in J({T},R)
. This observation is quite meaningful and, given
\Phi_{\rho}
, allows us to produce more Markov operators
\Phi_{\rho_{D}}
with
\rho_{D}\in J({T},R)
, indexed by measurable subsets
D\subset{\bar{X}}
, by:

(16)

\begin{array}[]{c}(\rho_{D})_{{\bar{x}}}=\rho_{{\bar{x}}},\text{ for }{\bar{x}}\in D\text{ and}\\
(\rho_{D})_{{\bar{x}}}=\delta_{\bar{x}}\otimes(\nu\otimes\kappa)\text{ or }\delta_{\bar{x}}\otimes(\nu_{n}\otimes\kappa)\text{ otherwise}\end{array}

(depending on whether
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}_{\infty}
or
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}_{n}
).
In what follows, in most cases we will only consider real-valued functions. Note that Markov operators send real-valued functions to real-valued functions. Moreover, the mean of a function is zero if and only if the mean of its real part and of imaginary part are zero.

Lemma 2.11
.

Assume that
\rho\in J(R,{T})
with
R
ergodic. Let
f\in L^{2}(X,\mu)
and
g\in L^{2}_{0}(Z,\mathcal{B}_{Z},\kappa)
be real-valued functions and assume that

f\perp F_{{\rm we},0}({T})
(in particular,

\int f\Phi_{\rho}(g)\,d\mu=0
).
Then, for a.a.
{\bar{x}}\in{\bar{X}_{\infty}}
, we have

\int_{Y}f(\bar{x},\cdot)\Phi_{\rho_{{\bar{x}}}}(g)\,d\nu=0,

and for every
n
, and a.a.
{\bar{x}}\in{\bar{X}_{n}}
, we have

\int_{\{1,\ldots,n\}}f(\bar{x},\cdot)\Phi_{\rho_{{\bar{x}}}}(g)\,d\nu_{n}=0.

Proof.

We consider the case
\bar{X}_{\infty}
(the proof is the same for other cases). Suppose that for some
\varepsilon_{0}>0
,

D:=\Big\{{\bar{x}}\in{\bar{X}_{\infty}}:\;\int_{Y}f(\bar{x},\cdot)\Phi_{\rho_{{\bar{x}}}}(g)\,d\nu\geq\varepsilon_{0}\Big\}=\\
\Big\{{\bar{x}}\in{\bar{X}_{\infty}}\colon\int_{Y\times Z}f(\bar{x},\cdot)\otimes g\,d\rho_{{\bar{x}}}\geq\varepsilon_{0}\Big\}

has positive
\bar{\mu}
-measure. Note that, since
\int g\,d\kappa=0
, we have

\int\Phi_{\rho_{{\bar{x}}}}(g)\,d\nu=\int g\,d\kappa=0
. In particular, if for
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}
,
\rho_{{\bar{x}}}=\delta_{\bar{x}}\otimes(\nu\otimes\kappa)
, then
\Phi_{\rho_{{\bar{x}}}}(g)=\int\Phi_{\rho_{{\bar{x}}}}(g)\,d\kappa=0
. By our assumption,
f\perp\Phi_{\rho_{D}}(g)
. But then,

\int f\Phi_{\rho_{D}}(g)\,d\mu=\int f\otimes g\,d\rho_{D}=\int\Big(\int f\otimes g\,d(\rho_{D})_{{\bar{x}}}\Big)d\bar{\mu}({\bar{x}})=\\
\int_{D}\Big(\int f({\bar{x}},y)\Phi_{\rho_{{\bar{x}}}}(g)(y)\,d\nu(y)\Big)d\bar{\mu}({\bar{x}})\ \geq\ \varepsilon_{0}\bar{\mu}(D)\ >\ 0,

a contradiction.

In the above sense, being orthogonal to Markov images of ergodic systems is shifted to examine Markov images of ergodic systems on the (ergodic) fibers.

2.9
Relative ergodic decomposition

We consider an invertible, bi-measurable transformation
T
of a standard Borel space
(X,{\cal B}_{X})
. Recall that
\mathcal{I}_{T}
is the sub-sigma-algebra of
T
-invariant Borel sets
Recall also that
M(X)
stands for the space of Borel, probability measures on
X
, and
M(X,T)\subset M(X)
for the subset of
T
-invariant measures. Let
(Y,{\cal A})
be another measurable space (not necessarily standard). Let
\varphi:X\to Y
be a measurable map which satisfies the property

(17)

\varphi^{-1}({\cal A})\subset\mathcal{I}_{T}.

We also fix a
T
-invariant probability measure
\mu\in M(X,T)
.

Proposition 2.12
.

There exists a probability kernel
[0,1]\ni t\mapsto\nu_{t}\in M(X)
such that:

(a) for all
t\in[0,1]
,
\nu_{t}\in M(X,T)
,

(b) we have

\int_{0}^{1}\nu_{t}\,dt=\mu

and

(c) each
\nu_{t}
satisfies
\varphi_{\ast}(\nu_{t})=\varphi_{\ast}(\mu)
and

\mathcal{I}_{T}=\varphi^{-1}({\cal A})\text{ mod }\nu_{t}\;(cf.\ \eqref{martim1}).

Moreover, if
\varphi^{-1}({\cal A})=\mathcal{I}_{T}\text{ mod }\mu
, then for each decomposition satisfying (a), (b) and (c), we have
\nu_{t}=\mu
for all
t
.

We postpone the proof of Proposition
2.12
to Section
9
.

2.10
Characteristic classes

A class
{\cal F}
of measure-preserving systems (implicitly closed under isomorphism) is called
characteristic
if it is closed under factors and countable joinings (the latter implies that it is closed under inverse limits). An
{\cal F}
-factor
of
(Z,{\cal B}_{Z},\kappa,R)
is an
R
-invariant sub-sigma-algebra
{\cal A}
of
{\cal B}_{Z}
such that the action of
R
restricted to
{\cal A}
defines a system in
{\cal F}
. One of the reasons to consider characteristic classes is the following (for the proof, see e.g.
[
32
]
):

Theorem 2.13
.

If
\mathcal{F}
is a characteristic class and
R\in{\rm Aut}(Z,\mathcal{B}_{Z},\kappa)
then the largest
{\cal F}
-factor
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}={\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(Z,{\cal B}_{Z},\kappa,R)\subset{\cal B}_{Z}
of
(Z,{\cal B}_{Z},\kappa,R)
always exists. Moreover, any joining
\rho
of
R
with some
(X,\mu,T)\in{\cal F}
is the relatively independent extension of the relevant restriction of the joining to an element of
J(R|_{{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}},T)
. Equivalently, for all
f\in L^{2}(\kappa)
and all
g\in L^{2}(\mu)
,

\int_{Z\times X}f(z)\,g(x)\,d\rho=\int_{Z\times X}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\rho}\bigl[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}\bigr](z)\,g(x)\,d\rho(z,x)

Corollary 2.14
.

Assume that
\mathcal{F}
is a characteristic class and
R\in{\rm Aut}(Z,\mathcal{B}_{Z},\kappa)
. Let
S\in\mathcal{F}
. Then
S\perp R
if and only if
S\perp R|_{{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}}
.

Of course the class ZE of zero entropy automorphisms is a characteristic class.

Proposition 2.15

(
[
23
]
)
.

If
{\cal F}
is a
proper
characteristic class (that is, if
{\cal F}
does not include
all
measure-preserving systems), then
{\cal F}\subset
ZE.

To any given characteristic class
{\cal F}
, we can associate another class
{\cal F}_{\rm ec}
, consisting of measure-preserving systems whose almost all ergodic components are in
{\cal F}
. Then
{\cal F}_{\rm ec}
is also a characteristic class, which is called an
ec-class
. It is explained in
[
23
]
that ec-classes are the right framework for characterizing orthogonality of a bounded arithmetic function to the topological systems whose invariant measures determine measure-preserving systems belonging to a fixed characteristic class. However the ec-issue disappears in Boshernitzan’s problem, and the following proposition provides some explanation for this.

Proposition 2.16
.

Let
{\cal F}
be a characteristic class. For each automorphism
T
of
(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu)
and
f\in L^{2}(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu)
, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(T)\bigr]-{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}_{\rm ec}}(T)\bigr]\in F_{\rm we}(T)^{\perp}.

Proof.

Take any ergodic automorphism
R
of
(Z,\mathcal{B}_{Z},\kappa)
and let
g\in L^{2}(Z,\mathcal{B}_{Z},\kappa)
. Assume that
\rho\in J(T,R)
. We apply now consecutively: Theorem
2.13
for
{\cal F}
(twice), the ergodicity of
R
(the
{\cal F}
-factor and the
{\cal F}_{\rm ec}
-factor of
R
are the same), and Theorem
2.13
for
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}_{\rm ec}}
(twice), to obtain the following:

\displaystyle\int{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(T)\bigr]\otimes g\,d\rho

\displaystyle=\int{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(T)\bigr]\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}\bigl[g\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(R)\bigr]\,d\rho

\displaystyle=\int f\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}\bigl[g\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(R)\bigl]\,d\rho

\displaystyle=\int f\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}\bigl[g\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}_{\rm ec}}(R)\bigl]\,d\rho

\displaystyle=\int{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}_{\rm ec}})(T)\bigr]\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}\bigl[g\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}_{\rm ec}}(R)\bigr]\,d\rho

\displaystyle=\int{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}_{\rm ec}}(T)\bigr]\otimes g\,d\rho.

It follows that
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}_{\rm ec}}(T)\bigr]-{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(T)\bigr]\perp{\rm Im}(\Phi_{\rho})
and since
R
is arbitrary ergodic, the claim follows.

It has been already noticed in
[
23
]
that if
{\cal F}
is a characteristic class and
T
is automorphism on
(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu)
such that a.a. its ergodic components are in
{\cal F}
then
T

need not
belong to
{\cal F}
.
However, the following general property holds.

Proposition 2.17
.

Let
{\cal F}
be a characteristic class and let
T
be an automorphism of
(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu)
. If
\mu
has a countable ergodic decomposition with respect to
T
, then
(X,{\cal B},\mu,T)
is in
{\cal F}
if and only if
(X,{\cal B},\mu_{i},T)
is in
{\cal F}
for every ergodic component
\mu_{i}
.

We postpone the proof of this proposition to Section
6.3
.

2.11
Measurable selectors

We recall:

Theorem 2.18

(Kallman
[
21
]
)
.

Suppose that
X
is a standard Borel space and
Y
is a separable topological space metrizable by a complete metric. Suppose that
A\subset X\times Y
is Borel and that for each
x\in X
,
A_{x}:=\{y\in Y:\>(x,y)\in A\}
is a countable union of compact sets (
\sigma
-compactness of
A_{x}
). Let
f:X\times Y\to X
be projection. Then
f(A)
is Borel and there is a Borel section
f(A)\to A
of the map
f|_{A}
.

We also recall the following useful result (see Theorem 4.5.2 in
[
35
]
):

Theorem 2.19
.

Assume that
X,Y
are Polish spaces and let
A\subset X
be Borel. Let
f:A\to Y
. Then
f
is Borel if and only if
{\rm graph}(f)
is a Borel subset of
A\times Y
.

For example, by (
6
),
the set
\{(x,T_{x}):\>x\in X\}
is a Borel subset of
X\times{\rm Aut}(Y,\nu)
.

2.12
Disjointness in
{\rm Aut}(X,\mu)

We recall that the set
{\rm Aut}(X,\mu)
of automorphisms of the standard Borel probability space
(X,\mu)
is a Polish group with the strong topology. Furthermore, the space
M(X\times X)
of Borel probability measures on
X\times X
is a Polish space with the weak
∗
-topology. Note that
C_{2}(\mu)
which is the set of couplings of
\mu
with itself, i.e. the subset of
M(X\times X)
consisting of measures with both marginals
\mu
, is a
compact
subset of
M(X\times X)
. Indeed, this is a consequence of Prokhorov’s theorem, as
C_{2}(\mu)
is both closed and tight in
M(X\times X)
, the tightness deriving from that of
\{\mu\}
in
M(X)
.

We consider the following pseudo-metrics “responsible” for the topologies on
{\rm Aut}(X,\mu)
and
C_{2}(\mu)
, respectively:

d(T,T^{\prime};Q):=\sum_{q\in Q}\mu(Tq\triangle T^{\prime}q)

and

(18)

d(\rho,\rho^{\prime};Q):=\sum_{q_{1},q_{2}\in Q}|\rho(q_{1}\times q_{2})-\rho^{\prime}(q_{1}\times q_{2})|,

where
Q
is a finite measurable partition of
X
.

We aim at proving the following:

Proposition 2.20
.

The set
\{(S,T):\>S,T\in{\rm Aut}(X,\mu),S\perp T\}
is a Borel subset of
{\rm Aut}(X,\mu)\times{\rm Aut}(X,\mu)
(in fact, it is a
G_{\delta}
-set).

We will adapt del Junco’s proof of Theorem 1
[
18
]
to our needs.
Given two finite (measurable) partitions
P,Q
of
X
and
\delta,\varepsilon>0
, we let

\mathscr{O}(P,\varepsilon,Q,\delta)

denote the set of pairs
(S,T)
of automorphisms of
(X,\mu)
satisfying: if
\rho\in C_{2}(\mu)
then (notation
(\cdot)_{\ast}(\rho)
stands for the image of the measure
\rho
under the map “
\cdot
”)

d(\rho,(S\times T)_{\ast}\rho;Q)<\delta\;\Longrightarrow\;d(\rho,\mu\otimes\mu;P)<\varepsilon.

Obviously, if
0<\delta^{\prime}<\delta
then

\mathscr{O}(P,\varepsilon,Q,\delta)\subset\mathscr{O}(P,\varepsilon,Q,\delta^{\prime}).

In fact, we have the following:

Lemma 2.21
.

\mathscr{O}(P,\varepsilon,Q,\delta)\subset{\rm Int}(\mathscr{O}(P,\varepsilon,Q,\delta/3)).

Proof.

Assume that
(S,T)\in\mathscr{O}(P,\varepsilon,Q,\delta)
. Suppose that (the pseudo-metrics in the product space giving the relevant topology are given by the relevant “max”)

d((S,T),(S^{\prime},T^{\prime});Q)<\delta/(3|Q|).

We want to show that
(S^{\prime},T^{\prime})\in\mathscr{O}(P,\varepsilon,Q,\delta/3)
.
So take
\rho\in C_{2}(\mu)
and assume that
d(\rho,(S^{\prime}\times T^{\prime})_{\ast}\rho;Q)<\delta/3
. Then

d((S^{\prime}\times T^{\prime})_{\ast}\rho,(S\times T)_{\ast}\rho;Q)\leq d((S^{\prime}\times T^{\prime})_{\ast}\rho,(S\times T^{\prime})_{\ast}\rho;Q)+

d((S\times T^{\prime})_{\ast}\rho,(S\times T)_{\ast}\rho;Q)=

\sum_{q_{1},q_{2}\in Q}|\rho(S^{\prime}q_{1}\times T^{\prime}q_{2})-\rho(Sq_{1}\times T^{\prime}q_{2})|+\sum_{q_{1},q_{2}\in Q}|\rho(Sq_{1}\times T^{\prime}q_{2})-\rho(Sq_{1}\times Tq_{2})|\leq

\sum_{q_{1},q_{2}\in Q}\rho((S^{\prime}q_{1}\triangle Sq_{1})\times X)+\sum_{q_{1},q_{2}\in Q}\rho(X\times(T^{\prime}q_{2}\triangle Tq_{2})\leq

\sum_{q_{1},q_{2}\in Q}\mu((S^{\prime}q_{1}\triangle Sq_{1})+\sum_{q_{1},q_{2}\in Q}\mu(T^{\prime}q_{2}\triangle Tq_{2})\leq 2|Q|\delta/(3|Q|)=\frac{2}{3}\delta.

It follows that

d(\rho,(S\times T)_{\ast}(\rho);Q)\leq\frac{2}{3}\delta+\delta/3=\delta

and since
(S,T)\in\mathscr{O}(P,\varepsilon,Q,\delta)
,
d(\rho,\mu\otimes\mu;P)<\varepsilon
.

We have obtained that the set

\mathscr{O}(P,\varepsilon,Q):=\bigcup_{\delta>0}\mathscr{O}(P,\varepsilon,Q,\delta)

is open, so the set

\mathscr{O}:=\bigcap_{m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}}\bigcup_{\ell\in{\mathbb{N}}}\mathscr{O}(P_{m},\frac{1}{n},P_{\ell})

is
G_{\delta}
,
where
P_{n}
is going to the partition into points and it consists of clopen sets (we assume additionally that
X
is a zero-dimensional space).

Lemma 2.22
.

If
(S,T)\notin\mathscr{O}
then
S\not\perp T
.

Proof.

By assumption, there exist
m,n\geq 1
such that for all
\ell\geq 1
,

(S,T)\notin\mathscr{O}(P_{m},\frac{1}{n},P_{\ell},\frac{1}{\ell})
. Hence, for some
\rho_{\ell}\in C_{2}(\mu)
, we have

(19)

d(\rho_{\ell},(S\times T)_{\ast}\rho_{\ell};P_{\ell})<\frac{1}{\ell},

(20)

d(\rho_{\ell},\mu\otimes\mu;P_{m})\geq\frac{1}{n}.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\rho_{\ell}\to\rho
. Then
\rho\in C_{2}(\mu)
and, by (
19
),
(S\times T)_{\ast}\rho=\rho
, so
\rho
is a joining. While (
20
) tells us that
\rho\neq\mu\otimes\mu
, so
T
and
S
are not disjoint.

Proof of Proposition
2.20
All we need to prove is that
\mathscr{O}=\{(S,T):\>S\perp T\}
. Assume that
(S,T)\in\mathscr{O}
. Then, for each
m,n\geq 1
there are
\ell\geq 1
and
\delta>0
such that
(S,T)\in\mathscr{O}(P_{m},\frac{1}{n},P_{\ell},\delta)
. But if
\rho\in C_{2}(\mu)
is a joining then this measure is
S\times T
-invariant, so
d(\rho,(S\times T)_{\ast}\rho;P_{\ell})=0<\delta
. Hence,
d(\rho,\mu\otimes\mu;P_{m})<\frac{1}{n}
and therefore
\rho=\mu\otimes\mu
. The result follows now from Lemma
2.22
.

Remark 2.23
.

In fact, del Junco proved that the set of automorphisms disjoint with a fixed one is Borel.

Remark 2.24
.

We can also study disjointness (and various problems of measurability of natural subsets) when automorphisms are defined on different spaces. For example, if
(X^{\prime},\mathcal{B}_{X^{\prime}},\mu^{\prime})
is another standard Borel probability space (we assume that
X^{\prime}
is a compact metric space), then the set

\{(T,T^{\prime})\in{\rm Aut}(X,\mu)\times{\rm Aut}(X^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\colon T\perp T^{\prime}\}

is a Borel subset (in fact, it is
G_{\delta}
). To obtain this result, we repeat the proof of Proposition
2.20
with the pseudo-metrics
d
on
C_{2}(\mu)
in (
18
) replaced with the pseudo-metrics
d
on the space
C(\mu,\mu^{\prime})
of couplings between
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
given by

d(\rho_{1},\rho_{2};Q,Q^{\prime})=\sum_{q\in Q,q^{\prime}\in Q^{\prime}}|\rho_{1}(q\times q^{\prime})-\rho_{2}(q\times q^{\prime})|

where
Q
and
Q^{\prime}
are finite (measurable) partitions of
X
and
X^{\prime}
, respectively.

PART I

3
Automorphisms disjoint from all ergodic systems

In all this section, we assume that
{T}:(\bar{x},u)\mapsto\bigl(\bar{x},T_{\bar{x}}(u)\bigr)
is an automorphism
acting on the space
X=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}\bar{X}_{n}\times\{1,\ldots,n\}\sqcup\bar{X}_{\infty}\times Y
, where
\mu|_{\bar{X}_{n}\times\{1,\ldots,n\}}=\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{n}}\otimes\nu_{n}
,

\mu|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}\times Y}=\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}}\otimes\nu
, and the automorphisms
T_{\bar{x}}
are ergodic, cf. Section
1.3
.

We aim at proving the following theorem:

Theorem 3.1
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

{T}\in{\rm Erg}^{\perp}
.

(ii) The extension
{T}\to{\rm Id}_{\bar{X}}
is confined.

(iii) For
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}
, we have
T_{\bar{x}}\perp T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}
.

Corollary 3.2
.

If
{T}\in{\rm Erg}^{\perp}
then
h({T})=0
.

Proof.

Ergodic positive entropy transformations are not disjoint because of Sinai’s theorem.

Corollary 3.3
.

If there exists
S\in{\rm Erg}
such that
\{T_{\bar{x}}\colon\bar{x}\in{\bar{X}}\}\subset S^{\not\perp}
then
{T}\not\perp{\rm Erg}
.

Proof.

If
\bar{\mu}
has an atom, say
\bar{\mu}(\{\bar{x}_{0}\})>0
, then also
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}(\{(\bar{x}_{0},\bar{x}_{0})\})>0
and (iii) of Theorem
3.1
applies. Otherwise, use Lemma
2.10
.

3.1
Proof of (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i)

The proof follows from Proposition
2.9
since classically
{\rm Id}\in{\rm Erg}^{\perp}
.

3.2
Proof of (iii)
\Rightarrow
(ii)

Assume that
\rho\in J_{2}({T})
,
\rho|_{{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}}=\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
. Then, by disintegrating over
\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
,

\rho=\int_{\bar{X}\times\bar{X}}\rho_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}\,d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x}^{\prime}),

where, for
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in\bar{X}_{m}\times\bar{X}_{n}
,
\rho_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}
is
T_{\bar{x}}\times T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}
-invariant, defined on
\{(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\}\times\{1,\ldots,m\}\times\{1,\ldots,n\}
if
m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,

\{(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\}\times\{1,\ldots,m\}\times Y
if
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
n=\infty
and
\{(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\}\times Y\times Y
if
m=n=\infty
.
Furthermore,

\rho=\sum_{m,n\in{\mathbb{N}}\cup\{\infty\}}\rho|_{X_{m}\times X_{n}}
,
where

\rho|_{X_{m}\times X_{n}}=\int_{\bar{X}_{m}\times\bar{X}_{n}}\rho_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}\,d(\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{m}})(\bar{x})d(\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{n}})(\bar{x}^{\prime}).

Consider the case
m=n=\infty
(the reasoning is similar in all remaining cases). For
A\in\mathcal{B}_{\bar{X}_{\infty}}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}_{Y}
, we have

(21)

\begin{array}[]{c}(\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}})\otimes\nu(A\times B)=(\rho|_{X_{\infty}\times X_{\infty}})(A\times B\times{\bar{X}}_{\infty}\times Y)=\\
\int_{A\times{\bar{X}}_{\infty}}\rho_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}(B\times Y)\,d(\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}})(\bar{x})d(\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}})(\bar{x}^{\prime}).\end{array}

By substituting
A={\bar{X}}_{\infty}
, we first obtain that the measure

B\mapsto\int\rho_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}(B\times Y)\,d(\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}})(\bar{x})d(\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}})(\bar{x}^{\prime})

is
\nu
, the integrand measures are
T_{\bar{x}}
-invariant and since
\nu
is
T_{\bar{x}}
-ergodic, we must have
\rho_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}\in J(T_{\bar{x}},T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}})
.
But
T_{\bar{x}}\perp T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}
for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}
, so
\rho_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}=\nu\otimes\nu
and
\rho|_{X_{\infty}\times X_{\infty}}
is the product measure.

Remark 3.4
.

Note that under the assumption
(iii)
, we must have
\bar{\mu}(\bar{X}_{n})=0
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}\setminus\{1\}
. Indeed, for
2\leq n<\infty
, we have
T_{\bar{x}}=R_{n}
for all
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}_{n}
.

Remark 3.5
.

Note also that the reasoning in the above proof can be reversed. Indeed, returning to (
21
), we obtain that if

\bar{X}_{\infty}\times\bar{X}_{\infty}\ni(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\mapsto\rho_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}\in J(T_{\bar{x}},T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}})\text{ is measurable}

then
\rho\in J_{2}(T|_{X_{\infty}})
as

\int_{A\times{\bar{X}_{\infty}}}\rho_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}(B\times Y)\,d(\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}})(\bar{x})d(\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}})(\bar{x}^{\prime})=

\int_{A\times{\bar{X}_{\infty}}}\nu(B)\,d(\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}})(\bar{x})d(\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}})(\bar{x}^{\prime})=(\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}})\otimes\nu(A\times B).

A similar argument works for the remaining cases of elements in
J(T|_{X_{m}},T|_{X_{n}})
.

3.3
Kallman theorem and joinings

From (
6
), Proposition
2.20
, Remark
2.24
and Remark
2.23
, we immediately get the following:

Corollary 3.6
.

(i) The set
\{(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}\colon T_{\bar{x}}\perp T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}\}
is Borel.

(ii) If
S_{0}\in{\rm Aut}(Y,\nu)
(or
S_{0}\in{\rm Aut}(\{1,\ldots,m\},\nu_{m})
,
m\geq 1
) then the set
\{\bar{x}\in{\bar{X}}\colon T_{\bar{x}}\perp S_{0}\}
is Borel.

Now, we can prove the following result as an application of Kallman theorem.

Lemma 3.7
.

Fix
S_{0}\in{\rm Aut}(Y,\nu)
(or
S_{0}\in{\rm Aut}(\{1,\ldots,m\},\nu_{m})
,
m\geq 1
) and suppose that
\bar{\mu}(\{\bar{x}\in{\bar{X}}\colon T_{\bar{x}}\not\perp S_{0}\})>0
. Then
{T}\not\perp S_{0}
.

Proof.

Let (by some abuse of notation)

C_{2}(Y,\nu):=\{J\colon L^{2}(Y,\nu)\to L^{2}(Y,\nu)\colon J\text{ is Markov}\}

be the space of Markov operators corresponding to couplings of
\nu
with itself. Then

C_{2}(Y,\nu)
is a compact metric space (with the weak operator topology).

Let
\Pi
denote the Markov operator corresponding to product measure
\nu\otimes\nu
, and consider the set

(22)

\{(\bar{x},T_{\bar{x}},J)\colon\bar{x}\in{\bar{X}},\Pi\neq J\in C_{2}(Y,\nu),JT_{\bar{x}}=S_{0}J\}

which is a Borel subset of

\bigl({\bar{X}}\times{\rm Aut}(Y,\nu)\bigr)\times C_{2}(Y,\nu)

as the intersection of

\{(\bar{x},T_{\bar{x}})\colon\bar{x}\in{\bar{X}}\}\times(C_{2}(Y,\nu)\setminus\{\Pi\})

and

{\bar{X}}\times\{(R,J)\in{\rm Aut}(Y,\nu)\times C_{2}(Y,\nu)\colon JR=S_{0}J\}.

Let
f
denote the projection of the set (
22
) on the first two coordinates. Note that this image is precisely
\{(\bar{x},T_{\bar{x}})\colon T_{\bar{x}}\not\perp S_{0}\}
. For each such
(\bar{x},T_{\bar{x}})
the fiber consists of all Markov operators
J\in C_{2}(Y,\nu)\setminus\{\Pi\}
such that
JT_{\bar{x}}=S_{0}J
, so it is a compact set up to the one-element set
\{\Pi\}
, and therefore it is
\sigma
-compact. We can now use Theorem
2.18
, to obtain a Borel map

\{\bar{x}\in{\bar{X}}\colon T_{\bar{x}}\not\perp S_{0}\}\ni\bar{x}\to J_{\bar{x}}\in C_{2}(Y,\nu)\setminus\{\Pi\}

satisfying
J_{\bar{x}}T_{\bar{x}}=S_{0}J_{\bar{x}}
which is precisely the fact that
J_{\bar{x}}
is a joining of
T_{\bar{x}}
and
S_{0}
.
Since we assume that
\bar{\mu}(\{\bar{x}\in{\bar{X}}\colon T_{\bar{x}}\not\perp S_{0}\})>0
, we obtain a nontrivial joining between
{T}
and
S_{0}
as in Section
2.8
, see (
16
).

3.4
Proof of (i)
\Rightarrow
(iii)

(By contraposition.) Suppose that
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}(\{(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}):\>T_{\bar{x}}\not\perp T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}\})>0
.
7
7

7

The set we are considering is measurable by the same token as (i) in Corollary
3.6
.
. By Fubini’s theorem, there is
\bar{x}_{0}\in{\bar{X}}
such that the set
D
of
\bar{x}^{\prime}
satisfying
T_{\bar{x}_{0}}\not\perp T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}
has positive
\bar{\mu}
-measure. By Lemma
3.7
, it follows that
{T}\not\perp T_{\bar{x}_{0}}
, hence
{T}\not\in{\rm Erg}^{\perp}
.

3.5
Application of the relative ergodic decomposition

Let us consider now the result stated in Proposition
2.12
in the context of self-joinings of
{T}
acting on
(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu)
by

{T}(\bar{x},u)=(\bar{x},T_{\bar{x}}(u)),

where
T_{\bar{x}}
are ergodic (as explained in Section
1.3
). Let
\lambda\in J_{2}({T})
,
\lambda|_{{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}}=\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
.
The disintegration of
\lambda
with respect to the projection on
{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}
writes

(23)

\lambda=\int_{{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}}\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}\,d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x}^{\prime}),

where
\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}\in J(T_{\bar{x}},T_{x^{\prime}})
for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}
.
We can apply Proposition
2.12
to
({T}\times{T},\lambda)
, when
\phi:X\times X\to{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}
is the projection onto
{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}
. We obtain that

\lambda=\int_{0}^{1}\lambda_{t}\,dt,

where, from (a), each
\lambda_{t}
is
{T}\times{T}
-invariant, and from (c) the marginal on
{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}
of each
\lambda_{t}
is the same as the marginal of
\lambda
, which is
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
, and moreover the system
\bigl(X\times X,\lambda_{t},{T}\times{T}\bigr)
is a relatively ergodic extension of
({\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}},\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu},{\rm Id})
. It follows that the ergodic decomposition of
\lambda_{t}
takes the form

\lambda_{t}=\int_{{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}}\lambda_{t,\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}\,d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x}^{\prime}),

where the measures
\lambda_{t,\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}
are ergodic (for
T_{\bar{x}}\times T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}})
.
Integrating with respect to
t
, and comparing with (
23
), we get that for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-almost all
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})
, we have

\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}=\int_{0}^{1}\lambda_{t,\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}\,dt.

Projecting on each
Y
-coordinate the above relation, and remembering that the automorphisms
T_{\bar{x}}
are ergodic, we get that for Lebesgue-almost all
t\in[0,1)
(depending on
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})
),
\lambda_{t,\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}
is an ergodic joining of
T_{\bar{x}}
and
T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}
.

Finally, defining

(24)

J_{2}^{\rm RelErg}({T}):=\{\lambda\in J_{2}({T})\colon\lambda|_{{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}}=\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu},\\
\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}\in J^{e}(T_{\bar{x}},T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}})\text{ for }\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}\text{-a.a. }(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}\},

we obtain the following:

Corollary 3.8
.

Each self-joining
\lambda
of
{T}
projecting into
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
(on
{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}
) is of the form
\lambda=\int_{0}^{1}\lambda_{t}\,dt
, where
\lambda_{t}\in J_{2}^{\rm RelErg}({T})
for all
t\in[0,1]
.

Remark 3.9
.

This proves in particular that
J_{2}^{\rm RelErg}({T})
is not empty, since the corollary applies to
\lambda=\mu\otimes\mu
.

3.6
Examples

Let
M=G/\Gamma
, where
G=H_{3}({\mathbb{R}})
and
\Gamma=H_{3}({\mathbb{Z}})
(
\Gamma
is an example of a lattice in the Heisenberg group;
M
is an example of a nil-manifold). On
M
we consider the (normalized) measure
\kappa
which is the image of Haar measure on
G
. Given
g\in G
, let
T_{g}:M\to M
:
T_{g}(x\Gamma)=gx\Gamma
. Denote by
\pi:M\to{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
the map

\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&a&c\\
0&1&b\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right]/\Gamma\mapsto(a~{\rm mod}~1,b~{\rm mod}~1)\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}.

Note that
\pi
is well defined. Moreover, since

\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&a&c\\
0&1&b\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right]\cdot\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&a^{\prime}&c^{\prime}\\
0&1&b^{\prime}\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&a+a^{\prime}&c^{\prime}+ab^{\prime}+c\\
0&1&b+b^{\prime}\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right],

the fibers of
\pi
are the circle
{\mathbb{T}}
. Moreover, the rotation
T_{g}
on
M
is sent to the rotation
R_{(a,b)}
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
. By a theorem of Auslander-Green-Hahn
[
3
]
, Chapter V, if
1,a,b
are rationally independent (i.e. when
R_{(a,b)}
is ergodic)
T_{g}
is ergodic and it is a compact group (circle) extension of the rotation (from the measure-theoretic point of view
M
is
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{T}}
and in these “coordinates”
\kappa
is Leb
{}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}}\otimes
Leb
T
) such that in the orthocomplement of
L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2},{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}})
the spectrum is countable Lebesgue.
It follows that when
T_{g}
and
T_{g^{\prime}}
are ergodic then

(25)

T_{g}\perp T_{g^{\prime}}\Leftrightarrow ka+\ell b=k^{\prime}a^{\prime}+\ell^{\prime}b^{\prime}\neq 0

for some
k,\ell,k^{\prime}\ell^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
(i.e.
R_{(a,b)}
and
R_{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}
have a non-trivial common factor).

Denote by
q
the natural quotient map
q:G\to G/\Gamma
and set
N_{k,\ell,m}:=\{(a,b)\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}\colon k+\ell a+mb=0\}
. Let

A:=(\pi\circ q)^{-1}\big(\bigcup_{k,\ell,m}({\mathbb{T}}^{2}\setminus N_{k,\ell,m})\big).

Then
A\subset G
is a subset of full Haar
G
-measure. Now, given
g=(a,b,c)\in G
, we can find only a subset of
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\in{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
of zero Leb
{}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}}
measure to see that
R_{(a,b)}\not\perp R_{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}
. It follows that the condition
T_{g}\perp T_{g^{\prime}}
is satisfied for Haar
{}_{G}\otimes
Haar
G
-a.a.
g,g^{\prime})\in G\times G
.

It follows that, by considering
any
probability measure
\bar{\mu}
on
G
equivalent to Haar
G
, the automorphism:

{T}(g,x\Gamma):=(g,gx\Gamma),\;{T}:G\times M\to G\times M,

preserves the measure
\bar{\mu}\otimes\kappa
, and
(G,\bar{\mu})
is the space of ergodic components. By Theorem
3.1
,
{T}\perp
Erg.

The above reasoning has its natural generalization for any connected nilpotent
d
-step group
G
and the corresponding rotations on nil-manifolds. Indeed, as before, we consider the quotient map

G/\Gamma\to G/([G,G]\Gamma)
, where the latter space is the torus
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
and a nil-rotation
T_{g}
is sent to a rotation on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
. Moreover, by Auslander-Green-Hahn theorem
[
3
]
, Chapter V,
T_{g}
is ergodic iff the corresponding rotation on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
is ergodic. As the set of ergodic rotations on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d}
is of full Lebesgue measure, also the set of ergodic nil-rotations on
G/\Gamma
is of full Haar measure. We now, apply the same reasoning to the product space
(G\times G)/(\Gamma\times\Gamma)
to obtain that the set of ergodic nil-rotations
T_{g,g^{\prime}}
is of full (product) Haar measure. As
T_{g,g^{\prime}}=T_{g}\times T_{g^{\prime}}
, we use now the following result: assuming
T_{g}
,
T_{g^{\prime}}
ergodic,

T_{g}\perp T_{g^{\prime}}\text{ iff the Cartesian product }T_{g}\times T_{g^{\prime}}\text{ is ergodic}

(see Corollary 1.5 in
[
31
]
) to conclude that on the set of
(g,g^{\prime})
of full (product) Haar measure, we have
T_{g}\perp T_{g^{\prime}}
. Hence the corresponding
{T}
(see (
25
)) is disjoint from all ergodic systems.

Remark 3.10
.

Automorphisms of that type considered for connected and simply connected nilpotent Lie groups are fundamental to prove Sarnak’s conjecture: they satisfy Sarnak’s conjecture (we can consider other measures
\bar{\mu}
on
G
than those equivalent to Haar measure, including those with compact support; but we should consider only those for which
T_{g}\perp T_{g^{\prime}}
for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
(g,g^{\prime})
; then
{T}
will be considered on the compact space
{\rm supp}(\bar{\mu})\times M
and it will be a homeomorphism of zero entropy) but whether the strong MOMO property holds for them is a big open problem, see
[
23
]
,
[
24
]
,
[
37
]
. We recall that for a fixed
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
, a topological system
(X,T)
is said to satisfy the strong MOMO property relative to
\boldsymbol{u}
(or simply strong
\boldsymbol{u}
-MOMO property) if

\lim_{K\to\infty}\frac{1}{b_{K}}\sum_{k\leq K}\Big\|\sum_{b_{k}\leq n<b_{k+1}}\boldsymbol{u}(n)\cdot f\circ T^{n}\Big\|_{C(X)}=0

for each choice of
(b_{k})
so that
b_{k+1}-b_{k}\to\infty
and all
f\in C(X)
. Clearly, the strong
\boldsymbol{u}
-MOMO implies (
1
). If
\boldsymbol{u}
is Möbius, we speak about the strong MOMO property.

Another class of examples of non-ergodic automorphisms arises when we consider (measurable) flows
\mathcal{S}=(S_{t})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
on
(Y,\mathcal{B}_{Y},\nu)
and we set

(26)

T(t,y)=(t,S_{t}y)\text{ on }([0,1]\times Y,{\rm Leb}_{[0,1]}\otimes\nu).

Now, using Theorem
3.1
,
[
14
]
(Theorem 8.4 therein) and
[
25
]
(Theorem 1.1 therein), we obtain the following:

Corollary 3.11
.

The automorphism (
26
) is in
{\rm Erg}^{\perp}
whenever:

(i) the flow
\mathcal{S}
has singular spectrum;

(ii)
\mathcal{S}
is a non-trivial smooth time change of a horocycle flow (each such flow has Lebesgue spectrum).

Note that our classical example
(x,y)\mapsto(x,y+x)
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
is a particular case of (i) (use as
\mathcal{S}
the linear flow on
{\mathbb{T}}
) and that the papers
[
14
]
,
[
25
]
provide many examples of smooth flows on surfaces for which the automorphisms (
26
) are in
{\rm Erg}^{\perp}
.

4
Orthogonality to ergodic Markov images

4.1
Preparatory observations and some motivations

Before we formulate the main result of this section, we need some preparatory observations.
We recall that we consider
{T}:(\bar{x},u)\mapsto(\bar{x},T_{\bar{x}}u)
acting on
(X,\mu)
with the fiber automorphisms
T_{\bar{x}}
ergodic. It follows that the extension

(27)

{T}\to{T}|_{\bar{X}}={\rm Id}_{\bar{X}}\text{ is relatively ergodic,}

i.e. the sigma-algebra generated by the projection on
\bar{X}
coincides with
\mathcal{I}_{{T}}
mod
\mu
.
Let us consider now
\lambda\in J_{2}({T})
,
\lambda|_{\bar{X}\times\bar{X}}=\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
.
If
f\in L^{2}(X,\mu)
and
g\in L^{2}(\bar{X},\bar{\mu})
then for each
N\geq 1
,

\int f(x)g(\bar{x}^{\prime})\,d\lambda|_{X\times{\bar{X}}}(x,\bar{x}^{\prime})=\frac{1}{N}\int\sum_{n\leq N}f\circ{T}^{n}(x)g(\bar{x}^{\prime})\,d\lambda|_{X\times{\bar{X}}}(x,\bar{x}^{\prime}).

Letting
N\to\infty
, and using the von Neumann theorem together with the fact that
\bar{X}
corresponds to
\mathcal{I}_{{T}}
, we obtain that

\begin{split}\int f(x)g(\bar{x}^{\prime})\,d\lambda|_{X\times{\bar{X}}}(x,\bar{x}^{\prime})=\int{{\mathbbm{E}}}(f\,|\,\bar{X})(\bar{x})g(\bar{x}^{\prime})\,d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x}^{\prime})\\
=\int f\,d\mu\int g\,d\bar{\mu}.\end{split}

Hence, we have

(28)

\lambda|_{X\times{\bar{X}}}=\mu\otimes\bar{\mu},

see also Section
5.6
.
If now
f\in L_{0}^{2}(X,\mu)

and if
\widetilde{f}:={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\bar{X}}]
, then (
28
) implies that

(29)

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[(f-\widetilde{f})\otimes(f-\widetilde{f})\,|\,\bar{X}\times\bar{X}]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[f\otimes f\,|\,\bar{X}\times\bar{X}]\\
-{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\bar{X}}]\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\bar{X}}]

as

(30)

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[f\otimes\widetilde{f}\,|\,\bar{X}\times\bar{X}]=(1\otimes\widetilde{f})\,{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}\bigl[(f\otimes 1)\,|\,\bar{X}\times\bar{X}\bigr]=\widetilde{f}\otimes\widetilde{f}

the latter equality due to (
28
).
Moreover, noticing that
\widetilde{f}\perp F_{\rm we}({T})
, we obtain

(31)

f\perp F_{\rm we}({T})
if and only if

f-\widetilde{f}\perp F_{\rm we}({T})
.

Therefore, in what follows, we will constantly assume that
f
satisfies

(32)

{{\mathbbm{E}}}[f\,|\,{\bar{X}}]=0.

In order to understand how natural this assumption is,
recall first the notion of Gowers-Host-Kra norms (GHK norms, for short) of
f
:

(33)

\|f\|^{2}_{u^{1}}:=\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\int f\circ{T}^{h}\cdot\overline{f}\,d\mu,

whence,

(34)

\|f\|^{2}_{u^{1}}=\Big|\int f\,d\mu\Big|^{2}\text{ if }{T}\text{ is ergodic}

and
\|f\|^{2^{s+1}}_{u^{s+1}}:=\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\|f\circ{T}^{h}\cdot\overline{f}\|_{u^{s}}^{2^{s}}
for
s\geq 1
.
Note that, by the von Neumann theorem,
\|f\|_{u^{1}}=\int|{{\mathbbm{E}}}[f\,|\,{\bar{X}}]|^{2}\,d\bar{\mu}
.
Remember that we aim at a study of (bounded) arithmetic functions
\boldsymbol{u}
through the associated Furstenberg systems
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
and we consider
f=\pi_{0}
, cf. (
4
). In other words, given a sequence
(N_{k})
which yields a Furstenberg system
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
, we can define
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{s}}
as
\|\pi_{0}\|_{u^{s}}
for the system
(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
.
In particular, we say that
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{s}}=0
if
\|\pi_{0}\|_{u^{s}}=0
for all
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
.
Hence, in the problem of classifying arithmetic functions
\boldsymbol{u}
which are orthogonal to uniquely ergodic systems, the assumption (
32
) is now explained by the following classical result (see also Section 3.4.1 in
[
9
]
):

Proposition 4.1
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(a)
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
.

(b) For each
(N_{k})
defining a Furstenberg system of
\boldsymbol{u}
, we have

\limsup_{H\to\infty}\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N}\Big|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\boldsymbol{u}(n+h)\Big|^{2}=0

(i.e. the property of “zero mean on typical short interval”).

(c)
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}]=0
for each
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
.

(d)
\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\pi_{0}\circ S^{h}=0
in
L^{2}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa)
for each
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
.

Remark 4.2
.

(For some basics about multiplicative, bounded by 1 functions and their multiplicative distance
D
, see Section
5.2
.)
A break-through theorem by Matomäki and Radziwiłł
[
29
]
established zero value for the first GHK norm for many classical multiplicative (unpretensious) functions like the Liouville or Möbius functions. But there are also pretentious multiplicative functions
\boldsymbol{u}
for which
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
. In fact, all multiplicative functions pretending being non-principal Dirichlet character
\chi
enjoy this property. Indeed, as shown in
[
13
]
, Furstenberg systems of all such arithmetic functions are ergodic, so (in view of (
34
)) our claim follows once we know that they have mean equal to zero. To see the latter claim, note that for the non-principal Dirichlet character
\chi
, we have

D(\chi,n^{it})=+\infty
for all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and
D(\boldsymbol{u},\chi)<+\infty
. It follows that
D(\boldsymbol{u},n^{it})=+\infty
as the multiplicative “distance”
D
satisfies the triangle inequality. The claim now follows from the Halás theorem.

Remark 4.3
.

Note that if all Furstenberg systems of
\boldsymbol{u}
are identities, then
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}>0
unless the Besicovitch norm vanishes
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B}=0
(see Section
5.2
). Indeed, if in the definition of
u^{1}
of
f
, the automorphism
T
is an identity then
\|f\|_{u^{1}}=\|f\|_{L^{2}}
. Hence, by an observation in
[
17
]
, all non-trivial slowly varying functions have positive GHK first norm. In particular, for all Archimedean characters, we have
\|n^{it}\|_{u^{1}}>0
.

Let us consider some examples of orthogonality to ergodic Markov images.

A.

If
{T}
is ergodic, then all
f\in L^{2}({X})
are in the Markov image of
\Phi_{\Delta_{\mu}}
, where
\Delta_{\mu}
stands for the diagonal joining on
(X,\mu)
.

B.

If for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}\in{\bar{X}}
, we have
T_{\bar{x}}\perp T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}
, then, by Theorem
3.1
, all zero mean functions are orthogonal to all ergodic Markov images.

Proposition 4.4
.

Assume that for all
\bar{x}\in{\bar{X}}
,
T_{\bar{x}}=S
(in other words,
{T}={\rm Id}_{\bar{X}}\times S
with
S
ergodic). Then for all
f\in L^{2}(\bar{\mu}\otimes\nu)
,
f\perp F_{{\rm we},0}({T})
if and only if
f
is measurable with respect to
\bar{x}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
f\perp F_{{\rm we},0}({T})
. Then, by considering the joining
\rho
of
{T}
with
S
in which we join diagonally
S
with itself and the
\bar{X}
part is independent of
Y
, and using Lemma
2.11
, for every
g\in L_{0}^{2}(Y,\nu)
, we have

(35)

\int_{Y}f(\bar{x},y)g(y)\,d\nu(y)=0

for
\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
\bar{x}\in{\bar{X}}
. This
\bar{\mu}
-a.a. condition persists if we consider a countable linearly dense set of
g\in L_{0}^{2}(Y)
.
It follows that for
\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
\bar{x}\in{\bar{X}}
,
y\mapsto f(\bar{x},y)
is
\nu
-almost everywhere equal to
\int_{Y}f(\bar{x},y)\,d\nu(y)
,
i.e.

f
is
\bar{\mu}\otimes\nu
-almost surely equal to a function of
\bar{x}
.

Conversely, if
f
is
\bar{x}
-measurable, the fact that
f\perp F_{{\rm we},0}({T})
is a consequence of the disjointness between identities and ergodic systems.

4.2
Characterization result

We aim at proving the following result.

Theorem 4.5
.

Assume that
f\in L^{2}_{0}(X,\mu)
satisfies (
32
). Then

f\perp F_{\rm we}({T})
if and only if for all
\lambda\in J^{\rm RelErg}_{2}({T})
, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[f\otimes f\,|\,\bar{X}\times\bar{X}](\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})=0

for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
.

•

Note that
\lambda\in J_{2}^{\rm RelErg}({T})
if and only if
\lambda|_{{\bar{X}}\times{\bar{X}}}=\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
and
\mathcal{I}_{{T}\times{T}}
is the sigma-algebra generated by the projection to
\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
modulo
\lambda
.

•

Under the assumption on
\lambda
,
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[f\otimes f\,|\,\bar{X}\times\bar{X}](\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})=0
for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
if and only if
\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}(f\otimes f)\circ({T}\times{T})^{n}=0
(by the von Neumann theorem).

•

Note also that (assuming the validity of Theorem
4.5
), due to Corollary
3.8
:
f\perp F_{\rm we}({T})
if and only if for all
\lambda\in J_{2}({T})
, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[f\otimes f\,|\,\bar{X}\times\bar{X}](\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})=0
for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
.

•

For another equivalent condition (important for applications), see Corollary
4.10
.

Remark 4.6
.

It follows directly from Theorem
4.5
that if
f\in L^{2}_{0}(X,\mu)
satisfies (
32
) and
f\perp F_{\rm we}({T})
then, for each
g\in L^{\infty}(\bar{X},\bar{\mu})
, we have
gf\perp F_{\rm we}({T})
.

Remark 4.7
.

If we do not want to assume (
32
) then the assertion
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[f\otimes f\,|\,\bar{X}\times\bar{X}](\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})=0
, in view of (
29
), should be replaced by

(\ast)\qquad{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[f\otimes f\,|\,\bar{X}\times\bar{X}](\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}(f\,|\,\bar{X})(\bar{x})\,{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}(f\,|\,\bar{X})(\bar{x}^{\prime})

for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
. For example, if we take
\boldsymbol{u}
such that all its Furstenberg systems are identity, then the only self-joining that we have to consider is the product measure, and the assumption
(\ast)
holds. We will see later (see Proposition
5.1
) that in this case
\boldsymbol{u}
will be orthogonal to all u.e. systems even though the function
\pi_{0}
does not satisfy (
32
).

We need the following:

Lemma 4.8
.

Assume that
\rho\in J({T},R)
with
R
ergodic. Set, by some abuse of notation
\lambda:=\rho^{\ast}\circ\rho\in J_{2}({T})

(i.e.
\lambda
is the only joining for which
\Phi_{\lambda}=\Phi_{\rho}^{\ast}\circ\Phi_{\rho}
). Then
\lambda|_{\bar{X}\times\bar{X}}=\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
.

Proof.

Since
R
is ergodic,
\rho|_{\bar{X}\times Z}=\bar{\mu}\otimes\kappa
. That is,
\Phi_{\rho}|_{L_{0}^{2}(\bar{X},\bar{\mu})}=0
. By the definition of
\lambda
,

\Phi_{\lambda}|_{L_{0}^{2}(\bar{X})}=\Phi^{\ast}_{\rho}\circ\Phi_{\rho}|_{L_{0}^{2}(\bar{X})}=0,

whence
\lambda|_{\bar{X}\times\bar{X}}=\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
.

Under the assumptions of the lemma, we have
\rho=\int_{\bar{X}}\rho_{\bar{x}}\,d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})
, where
\rho_{\bar{x}}\in J(T_{\bar{x}},R)
for
\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
\bar{x}\in{\bar{X}}
.
Moreover, by disintegrating

\lambda=\int_{\bar{X}\times\bar{X}}\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}\,d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x}^{\prime}),

we obtain that

(36)

\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}=\rho^{\ast}_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}\circ\rho_{\bar{x}}.

Indeed,

\Phi_{\lambda}=\Phi^{\ast}_{\rho}\circ\Phi_{\rho}=\int_{\bar{X}}\Phi^{\ast}_{\rho_{\bar{x}}}\,d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})\circ\int_{\bar{X}}\Phi_{\rho_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}}\,d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x}^{\prime})=\int_{\bar{X}\times\bar{X}}\Phi^{\ast}_{\rho_{\bar{x}}}\circ\Phi_{\rho_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}}\,d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x}^{\prime}).

4.3
Proof of Theorem
4.5
