. Sufficiency

Take any ergodic
R
on
(Z,\mathcal{B}_{Z},\kappa)
and let
\rho\in J(T,R)
.
Set
\lambda=\rho^{\ast}\circ\rho\in J_{2}({T})
. In view of Lemma
4.8
,
\lambda|_{\bar{X}\times\bar{X}}=\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
. Now, by Corollary
3.8
,
\lambda=\int_{0}^{1}\lambda_{t}\,dt
with
\lambda_{t}\in J_{2}^{\rm RelErg}({T})
. We then consider
f\in L^{2}(X,\mu)
such that (in particular)
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda_{t}}[f\otimes f|\,\bar{X}\times\bar{X}]=0

\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.e., whence
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[f\otimes f\,|\,\bar{X}\times\bar{X}]=0

\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.e. Hence, by (
36
), for
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}_{\infty}
(if
\bar{x}\in\bar{X}_{n}
we replace
\nu
with
\nu_{n}
in the calculation below)

0={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[f\otimes f\,|\,\bar{X}\times\bar{X}](\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})=\int f\otimes f\,d\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}=\\
\int\Phi_{\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}}(f)f|_{\{\bar{x}\}\times Y}\,d\nu=\int_{Z}\Phi_{\rho_{\bar{x}}}(f)\cdot\Phi_{\rho_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}}(f)\,d\kappa

for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
. In view of (
11
), all we need to show is that
\Phi_{\rho}(f)=0
, where
\Phi_{\rho}:L^{2}(X,\mu)\to L^{2}(Z,\kappa)
.
A point
\bar{x}\in{\bar{X}}
is called “good” if

\bar{\mu}(\{\bar{x}^{\prime}\in{\bar{X}}:\>\Phi_{\rho_{\bar{x}}}(f)\perp\Phi_{\rho_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}}(f)\})=1.

Since the set
of
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})
such that
\Phi_{\rho_{\bar{x}}}(f)\perp\Phi_{\rho_{\bar{x}^{\prime}}}(f)
is Borel, by Fubini’s theorem the set
G
of “good” points has full measure. Set

C:=\{\bar{x}\in{\bar{X}}:\>\Phi_{\rho_{\bar{x}}}(f)\neq 0\}.

Suppose that
\bar{\mu}(C)>0
. Then
\bar{\mu}(G\cap C)=\bar{\mu}(C)>0
. In the set
C\cap G
select a maximal subset
\{\bar{x}_{n}:\>n\geq 1\}
, so that
\Phi_{\rho_{\bar{x}_{n}}}(f)\perp\Phi_{\rho_{\bar{x}_{m}}}(f)
for
n\neq m
(this set is countable because
L^{2}(Z,\kappa)
is separable, and we can find a maximal set by Zorn’s lemma). Let

B_{n}:=\{\bar{x}\in{\bar{X}}:\>\Phi_{\rho_{\bar{x}}}(f)\perp\Phi_{\rho_{\bar{x}_{n}}}(f)\}.

Then
\bar{\mu}(B_{n})=1
and therefore

\bar{\mu}(\bigcap_{n\geq 1}B_{n}\cap G\cap C)>0

and adding any
\bar{x}\in\bigcap_{n\geq 1}B_{n}\cap G\cap C
makes a larger family than
\{\bar{x}_{n}:\>n\geq 1\}
, a contradiction.

It follows that the set
C
has measure zero, and this yields
\Phi_{\rho}(f)=0
.

4.4
Proof of Theorem
4.5
. Necessity

(By contraposition.)
Suppose that there exists
\lambda\in J_{2}^{\rm RelErg}({T})
for which the claim does not hold. Without loss of generality, we assume that
f
is real, and that, for
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})
in a set of positive
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-measure, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[f\otimes f\,|\,\bar{X}\times\bar{X}](\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})=\int f\otimes f\,d\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}>0.

Using Fubini’s theorem, there exists a point
{\bar{x}_{0}}
such that, for
\bar{x}
from a set
A\subset\bar{X}
of positive measure,

\int f\otimes f\,d\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}_{0}}\ >\ 0.

We set
R=T_{{\bar{x}_{0}}}
(which is ergodic, we also assume that
\bar{x}_{0}\in\bar{X}_{\infty}
), and we define
\rho\in J({T},R)
by setting (cf. Section
2.8
)

\rho:=\int\rho_{\bar{x}}\,d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})
, where
\rho_{\bar{x}}=\lambda_{\bar{x},{\bar{x}_{0}}}
for
\bar{x}\in A
, and
\rho_{\bar{x}}
equals the product measure otherwise. We easily obtain, using (
32
) for the last equality

\int\Phi_{\rho}(f)\cdot f\,d\mu=\int f\otimes f\,d\rho=\\
\int_{\bar{X}}\Big(\int f\otimes f\,d\rho_{\bar{x}}\Big)\,d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})=\int_{A}\Big(\int f\otimes f\,d\rho_{\bar{x}}\Big)\,d\bar{\mu}(\bar{x})\ >\ 0.

4.5
A condition for being orthogonal to ergodic Markov images

We need the following lemma (relativizing the case of ergodic decomposition).

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
R
be an automorphism of
(Z,\mathcal{B}_{Z},\rho)
and let
{\cal A}\subset\mathcal{I}_{R}
be a factor. Assume that
\rho=\int_{0}^{1}\rho_{t}\,dQ(t)
, where
\rho_{t}
are
R
-invariant and
{\cal A}=\mathcal{I}_{R}\mod\rho_{t}
for
Q
-a.a.
t\in[0,1]
. Let
f\in L^{\infty}(Z,\rho)
and
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\rho_{t}}[f|{\cal A}]=0
for
Q
-a.a.
t\in[0,1]
.
Then
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\rho}[f\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{R}]=0
.

Proof.

Denote
F_{N,t}:=\int_{Z}|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}f\circ R^{n}|^{2}\,d\rho_{t}
. Then, for
Q
-a.a.
t\in[0,1]
, we have
\lim_{N\to\infty}F_{N,t}=0
by the von Neumann theorem (in
L^{2}(Z,\rho_{t})
) and our assumptions. By the Lebesgue dominated theorem:
\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{0}^{1}F_{N,t}\,dQ(t)=0
. That is,

0=\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\int_{Z}\Big|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}f\circ R^{n}\Big|^{2}\,d\rho_{t}\Big)dQ(t)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{Z}\Big|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}f\circ R^{n}\Big|^{2}\,d\rho.

It follows again by the von Neumann theorem that
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\rho}[f\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{R}]=0
.

Returning to our context, by Corollary
3.8
, Theorem
4.5
and Lemma
4.9
, we obtain the following:

Corollary 4.10
.

Assume that
f\in L^{2}_{0}(X,\mu)
satisfies (
32
). Then

f\perp F_{\rm we}({T})
if and only if for all
\lambda\in J_{2}({T})
satisfying
\lambda|_{\bar{X}\times\bar{X}}=\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[f\otimes f\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{{T}\times{T}}]=0
.

Remark 4.11
.

The above corollary yields the full solution of Boshernitzan’s problem (see next section). To test whether
f\perp F_{\rm we}({T})
and assuming that
{{\mathbbm{E}}}(f|\bar{X})=0
, we only need (and it is sufficient) to show that

(\ast)\;\;\;\;\lim_{N\to\infty}\int_{{X}\times{X}}\Big|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}(f\otimes f)\circ({T}\times{T})^{n}\,d\lambda\Big|^{2}=0

for all self-joinings
\lambda
of
{T}
for which
\lambda|_{\bar{X}\times\bar{X}}=\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
. When we consider Furstenberg systems of
\boldsymbol{u}
(satisfying the condition: “zero mean on typical short interval”), then
f=\pi_{0}
and the condition
(\ast)
is expressed combinatorially (i.e. expressed in terms of properties of
\boldsymbol{u}
), see Proposition
4.1
and next sections.

Remark 4.12
.

If
{T}\perp{\rm Erg}
then of course each
f
satisfying
{{\mathbbm{E}}}(f|\bar{X})=0
is orthogonal to all ergodic images, but we can also see that the condition
(\ast)
is trivially satisfied because each
\lambda
as above will simply be product measure (as the extension
{T}\to{{\rm Id}}|_{\bar{X}}
is confined).

Remark 4.13
.

Note that if
\lambda|_{\bar{X}\times\bar{X}}=\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
then (unless
{T}
is ergodic)
\lambda
cannot be the
graph measure
{\mu}_{S}
for an element
{S}
of the centralizer
C({T})
of
{T}
. Indeed, if
S\in C({T})
then
{S}
must preserve the sigma-algebra of invariant sets. We obtain

{\mu}_{{S}}|_{\bar{X}\times\bar{X}}=\mu_{\bar{S}},

where
{\bar{S}}:={S}|_{\bar{X}}
. But
\mu_{\bar{S}}\neq\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
unless
\bar{X}
reduces to one point.

5
Orthogonality to uniquely ergodic systems

We recall that (cf. (
2
)) the meaning of orthogonality of
\boldsymbol{u}
to UE is that

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}f(T^{n}x)\boldsymbol{u}(n)=0

for each uniquely ergodic topological system
(X,T)
,
f\in C(X)
of zero mean (with respect to the unique invariant measure) and all
x\in X
.

5.1
Orthogonality and ergodic Markov images

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
. Then
\boldsymbol{u}\perp\,{\rm UE}
if and only if for each Furstenberg system
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
,
\pi_{0}\perp F_{{\rm we},0}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
(by contradiction) Suppose that for some
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
there is an ergodic system
(Z^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},R^{\prime})
and a joining
\rho^{\prime}\in J(\nu^{\prime},\kappa)
for which
\pi_{0}\not\perp{\rm Im}(\Phi_{\rho^{\prime}}|_{L^{2}_{0}(Z^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})})
. Using the Jewett-Krieger theorem, we obtain that there exists a uniquely ergodic system
(Z,\nu,R)
and a joining
\rho\in J(\nu,\kappa)
such that
\pi_{0}\not\perp{\rm Im}(\Phi_{\rho}|_{L^{2}_{0}(Z,\nu)})
(in
L^{2}(\kappa)
). It follows that there exists
f\in C(Z)
of zero mean such that

\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}\Phi_{\rho}(f)\pi_{0}\,d\kappa\neq 0
, in other words

\int_{Z\times X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}f\otimes\pi_{0}\,d\rho\neq 0
. Let
(N_{m})
satisfy
\frac{1}{N_{m}}\sum_{n\leq N_{m}}\delta_{S^{n}\boldsymbol{u}}\to\kappa
. In view of Theorem
2.1
, there exist a sequence
(z_{n})\subset Z
and a subsequence
(N_{m_{\ell}})
such that

\frac{1}{N_{m_{\ell}}}\sum_{n\leq N_{m_{\ell}}}\delta_{(z_{n},S^{n}\boldsymbol{u})}\to\rho

and the set
\{n\geq 1:\>z_{n}\neq Rz_{n-1}\}
is contained in a subset of
{{\mathbbm{N}}}
of the form
\{b_{1}<b_{2}<\ldots\}
, where

b_{k+1}-b_{k}\to\infty
. Adding if necessary some more
b_{k}
’s to this set, we may also assume that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{b_{k+1}}{b_{k}}=1
. In this way, defining
K_{\ell}:=\max\{k:b_{k}\leq N_{m_{\ell}}\}
, we get
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\frac{b_{K_{\ell}}}{N_{m_{\ell}}}=1
, and it follows that

0\neq\int f\otimes\pi_{0}\,d\rho=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{m_{\ell}}}\sum_{n\leq N_{m_{\ell}}}f(z_{n})\boldsymbol{u}(n)\ =\\
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\frac{1}{b_{K_{\ell}}}\sum_{k<K_{\ell}}\Big(\sum_{b_{k}\leq n<b_{k+1}}f(R^{n-b_{k}}z_{b_{k}})\boldsymbol{u}(n)\Big).

However, the latter limit is
0
because of Theorem
2.4
and our assumption of orthogonality on
\boldsymbol{u}
.

\Leftarrow
(by contradiction) Let
(X,T)
be uniquely ergodic. Suppose that for some subsequence
(N_{k})
we have

\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}f(T^{n}x)\boldsymbol{u}(n):=c\neq 0
(for some zero mean
f\in C(X)
and
x\in X
) and
\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\delta_{(T^{n}x,S^{n}\boldsymbol{u})}\to\rho
. Then
\rho
is a joining,
\rho\in J(\nu,\kappa)
, where
\nu
is the unique invariant measure for
T
, and
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
. Since now

c=\int f\otimes\pi_{0}\,d\rho=\int{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[f\,|\,X_{\boldsymbol{u}}]\pi_{0}\,d\kappa,

we obtain a contradiction as
\nu
is ergodic.

Remark 5.2
.

If we assume that
M(\boldsymbol{u})=0
(which is equivalent to
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}]=0
for each Furstenberg system
\kappa
of
\boldsymbol{u}
), then in the proposition above we can replace

F_{{\rm we},0}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
with
F_{{\rm we}}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
.

Remark 5.3
.

Note that in the proof we used unique ergodicity twice: each ergodic system has a uniquely ergodic model and then the use of Theorem
2.4
for orbital uniquely ergodic models. As the latter models are not (in general) minimal, the question arises whether orthogonality with respect to all uniquely ergodic systems is the same as orthogonality to all strictly ergodic systems.

Remark 5.4
.

If all Furstenberg systems of
\boldsymbol{u}
are identities then
\boldsymbol{u}
is orthogonal to all u.e. systems. Hence (see
[
17
]
) all mean slowly varying functions
are orthogonal to all u.e. systems.
In the next section we will consider Boshernitzan’s problem in the class of multiplicative functions.

Remark 5.5
.

If orthogonality is given by the logarithmic way of averaging then all the results in the paper persist, with the only change that instead of considering Furstenberg systems of
\boldsymbol{u}
we need to consider all logarithmic Furstenberg systems of
\boldsymbol{u}
. For example,
\boldsymbol{u}\perp_{\rm log}{\rm UE}
if and only if for each
\kappa\in V^{\rm log}(\boldsymbol{u})
, we have
\pi_{0}\perp F_{{\rm we},0}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
.

5.2
Pretentious multiplicative function orthogonal to all uniquely ergodic systems

We consider arithmetic functions
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
which are
multiplicative
, i.e.

(37)

\boldsymbol{u}(mn)=\boldsymbol{u}(m)\boldsymbol{u}(n)\text{ whenever }m,n\text{ are coprime},

where
{\mathbb{D}}
denotes the unit disc (if (
37
) is satisfied unconditionally then we speak about
complete multiplicativity
of
\boldsymbol{u}
). Let

\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B}:=\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}|\boldsymbol{u}(n)|

stand for the Besicovitch pseudo-norm of
\boldsymbol{u}
. For example,
\boldsymbol{u}(n):=n^{-r}
, where
r>0
, is a completely multiplicative function of zero Besicovitch pseudo-norm.
We recall that (see, e.g.
[
7
]
, Lemma 2.9)

(38)

\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B}=0\Leftrightarrow\sum_{p\in{\mathbb{P}}}\frac{1}{p}(1-|\boldsymbol{u}(p)|)=\infty

(the RHS in (
38
) yields many other possibilities to create multiplicative functions of zero Besicovitch pseudo-norm). When
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B}=0
then
\boldsymbol{u}(n)\to 0
along a subsequence of full density in
{\mathbb{N}}
. It is easy to see that in this case
\pi_{0}=0

\kappa
-a.e., where
\kappa=\delta_{\underline{0}}
is the only Furstenberg system of
\boldsymbol{u}
. It follows that multiplicative functions of Besicovitch pseudo-norm zero are orthogonal to all topological systems (this fact does not use the multiplicativity of
\boldsymbol{u}
), in particular, to all uniquely ergodic systems. Another easy case arises when
\boldsymbol{u}(n)=n^{it}
(
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
) is an Archimedean character. In this case,
\boldsymbol{u}
is mean slowly varying function, i.e. it satisfies (
8
):
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}|\boldsymbol{u}(n+1)-\boldsymbol{u}(n)|\to 0
when
N\to\infty
(in fact,
(n+1)^{it}-n^{it}\to 0
when
n\to\infty
); note also that, in general, if
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B}=0
then
\boldsymbol{u}
is mean slowly varying.
It is already remarked in
[
17
]
that mean slowly varying functions (without the multiplicativity restriction) are precisely those whose all Furstenberg systems yield identities (so we obtain functions orthogonal to all uniquely ergodic systems). Klurman
[
26
]
proved that if
\boldsymbol{u}
is multiplicative (
|\boldsymbol{u}|\leq 1
) and mean slowly varying then either
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{B}=0
or
\boldsymbol{u}
is an Archimedean character (Klurman’s theorem contains also the unbounded case:
n\mapsto n^{z}
, where
0<{\rm Re}\,z<1
which we do not consider as we assume
|\boldsymbol{u}(n)|\leq 1
).
Recall that the multiplicative “distance” between two multiplicative functions
\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
is defined as

D(\boldsymbol{u},\boldsymbol{v})^{2}:=\sum_{p\in{\mathbb{P}}}\frac{1}{p}(1-{\rm Re}(\boldsymbol{u}(p)\overline{\boldsymbol{v}(p)})).

Then,
\boldsymbol{u}
is called
pretentious
if for some
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and a Dirichlet character
\chi
, we have

D(\boldsymbol{u},\chi\cdot n^{it})<+\infty
.
Note that, in view of (
38
),
\boldsymbol{u}
is
not
pretentious whenever
\|u\|_{B}=0
.
We aim at proving the following result.

Corollary 5.6
.

The only pretentious multiplicative functions
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
orthogonal to all uniquely ergodic systems are Archimedean characters
n\mapsto n^{it}
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{u}
is not an Archimedean character. Then
D(\boldsymbol{u},\chi\cdot n^{it})<\infty
, where
\chi
is a (non-trivial) primitive Dirichlet character. If
t=0
then by Theorem 2.8(i)
[
13
]
all Furstenberg systems of
\boldsymbol{u}
are ergodic, and each of them is non-trivial (unless
\boldsymbol{u}=1
). If
t\neq 0
, then since
\boldsymbol{u}
is (by assumption) not an Archimedean character, its spectrum cannot be trivial by Theorem 2.8(ii)
[
13
]
, and it follows by the same theorem that each Furstenberg system of it is of the form “identity
\times T
” with
T
ergodic, and the ergodic part is non-trivial.
Now, since
\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}
,
\pi_{0}\perp F_{{\rm we},0}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
for each
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
in view of Proposition
5.1
.
By Proposition
4.4
, it follows that for each Furstenberg system
\kappa
,
\pi_{0}
is
\kappa
-almost-surely measurable with respect to
\mathcal{I}_{S}
. But then the sigma-algebra generated by the process
(\pi_{0}\circ S^{n})
is
\mathcal{I}_{S}
, which yields an identity action, contradicting non-triviality of the ergodic part of the Furstenberg systems.

Remark 5.7
.

In view of the recent development around the Chowla and Sarnak conjectures, it is reasonable to expect that Archimedean characters and zero Besicovitch pseudo-norm multiplicative functions are the only (bounded by 1) multiplicative functions which are orthogonal to all uniquely ergodic sequences.

5.3
Examples of sequences orthogonal to all uniquely ergodic systems

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
(X,T)
be a topological system and let
(x_{n})\subset X
satisfy:

(i) the density of
\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}\colon x_{n+1}\neq Tx_{n}\}
is zero,

(ii)
(x_{n})
is quasi-generic along
(N_{k})
for
\nu\in M(X)
(cf. Theorem
2.1
).

Then
\nu
is
T
-invariant.

Proof.

Obvious, by considering the integrals of
f,f\circ T\in C(X)
.

Remark 5.9
.

Recall that (ii) means
\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}f(x_{n})=\int f\,d\nu
for all
f\in C(X)
. A general problem arises of whether generic sequences satisfying (i) do exist. The lifting lemma (when applied directly), see Theorem
2.1
, yields the existence of such a sequence
(x_{n})
satisfying (i) and also (ii) along a subsequence
(N_{k})
; however we do not control other subsequences.

Lemma 5.10
.

Assume that
(Z,R)
is another topological system in which
z
is a generic point for a measure
\kappa
(which must be in
M(Z,R)
). Suppose that

\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\delta_{(x_{n},R^{n}z)}\to\rho\in M(X\times Z).

Then
\rho
is
T\times R
-invariant.

Proof.

The sequence
(x_{n},R^{n}z)
is generic for
\rho
along a subsequence and satisfies (i) from Lemma
5.8
for
T\times R
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Assume that in Lemma
5.8
additionally
(X,\nu,T)\in{\rm Erg}^{\perp}
for
any

(N_{k})
along which we have convergence, i.e. for each
\nu\in V((x_{n}))
. Then, for each
f\in C(X)
, we have

(\boldsymbol{u}(n):=f(x_{n}))\perp\,{\rm UE}.

Proof.

Take any
(Z,R)
u.e. (with a unique
R
-invariant measure
\kappa
). Suppose that for some
(N_{k})
and
z\in Z
, we have

\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\delta_{(x_{n},R^{n}z)}\to\rho.

By Lemma
5.10
, it follows that
\rho\in M(X\times Z,T\times R)
and, moreover, the marginals of
\rho
are
\nu
and
\kappa
, respectively. It follows that for each
\kappa
-zero mean
g\in C(Z)
, we have

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}(n)g(R^{n}z)=\int f\otimes g\,d\rho=\int f\,d\nu\cdot\int g\,d\kappa=0

by the disjointness of
(X,\nu,T)
and
(Z,\kappa,R)
.

Remark 5.12
.

Assume for simplicity that
f:X\to{\mathbb{C}}
has modulus 1. Note that for the sequence
\boldsymbol{u}
in the lemma if
\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\delta_{S^{n}\boldsymbol{u}}\to\kappa
, and

\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\delta_{x_{n}}\to\nu

then for all
m_{i},\ell_{i}\in{\mathbb{Z}}
(
i=1,\ldots,r
), if we set
g:=\prod_{i\leq r}\pi_{m_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}
, then using (i) from Lemma
5.8
to justify the fifth equality and (ii) for the last one, we have

\displaystyle\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}g\,d\kappa

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}g(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\prod_{i\leq r}\pi_{m_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\prod_{i\leq r}\boldsymbol{u}_{n+m_{i}}^{\ell_{i}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\prod_{i\leq r}\bigl(f(x_{n+m_{i}})\bigr)^{\ell_{i}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\prod_{i\leq r}\bigl(f(T^{m_{i}}x_{n})\bigr)^{\ell_{i}}=\int_{X}\prod_{i\leq r}(f\circ T^{m_{i}})^{\ell_{i}}\,d\nu.

It follows that, for any
H\geq 1
,

\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}\Big|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\pi_{0}\circ S^{h}\Big|^{2}\,d\kappa=\int_{X}\Big|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}f\circ T^{h}\Big|^{2}\,d\nu.

Hence, using the von Neumann theorem, we obtain that

(39)

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\nu}[f\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{T}]=0\text{ iff }{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}]=0.

In view of Proposition
4.1
, with the additional assumption that
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\nu}[f\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{T}]=0
, for each
\nu
for which
(x_{n})
is quasi-generic, the sequence
\boldsymbol{u}
which we obtain satisfies
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
.

Remark 5.13
.

If we also assume that the topological system
(X,T)
has zero topological entropy,
and that the set
\{n\in{\mathbb{N}}\colon x_{n+1}\neq Tx_{n}\}
is of the form
\{b_{1}<b_{2}<\cdots\}
with
b_{k+1}-b_{k}\to\infty
, then the sequence
\boldsymbol{u}=(f(x_{n}))
has zero topological entropy as well (see
[
2
, Proof of Corollary 9]
).

Now, we provide examples of sequences which are orthogonal to all uniquely ergodic systems. Fix an irrational
\alpha\in[0,1)
. We define the sequence
(w_{n})\subset{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
in the following way:

(40)

\begin{array}[]{c}(\alpha,0),(2\alpha,0),(2\alpha,2\alpha),(2\alpha,4\alpha),(2\alpha,6\alpha),\ldots\\
\ldots,(k\alpha,0),(k\alpha,k\alpha),\ldots,\bigl(k\alpha,(k^{2}-1)k\alpha\bigr),\ldots\end{array}

This sequence is the concatenation of pieces, indexed by
k\geq 1
: at stage
k
we take
(k\alpha,0)
and the initial
k^{2}
-long piece of its orbit via the map
T:(x,y)\mapsto(x,x+y)
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
, cf. Section
3.6
. We claim that the sequence we defined is generic for
\nu={\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}}\otimes{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}}
. Indeed, to see this, first notice that we only need to check the case
N=1^{2}+2^{2}+\ldots+L^{2}
as the RHS is of order
L^{3}
while the next group in the definition of
(w_{n})
has length
(L+1)^{2}
which is
o(1)
with respect to
N
. For
r,s\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, consider
F(x,y)=e^{2\pi i(rx+sy)}
(we check the weak convergence of our sequence testing on characters of
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
). Assume first that
s\neq 0
, then we have

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}F(w_{n})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\leq L}e^{2\pi irk\alpha}\sum_{j=0}^{k^{2}-1}e^{2\pi isjk\alpha}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\leq L}e^{2\pi irk\alpha}\frac{e^{2\pi ik^{2}sk\alpha}-1}{e^{2\pi isk\alpha}-1}.

Fix
\varepsilon_{0}>0
. Then, if
L
is large enough,

\Big|\{k\leq L\colon\Big|e^{2\pi ik(s\alpha)}-1\Big|\geq\varepsilon_{0}\}\Big|\geq(1-3\varepsilon_{0})L

and for each
k
in this large set, we have

\sum_{j=0}^{k^{2}-1}e^{2\pi isjk\alpha}=O(1/\varepsilon_{0}).

For the remaining
k\leq L
the sums are bounded by
k^{2}
. Thus, remembering that
\sum_{m\leq M}m^{2}=\frac{1}{3}M^{3}+O(M^{2})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}F(w_{n})\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k\leq L}O(1/\varepsilon_{0})+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=L(1-3\varepsilon_{0})}^{L}k^{2}

\displaystyle\leq O(1/\varepsilon_{0})\frac{L}{N}+\frac{1}{N}\Big(\frac{1}{3}L^{3}-\frac{1}{3}(L(1-3\varepsilon_{0}))^{3}+O(L^{2})\Big)

\displaystyle=o(1)+O(\varepsilon_{0})+\frac{O(L^{2})}{N}=o(1)+O(\varepsilon_{0})

when
L\to\infty
.

Now, let us deal with the case
(s=0,r\neq 0)
. We then write, using Abel’s summation formula,

(41)

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}F(w_{n})=\\
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq k\leq L}k^{2}e^{2\pi ikr\alpha}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq j\leq L}\bigl(j^{2}-(j-1)^{2}\bigr)\sum_{j\leq k\leq L}e^{2\pi ikr\alpha}.

For a fixed
\varepsilon_{0}>0
, we consider the contribution of
j
’s which are less than
(1-\varepsilon_{0})L
: for such a
j
, we have

\left|\frac{1}{L-j+1}\sum_{j\leq k\leq L}e^{2\pi ikr\alpha}\right|\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon_{0}L}\left|\frac{2}{1-e^{2\pi ir\alpha}}\right|=\frac{1}{L}O(1/\varepsilon_{0}),

and since

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq j\leq L}\bigl(j^{2}-(j-1)^{2}\bigr)(L-j+1)=1

(this corresponds to (
41
) when
r=0
),
we get

\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq j\leq(1-\varepsilon_{0})L}\bigl(j^{2}-(j-1)^{2}\bigr)\sum_{j\leq k\leq L}e^{2\pi ikr\alpha}\right|=\frac{1}{L}O(1/\varepsilon_{0}).

In the remaining terms, corresponding to
j>(1-\varepsilon_{0})L
, we just use

\left|\sum_{j\leq k\leq L}e^{2\pi ikr\alpha}\right|=O(\varepsilon_{0}L)

to get

\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{(1-\varepsilon_{0})L<j\leq L}\bigl(j^{2}-(j-1)^{2}\bigr)\sum_{j\leq k\leq L}e^{2\pi ikr\alpha}\right|=\frac{1}{N}O(L^{3}\varepsilon_{0})=O(\varepsilon_{0}).

We finally have for
(s=0,r\neq 0)

\Big|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}F(w_{n})\Big|=\frac{1}{L}O(1/\varepsilon_{0})+O(\varepsilon_{0}),

which concludes the proof of the genericity of the sequence
(w_{n})
.

We now apply Lemma
5.11
to
T:{\mathbb{T}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
,
T(x,y)=(x,x+y)
and
f(x,y)=e^{2\pi iy}
and the sequence (
40
) to obtain that the sequence

(42)

\begin{array}[]{c}1,1,e^{2\pi i2\alpha},e^{2\pi i4\alpha},e^{2\pi i6\alpha},\ldots\\
\ldots,1,e^{2\pi ik\alpha},\ldots,e^{2\pi i(k^{2}-1)k\alpha},\ldots\end{array}

is orthogonal to all uniquely ergodic systems.

5.4
Properties of arithmetic functions orthogonal to all u.e. systems

We start by the following consequence of Theorem
4.5
.

Corollary 5.14
.

Let
T\in{\rm Aut}(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu)
and let
f\perp F_{\rm we}({X},{\mu},{T})
. Let
\eta
be a
{T}
-invariant probability measure with
\eta\ll{\mu}
. Assume that
d\eta/d{\mu}
is bounded so that
f
can also be considered in
L^{2}(X,\eta)
. Then, we have
f\perp F_{\rm we}({X},\eta,{T})
.

Proof.

Since
(X,\mu)=\bigsqcup_{n}(\bar{X}_{n}\times\{1,\ldots,n\},\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{n}}\otimes\nu_{n})\sqcup(\bar{X}_{\infty}\times Y,\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}}\otimes\nu)
(with the action
(\bar{x},u)\mapsto(\bar{x},T_{\bar{x}}(u))
) and
f\in F_{\rm we}(X,\mu,T)
, also
f|_{X_{n}}\in F_{\rm we}(T|_{X_{n}})
. But if
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
, the action of
T|_{X_{n}}
is of the form
{\rm Id}\times R_{n}
, with
R_{n}
ergodic, whence
f|_{X_{n}}
must be
T|_{X_{n}}
-invariant by Proposition
4.4
, so it is in fact
\bar{X}_{n}
-measurable. Therefore, also
f|_{X_{n}}
will be
T|_{X_{n}}
-invariant for
\eta|_{X_{n}}
. It follows that w.l.o.g., we can assume that
T
is aperiodic, that is, we can represent
({X},{\mu},{T})
in the form
(\bar{X}\times Y,\bar{\mu}\otimes\nu,(\bar{x},y)\mapsto(\bar{x},T_{\bar{x}}y))
(with
x\mapsto T_{\bar{x}}
being the ergodic decomposition). Then
h:=d\eta/d(\bar{\mu}\otimes\nu)
is
\bar{X}
-measurable (because
\eta
is
{T}
-invariant and so is
h
). We consider the probability measure
\bar{\mu}_{h}
on
\bar{X}
defined by
d\bar{\mu}_{h}/d\bar{\mu}=h
, so that
\eta=\bar{\mu}_{h}\otimes\nu
. Since
f\perp F_{\rm we}({X},{\mu},{T})
, the condition in the statement of Theorem
4.5

(see also Remark
4.7
)
is satisfied, and we rewrite it as follows: for each measurable map
\lambda:(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\mapsto\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}\in J(T_{\bar{x}},T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}})
such that
\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}
is ergodic
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
- a.s, we have

\int_{Y\times Y}f(\bar{x},y)f(\bar{x}^{\prime},y^{\prime})\,d\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}(y,y^{\prime})=\Big(\int_{Y}f(\bar{x},y)\,d\nu(y)\Big)\Big(\int_{Y}f(\bar{x}^{\prime},y)\,d\nu(y)\Big).

But the same condition remains satisfied if we replace everywhere
\bar{\mu}
with
\bar{\mu}_{h}
. Indeed, we know (see Remark
3.9
) that there exists a map
\rho:(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\mapsto\rho_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}\in J(T_{\bar{x}},T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}})
such that
\rho_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}
is ergodic for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
- a.a.
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
. Therefore, if
\lambda:(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\mapsto\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}\in J(T_{\bar{x}},T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}})
satisfies
\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}
is ergodic
\bar{\mu}_{h}\otimes\bar{\mu}_{h}
- a.e., we can always modify this map by setting

\tilde{\lambda}_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}:=\begin{cases}\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}&\text{ if }h(\bar{x})h(\bar{x}^{\prime})>0,\\
\rho_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then
\tilde{\lambda}_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}
is ergodic for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
, and therefore, for
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})
, we have

\int_{Y\times Y}f(\bar{x},y)f(\bar{x}^{\prime},y^{\prime})\,d\tilde{\lambda}_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}(y,y^{\prime})=\Big(\int_{Y}f(\bar{x},y)\,d\nu(y)\Big)\Big(\int_{Y}f(\bar{x}^{\prime},y)\,d\nu(y)\Big).

But
\tilde{\lambda}_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}=\lambda_{\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime}}
,
\bar{\mu}_{h}\otimes\bar{\mu}_{h}
-a.s,
so again by Theorem
4.5
, this proves
f\perp F_{\rm we}({X},\eta,{T})
.

Now, we will prove the following result.

Proposition 5.15
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
,
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
, be a bounded arithmetic function orthogonal to all u.e. systems. Then
\boldsymbol{u}
is also orthogonal to all systems whose set of ergodic invariant measures is countable. More precisely: if
(Z,R)
is a topological system whose set
\{\rho_{i}\colon i\in I\}
of ergodic measures is countable, then for any
z_{0}\in Z
and any function
g\in C(Z)
,
we have

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n<N}\boldsymbol{u}(n)g(R^{n}z_{0})\to 0.

Proof.

Let
(N_{k})
be an increasing sequence along which
(u,z_{0})
is generic for some
S\times R
-invariant probability measure
\kappa
:

\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n<N_{k}}\delta_{(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u},R^{n}z_{0})}\to\kappa\text{ when }k\to\infty.

Let
\rho
be the marginal of
\kappa
on
Z
. Then
\rho=\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}\rho_{i}
for some non-negative real numbers
\alpha_{i}
and
\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}=1
. For each
i\in I
, set

Z_{i}:=\{z\in Z\colon z\text{ is generic for }\rho_{i}\}.

Then,
Z_{i}
is Borel,
R
-invariant and
Z_{i}\cap Z_{j}=\emptyset
for
i\neq j
. Moreover,
\rho(\bigcup_{i\in I}Z_{i})=1
. Let
\mu
be the marginal of

\kappa
on
X_{\boldsymbol{u}}
:
\mu
determines a Furstenberg system of
\boldsymbol{u}
. Since we assume
\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}
, Proposition
5.1
ensures that, in
L^{2}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\mu)
, we have

\pi_{0}\perp F_{\rm we}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\mu,S).

For each
i\in I
such that
\alpha_{i}>0
, we let
\kappa_{i}\ll\kappa
be the probability measure defined by

\frac{d\kappa_{i}}{d\kappa}:=\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}\mathbbm{1}_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times Z_{i}}.

This Radon-Nikodym derivative is
S\times R
-invariant, hence,
\kappa_{i}
is

S\times R
-invariant. Let
\mu_{i}
be the marginal of
\kappa_{i}
on
X_{\boldsymbol{u}}
. We have:

•

\mu_{i}\ll\mu
,

•

\mu_{i}
is
S
-invariant,

•

\frac{d\mu_{i}}{d\mu}\leq\frac{1}{\alpha_{i}}
.

Furthermore, we have

\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n<N_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}(n)g(R^{n}z_{0})\to\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times Z}\pi_{0}\otimes g\,d\kappa=\sum_{i\in I}\alpha_{i}\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times Z}\pi_{0}\otimes g\,d\kappa_{i}.

But
\kappa_{i}
is a joining of
\mu_{i}
with the ergodic measure
\rho_{i}
. By Corollary
5.14
, we have
\pi_{0}\perp F_{\rm we}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\mu_{i},S)
and finally, we get

\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n<N_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}(n)g(R^{n}z_{0})\to 0.

Remark 5.16
.

If we drop the assumption
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
, then we obtain that for
any function
g\in C(Z)
,
satisfying
\int_{Z}g\,d\rho_{i}=0
for all
i\in I
,
we have

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n<N}\boldsymbol{u}(n)g(R^{n}z)\to 0
when
N\to\infty
(for all
z\in Z
).

If we take
\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}
(
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
), we know that
\boldsymbol{u}
is in fact orthogonal to all topological systems whose set of invariant measures is countable. Using Proposition
5.15
and proceeding now as in the proof P
{}_{2}\Rightarrow{\rm P}_{3}
in Section 2.2
[
2
]
, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 5.17
.

Suppose that
\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}
(
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
). Then, for any uniquely ergodic system
(Y,S)
, for any increasing
(b_{k})
with
b_{k+1}-b_{k}\to\infty
, for all
(y_{k})\subset Y
and all
f\in C(Y)
with
\int f\,d\nu=0
(
\nu
is the unique invariant measure on
Y
), we have

\lim_{K\to\infty}\frac{1}{b_{K}}\sum_{k<K}\Big|\sum_{b_{k}\leq n<b_{k+1}}f(S^{n-b_{k}}y_{k})\boldsymbol{u}(n)\Big|=0,

i.e.
(Y,S)
satisfies the strong MOMO property (relative to
\boldsymbol{u}
).

5.5
Mean slowly varying functions are multipliers of the UE
⟂

We say that an arithmetic function
\boldsymbol{v}
is a
multiplier
for the problem of orthogonality to all u.e. systems if

\boldsymbol{u}\cdot\boldsymbol{v}\perp{\rm UE}\text{ whenever }\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}.

Note that if
\boldsymbol{v}
is a multiplier and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
satisfies

\|\boldsymbol{v}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\|_{B}=0
then also
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
is a multiplier.
Note also that
\boldsymbol{v}(n)=n^{it}
is a multiplier in the above sense since taking any u.e. sequence
(a(n))
and denoting
s_{n}:=\sum_{k\leq n}\boldsymbol{u}(k)a(k)
, we have (summation by parts):

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}\boldsymbol{v}(n)\boldsymbol{u}(n)a(n)=o(1)+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}n(\boldsymbol{v}(n+1)-\boldsymbol{v}(n))\frac{s_{n}}{n}

and the claim follows from the fact that
(n+1)^{it}-n^{it}=O(1/n)
(Archimedean characters are slowly varying functions with a speed). A natural question arises whether
we can replace here Archimedean characters by any function whose all Furstenberg systems are identities, in other words, is the above true for all mean slowly varying functions
[
17
]
? We will see that the answer to this question is positive.

We need a general lemma on mean slowly varying functions.

Lemma 5.18
.

If
\boldsymbol{v}
is a (bounded) mean slowly varying function then there exist:

•

another mean slowly varying function
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
such that

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}|\boldsymbol{v}(n)-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(n)|\to 0\text{ when }N\to\infty,

that is, the two functions are equal modulo Besicovitch pseudo-norm:
\|\boldsymbol{v}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}\|_{B}=0
,

•

an increasing sequence
(b_{k})
satisfying
b_{k+1}-b_{k}\to\infty
,

•

a bounded sequence
(z_{k})\subset{\mathbb{C}}
,

•

a sequence
0<\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
monotonically

such that for all
k\geq 1
and all
n\in\{b_{k},\ldots,b_{k+1}-1\}
we have
|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(n)-z_{k}|<\varepsilon_{k}
.

Proof.

Since
\boldsymbol{v}
is mean slowly varying, for any
\delta>0
, we have

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n<N}\mathbbm{1}_{|\boldsymbol{v}(n)-\boldsymbol{v}(n+1)|\geq\delta}\to 0\text{ when }N\to\infty.

Let us fix a decreasing sequence
0<\delta_{j}\to 0
. Then, we get an increasing sequence
(M_{j})
such that for all
j\geq 1
and all
N\geq M_{j}
, we have

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n<N}\mathbbm{1}_{|\boldsymbol{v}(n)-\boldsymbol{v}(n+1)|\geq\delta_{j}}<\frac{1}{2^{j}}.

We now define

B:=\bigcup_{j\geq 1}\{n\colon M_{j}\leq n<M_{j+1},|\boldsymbol{v}(n)-\boldsymbol{v}(n+1)|\geq\delta_{j}\}.

If
M_{j}\leq N<M_{j+1}
then we have

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n<N}\mathbbm{1}_{B}(n)\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n<N}\mathbbm{1}_{|\boldsymbol{v}(n)-\boldsymbol{v}(n+1)|\geq\delta_{j}}<\frac{1}{2^{j}}.

The
b_{k}
’s in the statement of the lemma are destined to cover this set where the sequence has large gaps. But as we want to have
b_{k+1}-b_{k}\to\infty
, we have to avoid integers in
B
which are too close to each other. Let us define more precisely what we mean: we say that two consecutive elements
b,b^{\prime}
of
B
are
too close
if

•

M_{j}\leq b<M_{j+1}
for some
j
, and

•

b^{\prime}\leq b+j
.

Then we set
C:=\bigcup_{b}\{b,b+1,\ldots,b^{\prime}-1\}
, where the union ranges over all pairs
(b,b^{\prime})
in
B
which are too close to each other. For each
n
in
C
, we now change
\boldsymbol{v}(n)
by setting
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(n):=\boldsymbol{v}(\ell(n))
, where
\ell(n)
is defined as the largest integer smaller than
n
which is not in
C
. For
n\in{\mathbb{N}}\setminus C
, we set
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(n):=\boldsymbol{v}(n)
. Define
\widetilde{B}
from
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
as we defined
B
from
\boldsymbol{v}
. We get
\widetilde{B}\subset B
, but we have removed
from
B
all integers
b
which are too close to the next element of
B
. Observe now that we have for
M_{j}\leq N<M_{j+1}

\frac{1}{N}|C\cap\{1,\ldots,N\}|\leq j\frac{1}{N}|B\cap\{1,\ldots,N\}|\leq\frac{j}{2^{j}}\to 0

when
j\to\infty
. This shows that
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n<N}|\boldsymbol{v}(n)-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(n)|\to 0
. Moreover, the set
\widetilde{B}
(where
|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(n)-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(n-1)|
is “too large”) now satisfies: if
b<b^{\prime}
are two elements in
\widetilde{B}
with
M_{j}\leq b<m_{j+1}
then
b^{\prime}-b>j
. Then, we can choose the increasing sequence
(b_{k})
such that:

•

\widetilde{B}\subset\{b_{k}\colon k\geq 1\}
,

•

if
M_{j}\leq b_{k}<M_{j+1}
then
b_{k+1}-b_{k}\in\{j,\ldots,2j-1\}
.

Then, the inequality
b_{k+1}-b_{k}\geq j
ensures that
b_{k+1}-b_{k}\to\infty
,
and the inequality
b_{k+1}-b_{k}\leq 2j
together with the fact that all
n\in\{b_{k}+1,\ldots,b_{k+1}-1\}
are not in
\widetilde{B}
ensure that for any such
n
,

|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(n)-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(b_{k})|\leq 2j\cdot\delta_{j}.

We set
z_{k}:=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(b_{k})
, and we choose
\delta_{j}
so that
2j\delta_{j}<2^{-j}
. Then we get

\sup_{n\in\{b_{k},\ldots,b_{k+1}-1\}}|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(n)-z_{k}|\to 0

when
k\to\infty
.

Proposition 5.19
.

If
\boldsymbol{v}
is mean slowly varying then
\boldsymbol{v}
is a multiplier of the class UE
⟂
.

Proof.

Using Lemma
5.18
, we only need to show that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}
is a multiplier. Given
\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}
and
(X,T)
a uniquely ergodic system with
f\in C(X)
and
x\in X
,
we have

\Big|\frac{1}{b_{K}}\sum_{k<K}\sum_{b_{k}\leq n<b_{k+1}}\boldsymbol{u}(n)\widetilde{\boldsymbol{v}}(n)f(T^{n}x)\Big|\leq

\frac{1}{b_{K}}\sum_{k<K}|z_{k}|\Big|\sum_{b_{k}\leq n<b_{k+1}}\boldsymbol{u}(n)f(T^{n}x)\Big|+\Big(\sum_{k<K}\varepsilon_{k}\frac{b_{k+1}-b_{k}}{b_{K}}\Big)\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{\infty}\|f\|_{\infty}

and when
K\to\infty
, the first summand is going to zero because of the strong MOMO property (Corollary
5.17
), while the second also goes to zero since
\varepsilon_{k}\to 0
.

We have been unable to answer the converse: Is every multiplier of
{\rm UE^{\perp}}
a mean slowly varying function?

5.6
How to recognize that a self-joining of a Furstenberg system has product measure as projection?

So we have a Furstenberg system
(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
of
\boldsymbol{u}
with identifications

(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa)=\bigsqcup_{n}(\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u},n}\times\{1,\ldots,n\},\overline{\kappa}|_{\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u},n}}\otimes\nu_{n})\sqcup(\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u},\infty}\times Y,\overline{\kappa}|_{\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u},\infty}}\otimes\nu)

Let
\rho\in J_{2}(S,\kappa)
.
Then, in view of (
28
),

\rho|_{\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u}}}=\overline{\kappa}\otimes\overline{\kappa}\text{ if and only if }\rho|_{(X_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u}})}=\kappa\otimes\overline{\kappa}

and the latter holds if and only if
for all “monomials”
P=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\pi_{0}^{p_{j}}\circ S^{r_{j}}
and
Q=\prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\pi_{0}^{q_{j}}\circ S^{s_{j}}
, we have

\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}P(x)\,{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[Q\,|\,\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u}}](x^{\prime})\,d\rho(x,x^{\prime})=\\
\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}P\,d\kappa\int_{\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u}}}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[Q\,|\,\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u}}](\bar{x}^{\prime})\,d\overline{\kappa}(\bar{x}^{\prime})=\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}P\,d\kappa\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}Q\,d\kappa.

Now, in
L^{2}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa)
, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[Q\,|\,\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u}}]=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}Q\circ S^{n}.

In this equality, we can replace
L^{2}(\kappa)
with
L^{2}(\rho)
which yields

\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}P(x)\,{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[Q\,|\,\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u}}](x^{\prime})\,d\rho(x,x^{\prime})=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}P\otimes Q\circ S^{n}\,d\rho.

Collecting the remarks above, we have proved the following result:

Proposition 5.20
.

Under the notation above, let
\rho\in J_{2}(S,\kappa)
. Then
\rho|_{\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u}}}=\overline{\kappa}\otimes\overline{\kappa}
if and only if we have

(43)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}P\otimes Q\circ S^{n}\,d\rho=\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}P\,d\kappa\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}Q\,d\kappa

for all monomials
P,Q
.

An alternative arises by using, instead of “monomials”, the characteristic functions
\mathbbm{1}_{[B]_{0}}
:
B\in A^{k}
, where
A

is the (finite) set of values of
\boldsymbol{u}
and
k\geq 1
(here
[B]_{s}=\{x\in X_{\boldsymbol{u}}\colon\boldsymbol{u}[s,s+k-1]=B\}
. As shifts of such functions yield a linearly dense subset in
L^{2}(\kappa)
, by repeating all arguments that led to Proposition
5.20
, we obtain the following:

Corollary 5.21
.

Under the notation above, let
\rho\in J_{2}(S,\kappa)
. Then
\rho|_{\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u}}}=\overline{\kappa}\otimes\overline{\kappa}
if and only if we have

(44)

\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}\rho([B]_{0}\times[C]_{-n})=\kappa([B]_{0})\kappa([C])

for all blocks
B\in A^{k},C\in A^{\ell}
with
k,\ell\geq 1
.

Remark 5.22
.

An equivalent form of this corollary is that: if
\Phi_{\rho}
stands for the Markov operator corresponding to
\rho
then
\Phi_{\rho}|_{L^{2}(\mathcal{I}_{S})}=0
if and only if
\Phi_{\rho}\circ\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}S^{n}\to 0
weakly in
L_{0}^{2}(\kappa)
. Indeed,
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}S^{n}\to{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\cdot\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}]
(in
L^{2}(\kappa)
) by the von Neumann theorem.

Note that the quantities we have: the LHS terms and the RHS term in (
43
) and in (
44
) are computable through the generic sequence
(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u},S^{\phi_{k}(n)}\boldsymbol{u})
along
(N_{k})
, see Proposition
8.1
, and
the generic point
\boldsymbol{u}
along
(N_{k})
, respectively.

5.7
Summing up

Our aim is to describe those
\boldsymbol{u}
which are orthogonal to all u.e. sequences (we assume that
\boldsymbol{u}
has zero mean on typical short interval). For that, for all Furstenberg systems
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
we need to check the assertion of Corollary
4.10
. In this corollary, we need to deal with some self-joinings
\lambda
(of
\kappa
). In fact, all of them are described combinatorially, using only
\boldsymbol{u}
, see Proposition
8.1
. We need to check the assertion of Corollary
4.10
, which by Remark
4.11
(see
(\ast)
there) is

\lim_{L\to\infty}\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}\Big|\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell\leq L}(\pi_{0}\otimes\pi_{0})(S\times S)^{\ell}\Big|^{2}d\lambda=0.

That is, given
\varepsilon>0
, for
L>L_{0}
, we want to see

\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}\Big|\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell\leq L}(\pi_{0}\otimes\pi_{0})(S\times S)^{\ell}\Big|^{2}d\lambda<\varepsilon,

where the integral is computable along a subsequence
(N_{k})
(in fact, a subsequence of it), so (using Proposition
8.1
), we need

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\Big|\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell\leq L}(\pi_{0}\otimes\pi_{0})\circ(S\times S)^{\ell}(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u},S^{\phi_{k}(n)}\boldsymbol{u})\Big|^{2}=

\limsup_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\Big|\frac{1}{L}\sum_{\ell\leq L}\boldsymbol{u}(n+\ell)\boldsymbol{u}(\phi_{k}(n)+\ell)\Big|^{2}<\varepsilon,

which is, from the combinatorial point of view, a certain condition on the behaviour on short intervals. However, it is clear that if we consider all self-joinings, then, the above condition is not satisfied (take the diagonal self-joining). The key here is that we only consider those
\lambda
which satisfy (
44
) (or (
43
)).

Part II

6
Characteristic classes and orthogonality to uniquely ergodic systems

6.1
Characteristic classes and disjointness

In what follows, we consider
{\cal F}
a characteristic class.
We recall that each non-trivial characteristic class contains the class ID of all identities
[
23
]
.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
{T}\in{\rm Aut}({X},{\mu})
and let
{\cal F}
be a characteristic class. Let
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}
be the largest
{\cal F}
-factor of
{T}
. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(i)
{T}\perp{\cal F}\cap{\rm Erg}
,

(ii)
{T}|_{{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}}\perp{\cal F}\cap{\rm Erg}
,

(iii)
{T}|_{{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}}\perp{\rm Erg}
.

Proof.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i) Take any
R
ergodic in
{\cal F}
. Then
{T}|_{{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}}
is disjoint with
R
by (ii), and this disjointness lifts to
T
by Corollary
2.14
, as
R\in{\cal F}
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(iii) Take any
R
ergodic. Then
{T}|_{{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}}
is disjoint with the largest
{\cal F}
-factor of
R
(by (ii)), and then this disjointness lifts to
R
by Corollary
2.14
, as
{T}|_{{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}}\in{\cal F}
.

The other implications are straightforward.

Remembering that
\mathcal{I}_{{T}}\subset{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}\mod{\mu}
and using Proposition
6.1
together with Theorem
3.1
, we obtain the following.

Corollary 6.2
.

{T}\perp{\cal F}\cap{\rm Erg}
if and only if

{T}|_{{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}}
is a confined extension of the sigma-algebra of invariant sets.

Before, we formulate a result which resembles more Theorem
3.1
than the above corollary, let us consider two examples.

Example 6.3
.

Let
{\cal F}
=DISP be the (characteristic) class of all automorphisms with discrete spectrum. Let
T:{\mathbb{T}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
,
T(x,y)=(x,y+x)
(considered with Leb
{}_{{\mathbb{T}}^{2}}
). It is not hard to see that
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}
is precisely
\mathcal{I}_{T}
(that is, the sigma-algebra of the first coordinate). Indeed, note that on
L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}^{2})\ominus L^{2}({\mathbb{T}}\otimes\{\emptyset,{\mathbb{T}}\})
the spectrum is purely Lebesgue. We also have
T\in{\rm Erg}^{\perp}
. Furthermore, for any automorphism from Erg
⟂
its only eigenvalue is 1, so its Kronecker factor is
always
the sigma-algebra of invariant sets, while for the fiber automorphisms we can have discrete spectrum. On the other hand, for our
T
, on a.a. fibers (ergodic components) the spectrum is discrete, so on a.a. fibers the largest
{\cal F}
-factor is the whole space. In other words, the restriction of the factor
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}
to fibers
does not
give the (largest)
{\cal F}
-factors on the fibers.

Example 6.4
.

Let
{\cal F}=
ZE be the class of automorphisms with zero entropy. According to
[
23
]
, this is the largest (proper) characteristic class. Assume that
{T}
is of the form
{T}(\bar{x},u)=(\bar{x},T_{\bar{x}}u)
acting on

X=\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}\bar{X}_{n}\times\{1,\ldots,n\}\sqcup\bar{X}_{\infty}\times Y
, where
\mu|_{\bar{X}_{n}\times\{1,\ldots,n\}}=\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{n}}\otimes\nu_{n}
and

\mu|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}\times Y}=\bar{\mu}|_{\bar{X}_{\infty}}\otimes\nu

(note that for each
2\leq n\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
T|_{X_{n}}
is not disjoint with
R_{n}
which is ergodic with zero entropy).
In Proposition
10.1
below, we will show that contrary to the phenomenon in the previous example, here, the largest
{\cal F}
-factor (the Pinsker sigma-algebra) of
{T}
is also the largest
{\cal F}
-factor for a.a. fiber automorphisms. By applying Theorem
3.1
(the equivalence of (ii) and (iii)), we hence obtain the following result.

Corollary 6.5
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(i)

{T}\perp{\rm Erg}\cap{\rm ZE}
.

(ii) The extension
{T}|_{\Pi({T})}\to{\color[rgb]{0.58,0,0.828}{\rm Id}}_{\bar{X}}
is confined.

(iii) For
\bar{\mu}\otimes\bar{\mu}
-a.a.
(\bar{x},\bar{x}^{\prime})\in\bar{X}\times\bar{X}
, we have
\Pi(T_{\bar{x}})\perp\Pi(T_{\bar{x}^{\prime}})
.

In particular, if the fiber automorphisms
T_{\bar{x}}
are Kolmogorov, then
{T}\perp{\rm Erg}\cap{\rm ZE}
.

Example 6.6
.

Let
\bar{X}=(0,1]
,
Y=SL_{2}({\mathbb{R}})/SL_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
(considered with the corresponding Haar measure) and
{T}(t,x\Gamma):=(t,g_{t}x\Gamma)
, where
\Gamma=SL_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
,
g_{t}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}e^{-t}&0\\
0&e^{t}\end{array}\right]
. Then
{T}\perp{\rm Erg}\cap{\rm ZE}
while
{T}
is not disjoint with Erg (because the fiber automorphisms are not a.a. disjoint;
T_{t}
is Bernoulli with entropy
t
) neither with ZE (its Pinsker factor equal to the sigma-algebra of invariant sets is non-trivial).

On the other hand, note that
{T}
in Example
6.6
has no non-trivial ergodic factor as each such would have to be a factor of a.a. ergodic components which is in conflict with entropy on the fibers.

Remark 6.7
.

Two further classes: ID and NIL
1
(as proved in
[
23
]
, the latter class consists of automorphisms whose a.a. ergodic components have discrete spectrum) behave similarly to ZE. For the ID class simply the trace of the sigma-algebra of invariant sets is the trivial sigma-algebra on each fiber. For the NIL
1
class the largest
{\cal F}
-factor is the relative Kronecker over the sigma-algebra of invariant sets. It is proved in
[
23
]
that this factor comes exactly from the Kronecker factors on the fibers and, in the ergodic case, the relative Kronecker factor over the sigma-algebra of invariant sets is exactly the Kronecker factor.

On the base of the above, we have:

Conjecture:

Corollary
6.5
holds for each characteristic class
{\cal F}
satisfying
{\cal F}={\cal F}_{\rm ec}
, that is, almost every ergodic component of a member in
{\cal F}
also belongs to
{\cal F}
. (Note that the last three classes satisfy
{\cal F}={\cal F}_{\rm ec}
.)

6.2
Orthogonality to zero entropy and uniquely ergodic systems. General characteristic class case

We now merge Boshernitzan’s problem with the approach of
[
23
]
to characterize
\boldsymbol{u}
orthogonal to the systems whose invariant measures determine systems belonging to a fixed characteristic class. As noticed in Proposition
2.16
, we do not need to resort to the so-called ec-classes in the setting of Boshernitzan’s problem.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
. Let
{\cal F}
be any non-trivial characteristic class. Then
\boldsymbol{u}\perp
uniquely ergodic systems in
{\cal F}
if and only if for each Furstenberg system
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
,

(45)

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{\cal F}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)]\perp F_{\rm we}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S).

Proof.

\Rightarrow
Assume that
\boldsymbol{u}\perp\rm{UE}\cap{\cal F}
, and suppose that for some
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
there are an ergodic system
(Z^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},R^{\prime})
, a joining
\rho^{\prime}\in J(\nu^{\prime},\kappa)
and a function
g\in L^{2}_{0}(\nu^{\prime})
for which

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)]\not\perp\Phi_{\rho^{\prime}}(g).

Since the function on the left-hand side is measurable with respect to a factor in the class
{\cal F}
, we can replace above
g
by
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\nu^{\prime}}[g\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(Z^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},R^{\prime})]
.
Thus, replacing if necessary
(Z^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},R^{\prime})
by its largest
{\cal F}
-factor, we can assume with no loss of generality that
R^{\prime}\in{\cal F}
. But once we know that, we have

0\neq{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}\bigl[{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)]\,\Phi_{\rho^{\prime}}(g)\bigr]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}\bigl[\pi_{0}\,\Phi_{\rho^{\prime}}(g)\bigr].

Then we proceed as in the proof of Proposition
5.1
: Using the Jewett-Krieger theorem, we obtain that there exists a uniquely ergodic system (belonging to
{\cal F}
)
(Z,\nu,R)
and a joining
\rho\in J(\nu,\kappa)
such that
\pi_{0}\not\perp{\rm Im}(\Phi_{\rho})
(in
L^{2}(\kappa)
). It follows that there exists
f\in C(Z)
such that
\int\Phi_{\rho}(f)\,\pi_{0}\,d\kappa\neq 0
, in other words

\int_{Z\times X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}f\otimes\pi_{0}\,d\rho\neq 0.

Let
(N_{m})
satisfy
\frac{1}{N_{m}}\sum_{n\leq N_{m}}\delta_{S^{n}\boldsymbol{u}}\to\kappa
. In view of Theorem
2.1
, there exist a sequence
(z_{n})\subset Z
and a subsequence
(N_{m_{\ell}})
such that

\frac{1}{N_{m_{\ell}}}\sum_{n\leq N_{m_{\ell}}}\delta_{(z_{n},S^{n}\boldsymbol{u})}\to\rho

and the set
\{n\geq 1:\>z_{n}\neq Rz_{n-1}\}
is contained in a subset of
{{\mathbbm{N}}}
of the form
\{b_{1}<b_{2}<\ldots\}
, where

b_{k+1}-b_{k}\to\infty
. Adding if necessary some more
b_{k}
’s to this set, we may also assume that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{b_{k+1}}{b_{k}}=1
. In this way, defining
K_{\ell}:=\max\{k:b_{k}\leq N_{m_{\ell}}\}
, we get
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\frac{b_{K_{\ell}}}{N_{m_{\ell}}}=1
, and it follows that

0\neq\int f\otimes\pi_{0}\,d\rho=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{m_{\ell}}}\sum_{n\leq N_{m_{\ell}}}f(z_{n})\boldsymbol{u}(n)\ =\\
\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\frac{1}{b_{K_{\ell}}}\sum_{k<K_{\ell}}\Big(\sum_{b_{k}\leq n<b_{k+1}}f(R^{n-b_{k}}z_{b_{k}})\boldsymbol{u}(n)\Big).

However, from our assumption of orthogonality on
\boldsymbol{u}
, the latter limit is
0
because the sequence
\bigl(f(z_{n})\bigr)
can be observed in the orbital system described in Theorem
2.4
, which is uniquely ergodic and in
{\cal F}
.

\Leftarrow
Let
(X,T)
be uniquely ergodic and in
{\cal F}
. Suppose that, for some
f\in C(X)
, some
x\in X
and some increasing sequence
(N_{k})
we have the existence of the limit

c:=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}f(T^{n}x)\boldsymbol{u}(n).

Extracting a subsequence if necessary, we can also assume that

\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\delta_{(T^{n}x,S^{n}\boldsymbol{u})}\to\rho.

Then
\rho
is a joining,
\rho\in J(\nu,\kappa)
, where
\nu
is the unique invariant measure for
T
, and
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
.
Now, since
(X,\nu,T)\in{\cal F}
, we get by Theorem
2.13

c=\int f\otimes\pi_{0}\,d\rho=\int f\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)]\,d\rho.

So, if we assume that

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)]\perp F_{\rm we}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S),

we get
c=0
.

Remark 6.9
.

The above result also holds in the logarithmic case. Now, by Frantzikinakis-Host’s theorem
[
11
]
, all zero entropy, uniquely ergodic systems are Möbius orthogonal. By the above Proposition
6.8
, the relevant Veech condition is satisfied for
\boldsymbol{\mu}
. However, to read a combinatorial reformulation of Frantzikinakis-Host’s theorem is unclear because we have been unable to get a logarithmic version of Proposition
8.1
(see Section
8.4
).

Note that if for
{\cal F}
we take the class of all measure-preserving systems, then we obtain the original Boshernitzan’s problem, and we recover the result stated in Proposition
5.1
.

Proposition
6.8
allows us to use directly Theorem
4.5
and Corollary
4.10
(with
{T}
replaced by any Furstenberg system of
\boldsymbol{u}
).

Corollary 6.10
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(a)
\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\cal F}\cap{\rm UE}
.

(b) For each Furstenberg system
\kappa
of
\boldsymbol{u}
the following holds: for each
\lambda\in J^{\rm RelErg}_{2}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
(in particular,
\lambda|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{S}}=\kappa|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}}\otimes\kappa|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}}
), we have

(46)

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}\Bigl[{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}]\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}]\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{S}\Bigr]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}]\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}].

(c) For each Furstenberg system
\kappa
of
\boldsymbol{u}
the following holds: for each
\lambda\in J_{2}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
with
\lambda|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{S}}=\kappa|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}}\otimes\kappa|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}}
, we have

(47)

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}\Bigl[{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}]\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}]\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S\times S}\Bigr]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}]\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}].

6.3
Orthogonality to systems with countably many ergodic invariant measures, all in
{\cal F}

The purpose of this section is to prove the following generalization of Proposition
5.15
.

Proposition 6.11
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
,
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
, be a bounded arithmetic function orthogonal to all u.e. systems in some characteristic class
{\cal F}
. Then
\boldsymbol{u}
is also orthogonal to all systems whose set of ergodic invariant measures is countable, all of them giving rise to systems in
{\cal F}
. More precisely: if
(Z,R)
is a topological system whose set
\{\rho_{i}\colon i\in I\}
of ergodic measures is countable, and satisfy for all
i
,
(Z,\rho_{i},R)\in{\cal F}
, then for any
z_{0}\in Z
and any function
g\in C(Z)
,
we have

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n<N}\boldsymbol{u}(n)g(R^{n}z_{0})\to 0.

The proof of the above proposition follows essentially the same lines as the proof of Proposition
5.15
, but with a further refinement to take into account the underlying characteristic class
{\cal F}
. This is what the lemmas below are made for.
The framework of these lemmas is quite the same as in Corollary
5.14
: we have a measure-preserving system
(X,\mu,T)
, and a
T
-invariant probability measure
\eta
such that
\eta\ll\mu
, and
\phi:=d\eta/d\mu
is assumed to be bounded. We identify elements of
L^{\infty}(\eta)
with elements of
L^{\infty}(\mu)
which vanish on
\{x:\phi(x)=0\}
. We constantly use this identification in the following lemmas.

Lemma 6.12
.

In the framework described above, let
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
satisfy
f=0
on
\{x:\phi(x)=0\}
.
Then, we have the equivalence:

f\text{ is }{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)\text{-measurable}\Longleftrightarrow f\text{ is }{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\eta,T)\text{-measurable}.

Proof.

Let
0<\alpha<1
be such that
\alpha\phi\leq 1
, so that
\alpha\eta\leq\mu
. For clarity, we will use the notation
X_{\mu}
(respectively,
X_{\eta}
) for the space
X
endowed with the measure
\mu
(respectively the measure
\eta
). We first construct a joining
\lambda
of
(X_{\mu},\mu,T)
and
(X_{\eta},\eta,T)
by setting, for all measurable subsets
A\subset X_{\mu}
,
B\subset X_{\eta}
,

\displaystyle\lambda(A\times B)

\displaystyle:=\int_{A}\Bigl(\alpha\phi\mathbbmss{1}_{B}+\bigl(1-\alpha\phi\bigr)\eta(B)\Bigr)\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\alpha\eta(A\cap B)+\bigl(\mu(A)-\alpha\eta(A)\bigr)\eta(B)

(note that the associated Markov operator
\Phi_{\lambda}:L^{2}(X,\eta)\to L^{2}(X,\mu)
is given by
\Phi_{\lambda}(f)=\alpha\phi f+(1-\alpha\phi)\int f\,d\eta
).
In particular, the conditional distribution of the second coordinate
x_{\eta}
given the first one
x_{\mu}
is

\alpha\phi(x_{\mu})\delta_{x_{\mu}}+\bigl(1-\alpha\phi(x_{\mu})\bigr)\eta.

We then construct the joining
\lambda_{\infty}^{\eta}
as the relative product of infinitely many copies of
(X_{\mu}\times X_{\eta},\lambda)
over
(X_{\mu},\mu)
:
this is the
T\times T^{{{\mathbbm{N}}}}
- invariant probability measure on
X_{\mu}\times X_{\eta}^{{{\mathbbm{N}}}}
whose marginal on
X_{\mu}
is
\mu
, and whose conditional distribution on
X_{\eta}^{{\mathbbm{N}}}
given
x_{\mu}
is the infinite product measure

\Bigl(\alpha\phi(x_{\mu})\delta_{x_{\mu}}+\bigl(1-\alpha\phi(x_{\mu})\bigr)\eta\Bigr)^{\otimes{{\mathbbm{N}}}}.

By applying the law of large numbers in each fiber determined by
x_{\mu}
, we get that for
\lambda_{\infty}^{\eta}
-almost all
\bigl(x_{\mu},(x_{\eta}^{k})_{k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}\bigr)
, the limit

\ell=\ell\bigl(x_{\mu},(x_{\eta}^{k})_{k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}\bigr):=\lim_{K\to\infty}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}f(x_{\eta}^{k})

exists, and satisfy

\ell=\alpha\phi(x_{\mu})f(x_{\mu})+\bigl(1-\alpha\phi(x_{\mu})\bigr)\int f\,d\eta.

It follows that,
\lambda_{\infty}^{\eta}
-almost surely, we can get the value
f(x_{\mu})
by the formula

f(x_{\mu})=\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi(x_{\mu})>0}\dfrac{\ell-\bigl(1-\alpha\phi(x_{\mu})\bigr)\int f\,d\eta}{\alpha\phi(x_{\mu})}.

Now, if
f
is
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X_{\eta},\eta,T)
-measurable, the limit
\ell
is measurable with respect to an infinite joining of
{\cal F}
-systems (namely the infinite self-joining of
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X_{\eta},\eta,T)
arising from
\lambda_{\infty}^{\eta}
). Moreover, as
\phi
is
T
-invariant, and since the class
{\cal F}
must contain the ID class,
\bigl(x_{\mu},(x_{\eta}^{k})_{k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}\bigr)\mapsto\phi(x_{\mu})
is measurable with respect to
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X_{\mu}\times X_{\eta}^{{{\mathbbm{N}}}},\lambda_{\infty}^{\eta},T\times T^{{{\mathbbm{N}}}})
. Therefore, the same holds for
\bigl(x_{\mu},(x_{\eta}^{k})_{k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}\bigr)\mapsto f(x_{\mu})
, and this proves that
f
is
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)
-measurable.

Conversely, assume that
f
is
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)
-measurable. We will use the same joining
\lambda
as before, but now we disintegrate it with respect to
x_{\eta}
: we have for all measurable subsets
A\subset X_{\mu}
,
B\subset X_{\eta}
,

\displaystyle\lambda(A\times B)

\displaystyle=\int_{X_{\mu}}\mathbbmss{1}_{A}(x_{\mu})\left(\int_{X_{\eta}}\mathbbmss{1}_{B}(x_{\eta})\left(\alpha\phi(x_{\mu})\,d\delta_{x_{\mu}}(x_{\eta})+\bigl(1-\alpha\phi(x_{\mu})\bigr)d\eta(x_{\eta})\right)\right)d\mu(x_{\mu})

\displaystyle=\int_{X_{\eta}}\mathbbmss{1}_{B}(x_{\eta})\int_{X_{\mu}}\mathbbmss{1}_{A}(x_{\mu})\left(\alpha\,d\delta_{x_{\eta}}(x_{\mu})+(1-\alpha)\frac{1-\alpha\phi(x_{\mu})}{1-\alpha}d\mu(x_{\mu})\right)d\eta(x_{\eta}).

Thus, under
\lambda
, the conditional distribution of the first coordinate
x_{\mu}
given the second one
x_{\eta}
is

\alpha\delta_{x_{\eta}}+(1-\alpha)\frac{\mu-\alpha\eta}{1-\alpha}.

Now, we construct the joining
\lambda_{\infty}^{\mu}
as the relative product of infinitely many copies of
(X_{\mu}\times X_{\eta},\lambda)
over
(X_{\eta},\eta)
:
this is the
T\times T^{{{\mathbbm{N}}}}
- invariant probability measure on
X_{\eta}\times X_{\mu}^{{{\mathbbm{N}}}}
whose marginal on
X_{\eta}
is
\eta
, and whose conditional distribution on
X_{\mu}^{{\mathbbm{N}}}
given
x_{\eta}
is the infinite product measure

\Bigl(\alpha\delta_{x_{\eta}}+(1-\alpha)\frac{\mu-\alpha\eta}{1-\alpha}\Bigr)^{\otimes{{\mathbbm{N}}}}.

Again, we apply the law of large number in each fiber determined by
x_{\eta}
. We get that for
\lambda_{\infty}^{\mu}
-almost all
\bigl(x_{\eta},(x_{\mu}^{k})_{k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}\bigr)
, the limit

\bar{\ell}=\bar{\ell}\bigl(x_{\eta},(x_{\mu}^{k})_{k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}\bigr):=\lim_{K\to\infty}\frac{1}{K}\sum_{k=1}^{K}f(x_{\mu}^{k})

exists, and satisfy

\bar{\ell}=\alpha f(x_{\eta})+\int f\,d\mu-\alpha\int f\,d\eta.

It follows that,
\lambda_{\infty}^{\mu}
-almost surely, we can get the value
f(x_{\eta})
by the formula

f(x_{\eta})=\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(\bar{\ell}-\int f\,d\mu+\alpha\int f\,d\eta\right).

But, since we assume here that
f
is
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)
-measurable, the limit
\bar{\ell}
is measurable with respect to an infinite joining of
{\cal F}
-systems. Therefore, the function
\bigl(x_{\eta},(x_{\mu}^{k})_{k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}\bigr)\mapsto f(x_{\eta})
is measurable with respect to
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X_{\eta}\times X_{\mu}^{{{\mathbbm{N}}}},\lambda_{\infty}^{\mu},T\times T^{{{\mathbbm{N}}}})
, and we conclude that
f
is
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\eta,T)
-measurable.

Lemma 6.13
.

We keep the same framework as in Lemma
6.12
. For all
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\eta}[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\eta,T)]=\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi>0}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi>0}f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)].

Proof.

The equality

\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi>0}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi>0}f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)]

is a straightforward consequence of the fact that
\phi
, being
T
-invariant, is
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)
-measurable. It remains to prove that

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\eta}[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\eta,T)]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi>0}f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)].

The right-hand side is a bounded,
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)
-measurable function which vanishes on the set
(\phi=0)
, so, by applying Lemma
6.12
, it is also
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\eta,T)
-measurable. So, we just have to check that for a given bounded
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\eta,T)
-measurable function g, we have

(48)

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\eta}[gf]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\eta}\Bigl[g\,{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi>0}f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)]\Bigr].

But this can be done through the following chain of equalities:

\displaystyle{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\eta}[gf]

\displaystyle={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\phi gf]\quad\text{(by the definition of $\phi$)}

\displaystyle={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\phi g\,\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi>0}f]

\displaystyle={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\Bigl[\phi g\,{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi>0}f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)]\Bigr]

\displaystyle={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\eta}\Bigl[g\,{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi>0}f\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)]\Bigr].

The justification of the third equality comes from Lemma
6.12
applied to
\phi g
, which proves that this function is
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)
-measurable.

Proof of Proposition
6.11
.

We repeat verbatim the proof of Proposition
5.15
to define
\kappa
,
\rho
,
(\alpha_{i})_{i\in I}
,
(\kappa_{i})_{i\in I}
and
(\mu_{i})_{i\in I}
. Our assumption now is that
\boldsymbol{u}\perp\text{UE}\cap{\cal F}
, so, by Proposition
6.8
, we get

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{\cal F}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\mu,S)]\perp F_{\rm we}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\mu,S)

for all Furstenberg system
\mu\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
. It follows from Remark
4.6
that if we multiply
\pi_{0}
by a bounded
T
-invariant function, then the product remains orthogonal to
F_{\rm we}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\mu,S)
. Therefore, setting for all
i\in I
,
\phi_{i}:=\frac{d\mu_{i}}{d\mu}
, we still have

\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi_{i}>0}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{\cal F}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\mu,S)]\perp F_{\rm we}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\mu,S).

We can apply Corollary
5.14
to
\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi_{i}>0}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{\cal F}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\mu,S)]
, which yields

\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi_{i}>0}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{\cal F}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\mu,S)]\perp F_{\rm we}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\mu_{i},S),

and then Lemma
6.13
gives

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{i}}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{\cal F}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\mu_{i},S)]\perp F_{\rm we}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\mu_{i},S).

Since
\kappa_{i}
is a joining between
(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\mu_{i},S)
and the ergodic system
(Z,\rho_{i},R)
in
{\cal F}
, the above property ensures by Theorem
2.13
that

\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}\times Z}\pi_{0}\otimes g\,d\kappa_{i}=0.

Then we can conclude as in the proof of Proposition
5.15
.

As a consequence of Lemma
6.12
, we are also ready to prove Proposition
2.17
.

Proof of Proposition
2.17
.

We assume that
\mu=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\alpha_{i}\mu_{i}
, where
\mu_{i}
are
T
-invariant, ergodic and mutually singular. For all
i\geq 1
, set
\phi_{i}:=\frac{d\mu_{i}}{d\mu}
. We can write any
f\in L^{\infty}(X,\mu_{i})
as

f=\sum_{i\geq 1}f\,\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi_{i}>0}.

Since
\phi_{i}
is
T
-invariant,
\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi_{i}>0}
is measurable with respect to
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)
. Moreover, we can apply Lemma
6.12
to the function
f\,\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi_{i}>0}
: this function is measurable with respect to
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)
if and only if it is measurable with respect to
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu_{i},T)
. It follows that the following conditions are equivalent:

•

f
is
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)
-measurable,

•

for each
i
,
f\,\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi_{i}>0}
is
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu,T)
-measurable,

•

for each
i
,
f\,\mathbbmss{1}_{\phi_{i}>0}
is
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X,\mu_{i},T)
-measurable.

The equivalence in the statement of the proposition follows easily.

6.4
Examples

6.4.1
The ID class

Example 6.14
.

Consider the class ID of all identities. Then
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}]
is
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}
-measurable, where
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}=\mathcal{I}_{S}
, and

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}]\otimes{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}]
is
\mathcal{I}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{S}
-measurable, so (
46
) holds. It follows the assertion (i) of Corollary
6.10
is satisfied. Hence the only restriction on
\pi_{0}
(and
\kappa
) is (
10
), i.e. the mean of
\boldsymbol{u}
equals zero. Notice that since the only ergodic identity is the one-point system, this fits to the obvious condition of zero mean of
\boldsymbol{u}
as those arithmetic functions being orthogonal to all uniquely ergodic identities.

6.4.2
Discrete spectrum case

We will need the following observation (
R_{\alpha}(z)=ze^{2\pi i\alpha}
stands for the irrational rotation by
\alpha
on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
):

Lemma 6.15
.

Assume that
{T}\in{\rm Aut}(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu)
.
Assume also that
0\neq F\in L^{2}(X,\mu)
satisfies
F\circ{T}=e^{2\pi i\alpha}F
(a.e.), where
\alpha\in[0,1)
is irrational. Then there exist
g\in L^{2}(\mathbb{S}^{1},{\rm Leb})
,
g\circ R_{\alpha}=e^{2\pi i\alpha}g
, and
\rho\in J({T},R_{\alpha})
such that

\int_{X\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}F(x)g(z)\,d\rho(x,z)\neq 0
.

Proof.

Because
\alpha
is irrational,
F|_{\bigsqcup_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}X_{n}}=0
, therefore we can assume w.l.o.g. that
T
is aperiodic, i.e.
T

is an automorphism of
({\bar{X}}\times Y,\bar{\mu}\otimes\nu)
, where

{T}({\bar{x}},y)=({\bar{x}},T_{\bar{x}}y)
with
{\bar{x}}\mapsto T_{\bar{x}}\in{\rm Aut}(Y,\nu)
being the ergodic decomposition of
{T}
.

Let
A:=\{{\bar{x}}\in{\bar{X}}\colon F({\bar{x}},\cdot)\neq 0\;\nu-\text{a.e.}\}
. Then
\bar{\mu}(A)>0
.
Note that for
{\bar{x}}\in A
,
F({\bar{x}},\cdot)
is an eigenfunction for
T_{\bar{x}}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
e^{2\pi i\alpha}
. By ergodicity, it follows that
|F({\bar{x}},\cdot)|=:\xi({\bar{x}})>0
(for a.a.
{\bar{x}}\in A
; outside of
A
,
F
vanishes).

Let
G:A\times Y\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
,
G({\bar{x}},y)=F({\bar{x}},y)/\xi({\bar{x}})
. Then
G
is still an eigenfunction for the automorphism
{T}|_{A\times Y}
corresponding to the eigenvalue
e^{2\pi i\alpha}
. Moreover, this is readily translated to:
G\circ{T}|_{A\times Y}=R_{\alpha}\circ G
. Moreover,
G_{\ast}(\bar{\mu}|_{A}\otimes\nu)={\rm Leb}
, since the LHS measure must be
R_{\alpha}
-invariant. It follows that
G
establishes a factor map from
{T}|_{A\times Y}
to
R_{\alpha}
, and we can consider the corresponding graph joining
\rho_{1}
. We also join
{T}|_{A^{c}\times Y}
with
R_{\alpha}
by taking
\rho_{2}
being the product measure. The final joining
\rho
is given as
\bar{\mu}(A)\rho_{1}+(1-\bar{\mu}(A))\rho_{2}
, that is, for
\tilde{B}\subset{\bar{X}}\times Y
and
C\subset\mathbb{S}^{1}
,

\rho(\tilde{B}\times C)=\bar{\mu}(A)\rho_{1}((\tilde{B}\cap(A\times Y))\times C)+(1-\bar{\mu}(A))\rho_{2}((\tilde{B}\cap(A^{c}\times Y))\times C).

Set
\chi(z)=z
, which is an eigenfunction of
R_{\alpha}
(corresponding to
e^{2\pi i\alpha}
). We have

\int_{{\bar{X}}\times Y\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}F({\bar{x}},y)\overline{\chi}(z)\;d\rho({\bar{x}},y,z)=

\bar{\mu}(A)\int_{A\times Y\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}F({\bar{x}},y)\overline{\chi}(z)\,d\rho_{1}({\bar{x}},y,z)+(1-\bar{\mu}(A))\int_{A^{c}\times Y\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}F({\bar{x}},y)\overline{\chi}(z)\,d\rho_{2}({\bar{x}},y,z)=

\bar{\mu}(A)\int_{A\times Y\times\mathbb{S}^{1}}F({\bar{x}},y)\overline{\chi\circ G}({\bar{x}},y)\,d(\bar{\mu}|_{A}\otimes\nu)({\bar{x}},y)=

\int_{A\times Y}F({\bar{x}},y)\overline{G({\bar{x}},y)}\,d(\bar{\mu}|_{A}\otimes\nu)({\bar{x}},y)=\int_{A}\xi({\bar{x}})\,d\bar{\mu}({\bar{x}})>0

and the result follows.

Remark 6.16
.

Note that the assertion of Lemma
6.15
fails if
\alpha=0
. Indeed, each zero mean invariant function
F
is orthogonal to all ergodic Markov images (as
F
is measurable with respect to the sigma-algebra of invariant sets and this factor is disjoint from all ergodic automorphisms). In the above proof it was important that the mean of
\chi
is zero which in the final computation made disappear the part of joining
\rho
given by product measure.

Despite the above remark, we will show that the assertion of Lemma
6.15
holds for
\alpha\neq 0
.
For simplicity, assume that
\alpha=1/2
, that is, we consider eigenvalue -1. Assume first that
T
is still aperiodic.
Note that if
F\circ{T}=-F
, then we have the same relation on the ergodic components. Then
F^{2}
is an invariant function, so we see that on each ergodic component
\{{\bar{x}}\}\times Y
the function
F({\bar{x}},\cdot)
has (as before) constant modulus
\xi({\bar{x}})
and takes two values either
c_{\bar{x}}
or
-c_{\bar{x}}
. Moreover, if
F({\bar{x}},y)=c_{\bar{x}}
then
F\circ{T}({\bar{x}},y)=-c_{\bar{x}}
. If we fix
y_{0}\in Y
, then by taking
F({\bar{x}},y_{0})
we make a measurable choice
d_{\bar{x}}
of either
c_{\bar{x}}
or
-c_{\bar{x}}
at each fiber
\{{\bar{x}}\}\times Y
.

We claim now that we can find a positive measure subset
A^{\prime}\subset A
and take a measurable choice
A^{\prime}\ni{\bar{x}}\mapsto d_{\bar{x}}
so that

(49)

\int_{A^{\prime}\times Y}|F({\bar{x}},y)|^{2}\frac{1}{d_{\bar{x}}}\,d(\bar{\mu}\otimes\nu)({\bar{x}},y)=\int_{A^{\prime}}\xi({\bar{x}})^{2}/d_{\bar{x}}\,d\bar{\mu}({\bar{x}})\neq 0.

Indeed, the existence of
A^{\prime}
follows from the fact that the integrand function is different from zero.
We define
G:A^{\prime}\times Y\to\{-1,1\}
by
G({\bar{x}},y)=F({\bar{x}},y)/d_{\bar{x}}
and repeat the previous proof with the circle replaced by the group
\{-1,1\}
,
R_{1/2}(j)=-j
and
\chi(j)=j
(whose mean is zero).

To cope with the general case, first assume that the aperiodic part is non-trivial, find a good joining of it with
R_{1/2}
and then complete to the full joining of
T
and
R_{1/2}
by taking the product joining of
T|_{X_{n}}
(
n\geq 1
) with
R_{1/2}
. Finally, if there is no aperiodic part, there must exists a non-trivial periodic part
T|_{X_{n_{0}}}
for some
n_{0}\geq 2
. Then simply repeat the proof of the aperiodic case with
T
replaced by
T|_{X_{n_{0}}}
.

Given a countable subgroup
G
denote by DISP
G
the family of discrete spectrum automorphisms whose set of eigenvalues is contained in
G
. This family is characteristic.

Proposition 6.17
.

Let
G
be a countable subgroup of the circle.
Then

\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}\cap{\rm DISP}_{G}
if and only if for each Furstenberg system
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
, we have
\sigma_{\pi_{0},\kappa}(\{z\})=0
for each
z\in G\setminus\{1\}
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
(by contraposition) Suppose that for some
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
, say

\kappa=\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\delta_{S^{n}\boldsymbol{u}},

and for some
1\neq z_{0}\in G
, we have
\sigma_{\pi_{0},\kappa}(\{z_{0}\})>0
, that is, the spectral measure of
\pi_{0}
has an atom at
z_{0}
. It follows that

\pi_{0}=\pi_{z_{0}}+\pi_{z_{0}}^{\perp},

where
\pi_{z_{0}}
stands for the orthogonal projection of
\pi_{0}
on the subspace of eigenfunctions corresponding to
z_{0}=e^{2\pi i\alpha}
(we assume that
\alpha
is irrational, in the rational case
z_{0}\neq 1
the proof goes similarly). In view of Lemma
6.15
, there exists a joining
\rho\in J((X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S),R_{\alpha})
such that

(50)

\int\pi_{z_{0}}(\omega)\overline{\chi(z)}\,d\rho(\omega,z)\neq 0.

By Theorem
2.1
, passing to a subsequence of
(N_{k})
if necessary, we obtain
((S^{n}\boldsymbol{u}),(w_{n}))
a generic sequence (along
(N_{k})
) for
\rho
:

\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\delta_{(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u},w_{n})}\to\rho,

where the set
\{n\colon w_{n+1}\neq R_{\alpha}w_{n}\}
is of the form
b_{1}<b_{2}<\ldots
with
b_{k+1}-b_{k}\to\infty
.
Using
(w_{n})
, we pass to the corresponding orbital model to obtain a new uniquely ergodic system
(Y,S)
which is a model of the irrational rotation
R_{\alpha}
. It follows that (
\underline{w}:=(w_{n})
and
\widetilde{\chi}((y_{n}))=\chi(y_{0})
)

\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}(n)\tilde{\chi}(S^{n}\underline{w})=\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\pi_{0}(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u})\chi(w_{n})\to\int\pi_{0}\otimes\overline{\chi}\;d\rho.

Since
\pi_{z_{0}}^{\perp}\perp\chi
(computed in
L^{2}(\rho)
, the spectral measures
\sigma_{\pi_{z_{0}}^{\perp}\otimes 1}
and
\sigma_{1\otimes\chi}
are mutually singular),

\int\pi_{0}\otimes\overline{\chi}\;d\rho=\int(\pi_{z_{0}}+\pi_{z_{0}}^{\perp})\otimes\overline{\chi}\;d\rho

=\int\pi_{z_{0}}\otimes\overline{\chi}\;d\rho\neq 0

and the result follows (
\boldsymbol{u}
correlates with a uniquely ergodic system having the group of eigenvalues contained in
G
).

\Leftarrow
(by contraposition) If

\boldsymbol{u}\not\perp(X,T)
(for some u.e.
(X,T)
with the group of eigenvalues contained in
G
), then for some
(N_{k})
,
f\in C(X)
and
x\in X
, we have
\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}(n)f(T^{n}x)\to c\neq 0
. Passing to a further subsequence of
(N_{k})
if necessary, we obtain then
c=\int\pi_{0}\otimes f\,d\rho=\int\pi_{0}\,{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\rho}[1\otimes f\,|\,X_{\boldsymbol{u}}]\,d\kappa
. Since
(X,T)
is u.e. with discrete spectrum (
\subset G
, we can also assume that
\int f=0
), the spectral measure of
f
has only atoms
\in G\setminus\{1\}
. The spectral measure of
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\rho}[1\otimes f\,|\,X_{\boldsymbol{u}}]
is absolutely continuous w.r.t.
\sigma_{f}
, so it is also purely atomic and has atoms only in
G\setminus\{1\}
. Since
\pi_{0}
is not orthogonal to
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\rho}[1\otimes f\,|\,X_{\boldsymbol{u}}]
, its spectral measure has an atom belonging to
G\setminus\{1\}
.

Remark 6.18
.

In fact, we have proved the following:

Assume that
T\in{\rm Aut}(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu)
and
f\in L^{2}_{0}(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu)
. Then
f\perp L^{2}({\rm Im}(\Phi_{\rho}))
for each
\rho\in J(R,T)
and all
R
ergodic with discrete spectrum if and only if
\sigma_{f}\ll\eta+\delta_{\{1\}}
for some continuous measure
\eta
(on
\mathbb{S}^{1}
).

Since the condition
\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}\cap{\rm DISP}
is equivalent to
\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}\cap{\rm DISP}_{G}
for all countable subgroups
G\subset\mathbb{S}^{1}
, we obtain the following result.

Proposition 6.19
.

Assume that
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
,
M(\boldsymbol{u})=0
. Then

\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}\cap{\rm DISP}
if and only if for each
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
the spectral measure
\sigma_{\pi_{0},\kappa}\ll\eta+\delta_{\{1\}}
for some continuous measure
\eta
.

An important observation is then that by Wiener’s lemma: for any measure
\sigma
on the circle

\sum_{1\neq z}|\sigma(\{z\})|^{2}=\sum_{z}|\sigma(\{z\})|^{2}-|\sigma(\{1\})|^{2}=

\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}|\widehat{\sigma}(h)|^{2}-\Big|\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\widehat{\sigma}(h)\Big|^{2}.

The limits of that kind applied to the function
\pi_{0}
in any Furstenberg system
(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
of
\boldsymbol{u}
are expressible in terms of autocorrelations of
\boldsymbol{u}
. For example:

Corollary 6.20
.

Assume that
\boldsymbol{u}
is generic. Then
\boldsymbol{u}
is orthogonal to all uniquely ergodic models of ergodic transformations with discrete spectrum if and only if

\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\Big|\sum_{n\leq N}\boldsymbol{u}(n+h)\overline{\boldsymbol{u}(n)}\Big|^{2}=

\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\Big|\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}\boldsymbol{u}(n+h)\overline{\boldsymbol{u}(n)}\Big|^{2}=

\Big|\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\lim_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}\boldsymbol{u}(n+h)\overline{\boldsymbol{u}(n)}\Big|^{2}.

6.4.3
When Furstenberg systems are almost ergodic

We consider
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfying (
32
) for each Furstenberg system
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
.
That is, we assume that

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}]=0.

Let
{\cal F}
be any characteristic class. We recall that in view of
[
23
]
(see Theorem B therein)

(51)

\Big[\forall\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})\;\;\pi_{0}\perp L^{2}({\cal A}_{{\cal F}}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S))\Big]\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{u}\perp\mathscr{C}_{{\cal F}},

where by
\mathscr{C}_{{\cal F}}
we denote the class of topological systems whose all visible invariant measures yield measure-preserving systems belonging to
{\cal F}
.
We aim at proving the following result.

Proposition 6.21
.

Assume that
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfies (
32
) and each
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
has purely atomic ergodic decomposition (i.e. it has countably many ergodic components). Assume that
{\cal F}
is a characteristic class. Then

\boldsymbol{u}\perp\mathscr{C}_{{\cal F}}\text{ if and only if }\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}\cap{\cal F}.

Proof.

We only need to show that if
