\boldsymbol{u}\perp{\rm UE}\cap{\cal F}
, then the LHS (Veech condition) of the implication (
51
) is satisfied.
Let
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
, so, by assumption,

\mbox{$\mathcal{I}_{S}$ is purely atomic}.

Let
{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}
stand for the largest
{\cal F}
-factor of
(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa,S)
.
We set

g={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}|{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}]=\mathrm{proj}_{L^{2}({\cal A}_{{\cal F}})}(\pi_{0}).

By our assumption, the ergodic decomposition of
\kappa
is purely atomic, i.e.

X_{\boldsymbol{u}}/\mathcal{I}_{S}={\color[rgb]{0.58,0,0.828}\bar{X}_{\boldsymbol{u}}}=\{c_{1},c_{2},\ldots\}=\{1,2,\ldots\}

with
q:X_{\boldsymbol{u}}\to X_{\boldsymbol{u}}/\mathcal{I}_{S}
the quotient map and
\kappa=\sum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{i}\kappa_{i}
(
\kappa_{i}
are all ergodic and supported on the fiber over
i
;
\alpha_{i}>0
,
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}=1
).
We now define a self-joining
\lambda
of
(S|_{{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}},\kappa|_{{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}})
(in fact, of
(S,\kappa)
; the spaces of ergodic components for
\kappa
and
\kappa|_{{\cal A}_{{\cal F}}}
are the same) by putting

\lambda|_{q^{-1}(i)\times q^{-1}(i)}=\alpha_{i}^{2}\Delta_{\kappa_{i}}\text{ and }\lambda|_{q^{-1}(i)\times q^{-1}(j)}=\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}(\kappa_{i}\otimes\kappa_{j})

for
i\neq j
. Since
\lambda|_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}/\mathcal{I}_{S}\times X_{\boldsymbol{u}}/\mathcal{I}_{S}}(i,j)=\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}
, it is not hard to see that
\lambda|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{S}}
equals
\kappa|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}}\otimes\kappa|_{\mathcal{I}_{S}}
. On the other hand, for
i\neq j
, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}[g\otimes\overline{g}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{S}](i,j)=\int g\otimes\overline{g}d\lambda_{i,j}=\int g\,d\kappa_{i}\cdot\overline{\int g\,d\kappa_{j}}=0

because of our (
32
) assumption. Moreover,

(52)

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}(g\otimes\overline{g}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{S})(i,i)=\int|g|^{2}\,d\kappa_{i}

But by Corollary
6.10
, it follows that

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}(g\otimes\overline{g}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S\times S})=0
, so the more

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\lambda}(g\otimes\overline{g}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}\otimes\mathcal{I}_{S})=0
, and
finally
g=0
by (
52
), whence the Veech condition holds.

Note that if all
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
are ergodic then (
32
) follows immediately whenever the mean of
\boldsymbol{u}
is zero, see (
34
).

Remark 6.22
.

A slightly more general form of Frantzikinakis’ theorem
[
10
]
:

If all logarithmic Furstenberg systems for
\boldsymbol{\mu}
have purely atomic ergodic decomposition then the logarithmic Chowla holds

follows now from Frantzikinakis-Host’s theorem
[
11
]
on the logarithmic Möbius orthogonality of all zero entropy uniquely ergodic systems which, by Proposition
6.21
, implies the validity of logarithmic Sarnak’s conjecture
and then by the Tao’s result about the equivalence of logarithmic versions of Sarnak and Chowla conjectures
[
37
]
.

6.5
Veech and Sarnak’s conditions are not equivalent - a counterexample

Let
{\cal F}
be a characteristic class.
Given an arithmetic function
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
, following
[
23
]
, we say that
\boldsymbol{u}

satisfies the Sarnak condition
(relative to
{\cal F}
) if

\boldsymbol{u}\perp(X,T)\text{ for all }(X,T)\in\mathscr{C}_{{\cal F}}.

In
[
23
]
, it has been proved that the Veech condition (which is the LHS of (
51
)) implies the Sarnak condition for
{\cal F}
, and the two conditions are equivalent whenever
{\cal F}={\cal F}_{\rm ec}
(see Section
2.10
for the definition of an ec-class). One of the problems in
[
23
]
left open was the question whether the Veech and Sarnak conditions are equivalent for an
arbitrary
characteristic class
{\cal F}
. We will now show that it is not the case.
8
8

8

The result has been obtained jointly with A. Kanigowski.

Let
{\cal F}={\rm Rig}((q_{n}))
, that is the class of automorphisms which are
(q_{n})
-rigid:
R\in{\cal F}
if
f\circ R^{q_{n}}\to f
for all
f
in the
L^{2}
-space of
R
. Similarly as in
[
23
]
, we use the result of Fayad and Kanigowski
[
8
]
which gives

(53)

(q_{n})
which is a rigidity time for a weakly mixing automorphism,

but

(54)

(q_{n})
is not a rigidity time for
any
(non-trivial) rotation.

Consider
{T}(x,y)=(x,y+x)
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
. We consider this automorphism with invariant measure
{\mu}:=\sigma\otimes{\rm Leb}_{{\mathbb{T}}}
(cf. Section
3.6
), where
\sigma
is the continuous measure given by the maximal spectral type of the weakly mixing automorphism in (
53
). As proved in
[
23
]
,
{\cal F}_{\rm ec}\subsetneq{\cal F}
, but in fact,
({T},{\mu})\in{\cal F}
while the maximal
{\cal F}_{\rm ec}
-factor of it, in view of (
54
), is equal to
\mathcal{I}_{{T}}
(corresponding to the first coordinate sigma-algebra
({\mathbb{T}},\sigma)
). Let us choose any Borel function
F:{\mathbb{T}}^{2}\to\{-1,1\}
, so that
{{\mathbbm{E}}}[F\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{{T}}]=0
(for example
F
equals -1 on
{\mathbb{T}}\times[0,1/2)
and 1 otherwise). Consider now the stationary process
(F\circ{T}^{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
denoting by
\kappa
its distribution. As a measure-preserving system, it is a factor of
({T},{\mu})
and
F
is orthogonal to
L^{2}({\mathbb{T}},\sigma)
which is the largest
{\cal F}_{\rm ec}
-factor (of
{T}
), so the more
F
is orthogonal to the
L^{2}
-space of its largest
{\cal F}_{\rm ec}
-factor. By passing to the shift model, we obtain a stationary
\pm 1
-valued process
(\pi_{0}\circ S^{n})
with distribution
\kappa
. Since we deal now with a shift invariant measure on the full shift, due to
[
6
]
, there is a generic point
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to\{-1,1\}
for
\kappa
.
Since
\pi_{0}
is orthogonal to the
L^{2}
-space of the largest
{\cal F}_{\rm ec}
-factor, the Veech condition is satisfied for
\boldsymbol{u}
. By Theorem B in
[
23
]
, it follows that the Sarnak condition (for
{\cal F}_{\rm ec}
) is satisfied. That is,
\boldsymbol{u}\perp\mathscr{C}_{{\cal F}_{\rm ec}}
. However, the class we deal with satisfies
{\cal F}_{\rm ec}\subset{\cal F}
, so as observed in
[
23
]
,

\mathscr{C}_{{\cal F}_{\rm ec}}=\mathscr{C}_{{\cal F}}.

It follows that
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfies the Sarnak condition for
{\cal F}
. But
\boldsymbol{u}
cannot satisfy the Veech condition for
{\cal F}
, as
({T},{\mu})\in{\cal F}
, so also the factor determined by the process
(\pi_{0}\circ S^{n})
is in
{\cal F}
, and the largest
{\cal F}
-factor is the whole sigma-algebra. If the Veech condition holds, then
F=0
which is an absurd.

Remark 6.23
.

In our reasoning, it was important that the class we have chosen
satisfies

{\cal F}_{\rm ec}\subsetneq{\cal F},

since on one hand
\mathscr{C}_{{\cal F}}=\mathscr{C}_{{\cal F}_{\rm ec}}
and on the other we can play with members which are in
{\cal F}
but not in
{\cal F}_{\rm ec}
.
Note that the characteristic classes included in Erg
⟂
pointed out in
[
5
]
(there are
R\in{\rm Erg}^{\perp}
such that
all
self-joinings remain in Erg
⟂
)
will satisfy our requirements as each characteristic class
{\cal F}\subset{\rm Erg}^{\perp}
satisfies
{\cal F}_{\rm ec}=
ID, so our reasoning applies.

Note finally that the sequence
\boldsymbol{u}
which we used to obtain the counterexample is generating for a measure which yields the (unique) Furstenberg system and the corresponding measure-preserving system is in Erg
{}^{\perp}\cap{\rm Rig}((q_{n}))
.

6.6
Application: an averaged Chowla property

We will now show that an averaged Chowla conjecture for
\boldsymbol{u}
(defined below) is equivalent to an orthogonality conjecture (
1
) for topological systems whose all invariant measures yield systems from a special characteristic class. We fix a bounded
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
and start with a small extension of Proposition
4.1
.

Proposition 6.24
.

The following conditions are equivalent:

(a)
\boldsymbol{u}
has zero mean on typical short interval (this is equivalent to the fact that the first GHK-norm of
\boldsymbol{u}
vanishes).

(b)
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\kappa}[\pi_{0}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{S}]=0
for each Furstenberg system
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
.

(c) The spectral measure
\sigma_{\pi_{0},\kappa}
has no atom at 1 for each Furstenberg system
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
.

Proof.

The fact that (b) and (c) are equivalent comes from spectral theory.

We now consider
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfying an averaged Chowla property
[
23
]
based on
[
30
]
:

(55)

\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{M_{k}}\Big|\sum_{m\leq M_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}(m+h)\boldsymbol{u}(m)\Big|=0

for each
(M_{k})
defining a Furstenberg system of
\boldsymbol{u}
.

Remark 6.25
.

As stated, (
55
) should be called an averaged 2-Chowla property. However, as shown in
[
30
]
, see also Appendix A in
[
23
]
, the averaged 2-Chowla property implies:

\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H^{k}}\sum_{h_{1},\ldots,h_{k}\leq H}\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{M_{k}}\Big|\sum_{n\leq M_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}(n)\boldsymbol{u}(n+h_{1})\ldots\boldsymbol{u}(n+h_{k})\Big|=0

for each
k\geq 1
,
1\leq h_{1}<\ldots<h_{k}
. From the ergodic theory point of view, it is the classical result that weak mixing property implies weak mixing of all orders.

As shown in
[
9
]
,
[
23
]
, (
55
) is equivalent to the fact that
\sigma_{\pi_{0},\kappa}
is continuous for each Furstenberg system
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
. It follows (see Proposition
6.24
above) that the averaged Chowla property implies the zero mean on typical short interval, that is (see Proposition
4.1
),
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
. We aim at proving the following result.

Theorem 6.26
.

Assume that
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
. The following conditions are equivalent:

(a)
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfies an averaged Chowla property.

(b)
\boldsymbol{u}
is orthogonal with all uniquely ergodic models of discrete spectrum automorphisms.

(c)
\boldsymbol{u}
is orthogonal to all topological systems whose all invariant measures yield discrete spectrum measure-preserving systems.

Moreover, every of the conditions (a), (b) and (c) implies:

(d) For each
\varepsilon>0
there exists
H_{0}\geq 1
such that for each
H\geq H_{0}
and each
Q\geq 1
, we have
9
9

9

For the Liouville function this assertion has been proved to hold by Sacha Mangerel in 2019 (private communication). The property in (d) gives an averaged uniformity of zero mean on “intervals” along arithmetic progressions.

\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{q\leq Q}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}\left|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\boldsymbol{u}(hq+n)\right|^{2}<\varepsilon.

Proof.

(a)
\Rightarrow
(b) Remembering that satisfying the averaged Chowla conjecture yields the continuity of spectral measure of
\pi_{0}
, the claim follows directly from Proposition
6.8
and the result in Remark
6.18
(see also Corollary
6.20
).

(b)
\Rightarrow
(a) Again it follows from the result formulated in Remark
6.18
(remembering that by Proposition
6.24
, the spectral measure of
\pi_{0}
has no atom at 1).

Since (c)
\Rightarrow
(b), it is enough to show that (a)
\Rightarrow
(c) which follows from
[
9
]
(see the proof of Corollary 3.20 therein) since (a) implies that the spectral measure of
\pi_{0}
is continuous (for each
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
).

As the proof of the second assertion, it is interesting for its own, we postpone it to a separate section, see Section
7
.

Remark 6.27
.

Note that the implication (c)
\Rightarrow
(a) is implicit in
[
23
]
. Indeed, the Veech condition for DISP means that the spectral measure
\sigma_{\pi_{0},\kappa}
has no atoms (for each
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
). Then in Section 5.6
[
23
]
the Veech condition is obtained for all
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfying strong
\boldsymbol{u}
-MOMO property for all rotations on the circle. Now, (c) implies (b) and (b) implies that
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfies the strong
\boldsymbol{u}
-MOMO property for all irrational rotations. On the other hand, (c) also implies that
\boldsymbol{u}
is orthogonal to all systems whose all invariant measures yield measure-preserving systems with rational discrete spectrum. But this class of measure-preserving systems forms so called ec characteristic class. So the claim now follows from Proposition 2.17 in
[
23
]
.

If in the assumption of Theorem
6.26
we know additionally that
\sigma_{\pi_{0}}
cannot have irrational atoms, then (d) is equivalent to all other conditions (a)-(c).
As shown by Frantzikinakis and Host, Theorem 1.5
[
12
]
, if
\boldsymbol{u}
is multiplicative (bounded by 1) then for no
\kappa\in V^{\rm log}(\boldsymbol{u})
, the spectral measure of
\pi_{0}
has an irrational atom. Therefore, we obtain the following (cf. Remark
5.5
).

Corollary 6.28
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be a bounded by 1 multiplicative function satisfying (the logarithmic)
\|\boldsymbol{u}\|_{u^{1}}=0
. Then, the following conditions are equivalent:

(a)
\boldsymbol{u}
satisfies the averaged log Chowla property:

\lim_{H\to\infty}\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{\log M_{k}}\Big|\sum_{m\leq M_{k}}\frac{1}{m}\boldsymbol{u}(m+h)\boldsymbol{u}(m)\Big|=0

for each
(M_{k})
defining a logarithmic Furstenberg system of
\boldsymbol{u}
.

(b)
\boldsymbol{u}
is log orthogonal to all uniquely ergodic models of discrete spectrum automorphisms.

(c)
\boldsymbol{u}
is log orthogonal to all topological systems whose all invariant measures yield discrete spectrum measure-preserving systems.

(d) For each
\varepsilon>0
there exists
H_{0}\geq 1
such that for each
H\geq H_{0}
and each
Q\geq 1
, we have

\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{q\leq Q}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{\log N}\sum_{n\leq N}\frac{1}{n}\left|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\boldsymbol{u}(hq+n)\right|^{2}<\varepsilon.

It is reasonable to conjecture that the above is also true for the Cesàro averages. For example, recently in
[
13
]
, it has been proved that for pretentious multiplicative functions we cannot have irrational eigenvalues for the Furstenberg systems.

7
Proof of the second assertion in Theorem
6.26

7.1
Ergodic theory

Proposition 7.1
.

Let
(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu,T)
be a measure-preserving system. Let
f\in L_{0}^{2}(X,\mathcal{B}_{X},\mu)
and assume that its spectral measure
\sigma_{f}
has no atoms at rationals. Then, for each
\varepsilon>0
there exists
H_{0}\geq 1
such that for each
H\geq H_{0}
and each
Q\geq 1
, we have

(56)

\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{q\leq Q}\left\|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}f\circ T^{qh}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}<\varepsilon.

Proof.

By assumption,
\sigma_{f}(\{0\})=0
and we can assume that
\sigma_{f}({\mathbb{T}})=1
(which is equivalent to
\|f\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}=1
).

Suppose that (
56
) does not hold. Therefore,

(57)

\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\left(\exists\varepsilon>0\right)\left(\forall H_{0}\geq 1\right)\left(\exists H\geq H_{0}\right)\left(\exists Q\geq 1\right)\\
\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{q\leq Q}\left\|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}f\circ T^{qh}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\geq\varepsilon.

Equivalently (with the same quantifiers),

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{q\leq Q}\left|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\left(e^{2\pi iqt}\right)^{h}\right|^{2}\,d\sigma_{f}(t)\geq\varepsilon.

For
t\in{\mathbb{T}}=[0,1)
, denote

\phi^{H}_{q}(t):=\left|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\left(e^{2\pi iqt}\right)^{h}\right|^{2},

\Phi_{Q}^{H}(t)=\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{q\leq Q}\phi^{H}_{q}(t).

We have
0\leq\phi^{H}_{q}(t)\leq 1
and
0\leq\Phi^{H}_{Q}(t)\leq 1
.
It follows that

\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\Phi^{H}_{Q}(t)\,d\sigma_{f}(t)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\sigma_{f}\left(\Big\{t\in{\mathbb{T}}:\>\Phi^{H}_{Q}(t)<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Big\}\right)+\sigma_{f}\left(\Big\{t\in{\mathbb{T}}:\>\phi^{H}_{Q}(t)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Big\}\right).

Since
\int_{{\mathbb{T}}}\phi^{H}_{Q}(t)\,d\sigma_{f}(t)\geq\varepsilon
(and
\sigma_{f}({\mathbb{T}})=1
), we have

\sigma_{f}\left(\Big\{t\in{\mathbb{T}}:\>\phi^{H}_{Q}(t)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}\Big\}\right)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

The same argument shows that

\Phi^{H}_{Q}(t)\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\frac{|\{q\leq Q:\>\phi^{H}_{q}(t)<\varepsilon/4\}|}{Q}+\frac{|\{q\leq Q:\>\phi^{H}_{q}(t)\geq\varepsilon/4\}|}{Q},

so if
\Phi^{H}_{Q}(t)\geq\varepsilon/2
, we obtain

\frac{|\{q\leq Q:\>\phi^{H}_{q}(t)\geq\varepsilon/4\}|}{Q}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}.

On the other hand,

\phi^{H}_{q}(t)\leq\frac{4}{H^{2}\left|1-e^{2\pi iqt}\right|^{2}},

so if
\phi^{H}_{q}(t)\geq\varepsilon/4
, we obtain that

\left|1-e^{2\pi iqt}\right|\leq\frac{1}{H}\cdot\frac{4}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}.

We hence obtain that:

(58)

\mbox{for infinitely many $H\geq 1$, we have $\sigma_{f}(A_{H})\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}$},

where
A_{H}:=\left\{t\in{\mathbb{T}}:\>\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{q\leq Q}\mathbbm{1}_{\left|1-e^{2\pi iqt}\right|\leq\frac{1}{H}\cdot\frac{4}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\right\}
.
It follows that

\sigma_{f}\left(\bigcap_{H_{0}}\bigcup_{H\geq H_{0}}A_{H}\right)\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Let us take
t\in\bigcap_{H_{0}}\bigcup_{H\geq H_{0}}A_{H}
. Hence, for infinitely many
H
, there is
Q
(which depends on
H
) such that

(59)

\frac{1}{Q}\left|\left\{q\leq Q:\>\left|1-e^{2\pi iqt}\right|\leq\frac{1}{H}\cdot\frac{4}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right\}\right|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}.

Assume that
H
and
Q
satisfy (
59
). Let

E:=\left\{q\leq Q:\>\left|1-e^{2\pi iqt}\right|\leq\frac{1}{H}\cdot\frac{4}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}\right\}\neq\emptyset.

Then either

•

there is only one element in
E
and then (by (
59
))
1\leq q\leq Q\leq\frac{4}{\varepsilon}
or

•

there are at least two distinct elements in
E
.

In the latter case, set
m:=\min\{q_{2}-q_{1}:\>q_{1}<q_{2}\text{ both in }E\}
. Then,
m\leq Q
and

|E|\leq\frac{Q}{m}+1\leq\frac{2Q}{m}.

But, by (
59
), we know that
\frac{|E|}{Q}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
, so

\frac{2}{m}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{4}
, that is,
m\leq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
. Hence, in
E
, there are two different integers
q_{1},q_{2}
with
0<q_{2}-q_{1}\leq\frac{8}{\varepsilon}
. Since

\left|1-e^{2\pi iq_{j}t}\right|\leq\frac{1}{H}\cdot\frac{4}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}

for
j=1,2
, we obtain

\left|1-e^{2\pi i(q_{2}-q_{1})t}\right|=\left|e^{2\pi iq_{1}t}-e^{2\pi iq_{2}t}\right|\leq\frac{1}{H}\cdot\frac{8}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}.

We have proved that (in both cases!) whenever
t\in\bigcap_{H_{0}\geq 1}\bigcup_{H\geq H_{0}}A_{H}
, then for infinitely many
H
there is
1\leq q\leq 8/\varepsilon
satisfying

\left|1-e^{2\pi iqt}\right|\leq\frac{1}{H}\cdot\frac{8}{\sqrt{\varepsilon}}
. It follows that the set
\bigcap_{H_{0}\geq 1}\bigcup_{H\geq H_{0}}A_{H}
contains only rational numbers with denominators at most
8/\varepsilon
. On the other hand
\sigma_{f}(\bigcap_{H_{0}\geq}\bigcup_{H\geq H_{0}}A_{H})>0
, so the spectral measure of
f
has rational atoms, a contradiction.

Remark 7.2
.

The other direction in Proposition
7.1
is trivial: Let
F_{r}\subset L^{2}_{0}(\mu)
be the space of eigenfunctions corresponding to
1/r
,
f=g+h
,
g\in F_{r}
,
h\in F_{r}^{\perp}
(both
F_{r}
and
F_{r}^{\perp}
are
T
-invariant). By the Pythagorean theorem:

\left\|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}f\circ T^{qh}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}\geq\left\|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}g\circ T^{qh}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}.

Since
g\circ T^{qh}=e^{2\pi iqh/r}g,

\left\|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}g\circ T^{qh}\right\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}=\left|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}e^{2\pi iqh/r}\right|\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}^{2}.

Now, for
q
which is a multiple of
r
we obtain the constant value
\|g\|_{L^{2}(\mu)}
, otherwise, it is zero.

7.2
(a) implies the second assertion

Our aim is to give an “ergodic proof” of the following result:

Proposition 7.3
.

Assume that
\boldsymbol{u}
is a (bounded) arithmetic function such that the spectral measure of
\pi_{0}
has no rational atoms for all
\kappa\in V(\boldsymbol{u})
. Then,
for each
\varepsilon>0
there exists
H_{0}\geq 1
such that for each
H\geq H_{0}
and each
Q\geq 1
, we have

\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{q\leq Q}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}\left|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\boldsymbol{u}(hq+n)\right|^{2}<\varepsilon.

Proof.

Suppose the result does not hold. So there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for each
H_{0}\geq 1
there exist
H\geq H_{0}
and
Q
(which will depend on
H
) such that

\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{q\leq Q}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}\left|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\boldsymbol{u}(hq+n)\right|^{2}\geq\varepsilon_{0}.

By taking
H_{0}=k
, we obtain infinitely many
H_{k}
(with the corresponding choice of
Q_{k}
) such that

\frac{1}{Q_{k}}\sum_{q\leq Q_{k}}\limsup_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\leq N}\left|\frac{1}{H_{k}}\sum_{h\leq H_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}(hq+n)\right|^{2}\geq\varepsilon_{0}.

Then choose
N_{k}
so that

(60)

\frac{1}{Q_{k}}\sum_{q\leq Q_{k}}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\left|\frac{1}{H_{k}}\sum_{h\leq H_{k}}\boldsymbol{u}(hq+n)\right|^{2}\geq\varepsilon_{0}/2.

By passing to a subsequence if necessary, we can assume that
\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\delta_{S^{n}\boldsymbol{u}}\to\kappa
. Due to our assumption on
\boldsymbol{u}
, we can apply Proposition
7.1
(for
f=\pi_{0}\in L^{2}(X_{\boldsymbol{u}},\kappa)
) to obtain: for some
H_{0}\geq 1
, all
H\geq H_{0}
and all
Q\geq 1
, we have

\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{q\leq Q}\int_{X_{\boldsymbol{u}}}\left|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\pi_{0}\circ S^{qh}\right|^{2}\,d\kappa<\varepsilon_{0}/4,

or, equivalently (by the definition of
\kappa
)

\frac{1}{Q}\sum_{q\leq Q}\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{n\leq N_{k}}\left|\frac{1}{H}\sum_{h\leq H}\boldsymbol{u}(qh+n)\right|^{2}<\varepsilon_{0}/4,

which because of quantifiers on
H
and
Q
is in conflict with (
60
).

Remark 7.4
.

For
\boldsymbol{u}
equal to the Liouville function, Proposition
7.3
has been proved by S. Mangerel using purely number theoretic tools in 2019 (private communication).

8
Empirical approximations of self-joinings of Furstenberg systems

Given a bounded arithmetic function
\boldsymbol{u}:{\mathbb{N}}\to{\mathbb{D}}
, we know concretely how to approach the Furstenberg systems of
\boldsymbol{u}
: they are (by definition) all weak
∗
-limits of empirical measures, that is, of the form

(61)

\kappa=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N_{k}}\delta_{S^{n}\boldsymbol{u}},

where
S
is the shift map on
{\mathbb{D}}^{\mathbb{N}}
, and
(N_{k})
is any increasing sequence of natural integers such that the limit exists.

The purpose of this section is to get a similar description of
any 2-fold self-joining of Furstenberg systems
of
\boldsymbol{u}
. Since the subshift context is irrelevant, we consider the more general setting:
X
is a compact metric space,
S
is a homeomorphism of
X
, and
\boldsymbol{u}\in X
. We continue to refer to any weak
∗
-limit of the form (
61
) as a Furstenberg system of
\boldsymbol{u}
. We need the following definition.

Definition 8.1

(Locally orbital sequence of permutations)
.

Let
(N_{k})_{k\geq 1}
be an increasing sequence of natural integers, and for each
k
, let
\phi_{k}
be a permutation of
\{1,\ldots,N_{k}\}
. The sequence
(\phi_{k})_{k\geq 1}
is said to be
locally orbital
if

\frac{1}{N_{k}}\Bigl|\Bigl\{n\in\{1,\ldots,N_{k}-1\}:\phi_{k}(n+1)=\phi_{k}(n)+1\Bigr\}\Bigr|\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}1.

Here is the explanation for the terminology: given such a sequence of permutations, we consider on the Cartesian square
X\times X
the sequence of “empirical measures” of the form

(62)

\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N_{k}}\delta_{(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u},S^{\phi_{k}(n)}\boldsymbol{u})}.

When
(\phi_{k})
is locally orbital, for large
k
these empirical measures are mostly supported on long pieces of orbits for
S\times S
.

Observe that any empirical measure of the form (
62
) has both marginals equal to
\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N_{k}}\delta_{S^{n}\boldsymbol{u}}
.
Therefore, a necessary condition for such a sequence to converge is that
\boldsymbol{u}
be quasi-generic for some
S
-invariant measure
\kappa
along
(N_{k})
. Moreover, if
\lambda
is the weak
∗
-limit of such a sequence, then the locally-orbital condition implies the
S\times S
-invariance of
\lambda
, hence under this assumption
\lambda
is a 2-fold self-joining of a Furstenberg system
\kappa
of
\boldsymbol{u}
. Our goal now is to prove the reciprocal:

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
\boldsymbol{u}
be quasi-generic along
(N_{k})
for some Furstenberg system
\kappa
, and let
\lambda
be a 2-fold self-joining of
\kappa
. Then there exists a locally orbital sequence
(\phi_{k})
, where each
\phi_{k}
is a permutation of
\{1,\ldots,N_{k}\}
, such that

(63)

\lambda=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N_{k}}\delta_{(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u},S^{\phi_{k}(n)}\boldsymbol{u})}.

8.1
Without dynamics

We first describe a strategy to construct appropriate permutations without taking the dynamics into account. We only use for now that
\kappa
is a probability measure on
X
and that
\lambda
is a 2-fold coupling of
\kappa
, that is, a probability measure on
X\times X
with both marginals equal to
\kappa
. We fix a finite partition
\mathscr{P}
of
X
, each atom
P
of which satisfies
\kappa(P)>0
. We consider a finite number of points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\in X
, and for each atom
P\in\mathscr{P}
, we define the number of visits in
P
by

(64)

V(P):=\sum_{1\leq n\leq N}\mathbbmss{1}_{P}(x_{n}).

Let us assume that the empirical measure
(1/N)\sum_{1\leq n\leq N}\delta_{x_{n}}
is a good approximation of
\kappa
on
\mathscr{P}
in the following sense: for some (small) real number
\varepsilon>0
, we have

(65)

\forall P\in\mathscr{P},\quad\left|\frac{V(P)}{N}-\kappa(P)\right|\leq\varepsilon\kappa(P).

Lemma 8.2
.

Under the above hypotheses, for
N
large enough (depending on
\mathscr{P}
,
\varepsilon
,
\kappa
and
\lambda
), we can define a family of nonnegative integers

\bigl(V(P\times P^{\prime})\bigr)_{P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}}

such that

(C1)

\displaystyle\forall P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P},

\displaystyle\quad\left|\frac{V(P\times P^{\prime})}{N}-\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})\right|\leq 2\varepsilon\lambda(P\times P^{\prime}),

(C2)

\displaystyle\forall P\in\mathscr{P},

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}}V(P\times P^{\prime})\leq V(P),

(C3)

\displaystyle\forall P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P},

\displaystyle\quad\sum_{P\in\mathscr{P}}V(P\times P^{\prime})\leq V(P^{\prime}).

Moreover, these numbers are such that the following implication holds: for all
P_{1}
,
P^{\prime}_{1}
,
P_{2}
,
P^{\prime}_{2}
atoms of
\mathscr{P}
,

(C4)

\left.\begin{aligned} \kappa(P_{1})&=\kappa(P_{2}),\\
V(P_{1})&=V(P_{2}),\\
\kappa(P^{\prime}_{1})&=\kappa(P^{\prime}_{2}),\\
V(P^{\prime}_{1})&=V(P^{\prime}_{2}),\\
\lambda(P_{1}\times P^{\prime}_{1})&=\lambda(P_{2}\times P^{\prime}_{2}),\\
\end{aligned}\right\}\Longrightarrow V(P_{1}\times P^{\prime}_{1})=V(P_{2}\times P^{\prime}_{2})

Proof.

For
P,P^{\prime}
atoms of
\mathscr{P}
, we first define

V_{1}(P\times P^{\prime}):=\left\lfloor\frac{V(P)\,\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})}{\kappa(P)}\right\rfloor.

Note that, if
\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})=0
, then Condition (
C1
) automatically holds for
V_{1}(P\times P^{\prime})
.
Now, for atoms
P,P^{\prime}
of
\mathscr{P}
such that
\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})>0
, using (
65
) we get

\displaystyle\left|\frac{V_{1}(P\times P^{\prime})}{N}-\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\left|V_{1}(P\times P^{\prime})-\frac{V(P)\,\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})}{\kappa(P)}\right|+\frac{\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})}{\kappa(P)}\left|\frac{V(P)}{N}-\kappa(P)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}+\varepsilon\lambda(P\times P^{\prime}).

Hence, we also have (
C1
) for
V_{1}(P\times P^{\prime})
provided

(66)

N\geq\max_{P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}:\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})>0}\ \frac{1}{\varepsilon\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})}.

Since
V_{1}(P\times P^{\prime})\leq\frac{V(P)\,\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})}{\kappa(P)}
, and using the fact that the first marginal of
\lambda
is
\kappa
, summing this inequality over
P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
we get Condition (
C2
) for
V_{1}
. But there is no obvious reason why (
C3
) should hold for
V_{1}
, which is why we also introduce, for atoms
P,P^{\prime}
of
\mathscr{P}
,

V_{2}(P\times P^{\prime}):=\left\lfloor\frac{V(P^{\prime})\,\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})}{\kappa(P^{\prime})}\right\rfloor.

By the same arguments, (
C1
) holds for
V_{2}(P\times P^{\prime})
if
N
satisfies (
66
), and this time (
C3
) is valid for
V_{2}
. And finally we set

\forall P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P},\quad V(P\times P^{\prime}):=\min\bigl\{V_{1}(P\times P^{\prime}),V_{2}(P\times P^{\prime})\bigr\}.

Then
V
clearly satisfies all required conditions.

Corollary 8.3
.

With the same assumptions as in Lemma
8.2
, and if
N
is large enough to satisfy (
66
), we can construct a permutation
\phi
of
\{1,\ldots,N\}
such that

(67)

\forall P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P},\ \left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N}\mathbbmss{1}_{P\times P^{\prime}}(x_{n},x_{\phi(n)})-\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})\right|\leq 4\varepsilon.

Proof.

We use the family of numbers
\bigl(V(P\times P^{\prime})\bigr)_{P\in\mathscr{P},P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}}
provided by Lemma
8.2
.
For a fixed atom
P\in\mathscr{P}
, we consider

(68)

A(P):=\Bigl\{n\in\{1,\ldots,N\}:x_{n}\in P\Bigr\}.

Note that
|A(P)|=V(P)
. Using (
C2
), we can find disjoint subsets

A(P\times P^{\prime})\subset A(P),\quad P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P},

with
|A(P\times P^{\prime})|=V(P\times P^{\prime})
for all
P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
. We denote

A:=\bigsqcup_{P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}}A(P\times P^{\prime}).

Likewise, for any fixed
P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
, using (
C3
) we can find disjoint subsets

A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime})\subset A(P^{\prime}),\quad P\in\mathscr{P},

with
|A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime})|=V(P\times P^{\prime})
for all
P\in\mathscr{P}
.

Then we can build a permutation
\phi
of
\{1,\ldots,N\}
as follows:

•

For all
P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
, we define
\phi|_{A(P\times P^{\prime})}
as an arbitrary bijection from
A(P\times P^{\prime})
to
A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime})
.

•

Then, we define
\phi|_{\{1,\ldots,N\}\setminus A}
as an arbitrary bijection from the complement of
A
to the complement of
\bigsqcup_{P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}}A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime})
.

We observe that, with this choice of
\phi
, for all
n\in A
and
P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
, we have

(x_{n},x_{\phi(n)})\in P\times P^{\prime}\Longleftrightarrow n\in A(P\times P^{\prime}).

Therefore, for all
P,P^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{P}
,

(69)

\sum_{n\in A}\mathbbmss{1}_{P\times P^{\prime}}(x_{n},x_{\phi(n)})=|A(P\times P^{\prime})|=V(P\times P^{\prime}).

By (
C1
), for all
P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
, we have the inequality

|A(P\times P^{\prime})|=V(P\times P^{\prime})\geq N\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})(1-2\varepsilon).

Summing over all
P,P^{\prime}
, we get

(70)

|A|=\sum_{P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}}V(P\times P^{\prime})\geq N(1-2\varepsilon).

Using (
69
),
(
C1
) and (
70
), we get

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N}\mathbbmss{1}_{P\times P^{\prime}}(x_{n},x_{\phi(n)})-\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\in A}\mathbbmss{1}_{P\times P^{\prime}}(x_{n},x_{\phi(n)})-\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})\right|+\frac{N-|A|}{N}

\displaystyle=\

\displaystyle\left|\frac{V(P,P^{\prime})}{N}-\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})\right|+1-\frac{|A|}{N}

\displaystyle\leq\

\displaystyle 2\varepsilon\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})+2\varepsilon\leq 4\varepsilon,

which is the announced inequality.

8.2
Taking dynamics into account

We now want to improve Corollary
8.3
, by taking into account the action of
S
on
(X,\kappa)
. We will use the same strategy and the same notations as in the preceding section, but with some additional ingredients that make it possible to obtain the “locally orbital” condition on the permutations.

Here is the new setting. The measure
\lambda
is a self-joining of
(X,\kappa,S)
(compared to the previous hypotheses, we further assume that
\lambda
is
(S\times S)
-invariant). We fix a finite partition
\mathscr{Q}
of
X
, which at the end serves to estimate the gap between the empirical measure and the joining
\lambda
. But first we repeat the arguments of the preceding section with another partition
\mathscr{P}
, obtained as follows:
suppose that for some (large) integer
h
, we have a Rokhlin tower
\bigl(B,SB,\ldots,S^{h-1}B\bigr)
in
(X,\kappa,S)
, which means that the subsets
B,SB,\ldots,S^{h-1}B
are disjoint. We denote
F:=\bigsqcup_{0\leq j\leq h-1}S^{j}B
, and we assume that for some real number
\varepsilon>0
, we have
\kappa(F)>1-\varepsilon
. We consider the partition
\mathscr{P}
whose atoms are
X\setminus F
, and all subsets of the form

S^{j}B\cap\bigcap_{-j\leq r\leq h-1-j}S^{-r}Q_{r},

for
0\leq j\leq h-1
, and
Q_{j},\ldots,Q_{h-1-j}
arbitrary atoms of
\mathscr{Q}
(provided such a subset has positive
\kappa
-measure). In other words, the information provided by
\mathscr{P}
is exactly: in which level of the Rokhlin tower the point is (or if it is in
X\setminus F
) and, when the point is in
F
, what is the
\mathscr{Q}
-name read on the piece of the orbit corresponding to the ascent in the Rokhlin tower. Note that for each
\mathscr{P}
-atom contained in
S^{j}B
for some
0\leq j\leq h-2
,
SP
is a
\mathscr{P}
-atom contained in
S^{j+1}B
.

For some
N
large enough to satisfy (
66
), we have
N
points
x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\in X
which are successive images by
S
:
x_{n}=S^{n-1}x_{1}
for all
2\leq n\leq N
. For each atom
P\in\mathscr{P}
we define
V(P)
and
A(P)
as before (see (
64
) and (
68
)), and we assume that for the same number
\varepsilon>0
, (
65
) is satisfied. For technical reasons, we also assume that

(71)

x_{1}\notin\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{h-1}S^{j}B,\text{ and }x_{N}\notin\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{h-2}S^{j}B,

so that for each
\mathscr{P}
-atom
P\subset\bigsqcup_{j=0}^{h-2}S^{j}B
,

(72)

V(SP)=V(P),

and

(73)

A(SP)=A(P)+1.

Lemma 8.4
.

Under the above hypotheses, we can construct a permutation
\phi
of
\{1,\ldots,N\}
satisfying

(74)

\forall Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathscr{Q},\quad\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N}\mathbbmss{1}_{Q\times Q^{\prime}}(x_{n},x_{\phi(n)})-\lambda(Q\times Q^{\prime})\right|\leq 8\varepsilon,

and

(75)

\frac{1}{N}\Bigl|\Bigl\{n\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}:\phi(n+1)\neq\phi(n)+1\Bigr\}\Bigr|\leq 4\varepsilon+\frac{2}{h}+\frac{2}{N}.

Proof.

As all the assumptions of Lemma
8.2
hold, we have at our disposal the numbers
V(P\times P^{\prime})
provided by this lemma. We will follow the same strategy as in the proof of Corollary
8.3
to get the permutation
\phi
, that is, we will construct the subsets
A(P\times P^{\prime})
and
A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime})
satisfying all the above-mentioned properties, and define
\phi
by its restriction to each
A(P\times P^{\prime})
as a bijection to
A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime})
.
In the non-dynamical context of Corollary
8.3
, we have a significant flexibility to choose the subsets and the bijection. Here, we will also use the specific structure of the partition
\mathscr{P}
to impose additional conditions leading to the “locally orbital” condition. To do so, we observe that the partition of
X
defined by the Rokhlin tower
\bigl(B,SB,\ldots,S^{h-1}B\bigr)
induces a partition of
X\times X
into a family of disjoint Rokhlin towers for
S\times S
. These Rokhlin towers are of the form

\Bigl(B\times S^{j}B,SB\times S^{j+1}B,\ldots,S^{h-1-j}B\times S^{h-1}B\Bigr)\quad(0\leq j\leq h-1),

and

\Bigl(S^{j}B\times B,S^{j+1}B\times SB,\ldots,S^{h-1}B\times S^{h-1-j}B\Bigr)\quad(1\leq j\leq h-1).

(See Figure
1
.)

Figure 1:
The partition of
X\times X
into Rokhlin towers. We represented the atoms of
\mathscr{P}\times\mathscr{P}
inside only one of these Rokhlin towers.

We use this structure to define the subsets
A(P\times P^{\prime})
and
A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime})
, and the restriction of the desired permutation
\phi
to
A(P\times P^{\prime})
, in a specific order, so that the following condition holds: for all atoms
P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}
, if for some
j,j^{\prime}\in\{0,\ldots,h-2\}
we have
P\subset S^{j}B
and
P^{\prime}\subset S^{j^{\prime}}B
(that is:
P\times P^{\prime}
is contained in a level of one of the
(S\times S)
-Rokhlin towers which is not the top one), then

(76)

\displaystyle\begin{split}A(SP\times SP^{\prime})&=A(P\times P^{\prime})+1,\\
A^{\prime}(SP\times SP^{\prime})&=A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime})+1,\\
\text{and }\forall n\in A(P\times P^{\prime}),\ \phi(n+1)&=\phi(n)+1.\end{split}

Note that in the above requirements, the first two equalities are likely to be achieved since, by
S
-invariance of
\kappa
,
(S\times S)
-invariance of
\lambda
and (
72
),
Condition (
C4
) ensures that
V(SP\times SP^{\prime})=V(P\times P^{\prime})
.

Here is how we proceed for the construction.

•

First, we define the subsets
A(P\times P^{\prime})
and
A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime})
for all atoms
P\times P^{\prime}
contained in the basis
B\times B
of the highest Rokhlin tower, and we define the restriction of
\phi
to any of these
A(P\times P^{\prime})
as an arbitrary bijection between
A(P\times P^{\prime})
and
A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime})
.

•

Next, for the same atoms
P\times P^{\prime}
, we use Conditions (
76
) to define inductively the subsets
A(S^{j}P\times S^{j}P^{\prime})
and
A^{\prime}(S^{j}P\times S^{j}P^{\prime})
,
(1\leq j\leq h-1)
, and the restrictions of
\phi
to all of these
A(S^{j}P\times S^{j}P^{\prime})
. At this point we have processed all atoms of
\mathscr{P}\times\mathscr{P}
contained in the Rokhlin tower whose basis is
B\times B
.

•

We proceed in the same way for the second Rokhlin tower (the one whose basis is
B\times SB
): for all
\mathscr{P}
-atoms
P\subset B
,
P^{\prime}\subset SB
, we start by choosing the subsets
A(P\times P^{\prime})
and
A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime})
, ensuring that
A(P\times P^{\prime})
is disjoint from all
A(P\times P^{\prime}_{1})
,
P^{\prime}_{1}\subset B
, previously chosen (this is always possible by (
C2
)), and that
A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime})
is disjoint from all
A^{\prime}(P_{1}\times P^{\prime})
,
P_{1}\subset SB
, previously chosen (again, this is always possible by (
C3
)). Then we use Conditions (
76
) to define inductively the subsets
A(S^{j}P\times S^{j}P^{\prime})
and
A^{\prime}(S^{j}P\times S^{j}P^{\prime})
,
(1\leq j\leq h-2)
, and the restrictions of
\phi
to all of these
A(S^{j}P\times S^{j}P^{\prime})
. Note that the disjunction at the base level implies disjunction at higher levels.

•

We treat in this way all the Rokhlin towers one by one. For each tower successively, we first choose the subsets
A(P\times P^{\prime})
and
A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime})
in the basis of the tower, taking care of ensuring all necessary disjunctions which is possible by (
C2
) and (
C3
), then we choose an arbitrary bijection between these two subsets. Once the basis is processed, we extend to all higher levels in the tower by (
76
).

With this procedure, we choose all subsets
A(P\times P^{\prime})
and
A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime})
and all bijections

\phi|_{A(P\times P^{\prime})}:A(P\times P^{\prime})\to A^{\prime}(P\times P^{\prime}),

for all
\mathscr{P}
-atoms
P,P^{\prime}\subset F
, ensuring that (
76
) holds. Let us denote

A:=\bigsqcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}\\
P\subset F,P^{\prime}\subset F\end{subarray}}A(P\times P^{\prime}).

Observe that, by (
C1
), and using the assumption
\kappa(F)\geq 1-\varepsilon
, we have

(77)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{|A|}{N}&=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}\\
P\subset F,P^{\prime}\subset F\end{subarray}}V(P\times P^{\prime})\\
&\geq(1-2\varepsilon)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P,P^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}\\
P\subset F,P^{\prime}\subset F\end{subarray}}\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})\\
&=(1-2\varepsilon)\lambda(F\times F)\\
&\geq(1-2\varepsilon)^{2}\geq 1-4\varepsilon.\end{split}

To complete the picture, we define
\phi
arbitrarily on
\{1,\ldots,N\}\setminus A
to get a permutation of
\{1,\ldots,N\}
.

With this choice of
\phi
, let us check the validity of (
74
). For this, we have to estimate the sum

\sum_{1\leq n\leq N}\mathbbmss{1}_{Q\times Q^{\prime}}(x_{n},x_{\phi(n)}),

in which we distinguish two types of
n
’s: those who are in
A
and those who are not. By (
77
), the contribution to the sum of
\{1,\ldots,N\}\setminus A
is bounded by
4\varepsilon N
. Now, for
n
in
A
, there exist (unique)
\mathscr{P}
-atoms
P,P^{\prime}\subset F
such that
n\in A(P\times P^{\prime})
, and then by the construction of
\phi
we know that
(x_{n},x_{\phi(n)})\in P\times P^{\prime}
. Therefore, the contribution of this
n
is 1 if and only if
P\subset Q
and
P^{\prime}\subset Q^{\prime}
. It follows that the total contribution of
n
’s in
A
to the sum amounts to

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\subset Q\cap F\\
P^{\prime}\subset Q^{\prime}\cap F\end{subarray}}V(P\times P^{\prime}).

Thus, using (
C1
), we get

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N}\mathbbmss{1}_{Q\times Q^{\prime}}(x_{n},x_{\phi(n)})-\lambda(Q\times Q^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n\notin A}\mathbbmss{1}_{Q\times Q^{\prime}}(x_{n},x_{\phi(n)})+\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\subset Q\cap F\\
P^{\prime}\subset Q^{\prime}\cap F\end{subarray}}V(P\times P^{\prime})-\lambda(Q\times Q^{\prime})\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\varepsilon+\left|\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\subset Q\cap F\\
P^{\prime}\subset Q^{\prime}\cap F\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{V(P\times P^{\prime})}{N}-\lambda(P\times P^{\prime})\right)\right|+\lambda\bigl((Q\times Q^{\prime})\setminus(F\times F)\bigr).

By (
C1
), the second term in the above sum is bounded by
2\varepsilon
, and the same for the last term since
\kappa(F)\geq 1-\varepsilon
. Thus, (
74
) is established and it remains now to prove (
75
).
By (
76
), the set

\Bigl\{n\in\{1,\ldots,N-1\}:\phi(n+1)\neq\phi(n)+1\Bigr\}

is contained in the union of the following three subsets of
\{1,\ldots,N\}
:

•

\{1,\ldots,N\}\setminus A
, whose cardinality is bounded by
4\varepsilon N
by (
77
);

•

\{n:x_{n}\in S^{h-1}B\}
, but since the
x_{n}
’s are successive points on some
S
-orbit, the gap between 2 integers in this set is always at most
h
, therefore the cardinality of this set is at most
\frac{N}{h}+1
;

•

\{n:x_{\phi(n)}\in S^{h-1}B\}
, which has the same cardinality as the preceding one because
\phi
is a bijection.

The union of these 3 subsets has cardinality bounded by
4\varepsilon N+\frac{2N}{h}+2
, which proves (
75
).

8.3
Proof of Proposition
8.1

We assume now that (
61
) holds:
\boldsymbol{u}\in X
is quasi-generic along some increasing sequence
(N_{k})
for some Furstenberg system
\kappa
,
\lambda
is a 2-fold self-joining of
\kappa
, and we explain the strategy to construct the locally orbital sequence
(\phi_{k})
announced in the statement of the proposition.

First, we point out that, without loss of generality, we may always assume that the measure-preserving system
(X,\kappa,S)
is aperiodic. Indeed, if it is not the case, we fix some irrational number
\alpha
such that for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}\setminus\{0\}
,
e^{2\pi in\alpha}
is not an eigenvalue of the Koopman operator
f\mapsto f\circ S
on
L^{2}(X,\kappa)
. We consider the auxilliary measure-preserving system
(Y,\nu,T_{\alpha})
where
Y:={\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
,
\nu
is the normalized Haar measure and
T_{\alpha}:y\mapsto y+\alpha\mod 1
. As
(Y,T_{\alpha})
is uniquely ergodic, any point
y\in Y
is generic for
\nu
(we take for example
y=0
). Since
(X,\kappa,S)
is disjoint from
(Y,\nu,T_{\alpha})
, in the system
(X\times Y,S\times T_{\alpha})
, the point
(\boldsymbol{u},0)
is quasi-generic along the same sequence
(N_{k})
for the product measure
\kappa\otimes\nu
. So, we can consider
(\boldsymbol{u},0)\in X\times Y
instead of
\boldsymbol{u}\in X
, and then the measure-preserving system
(X\times Y,\kappa\otimes\nu,S\times T_{\alpha})
is aperiodic. In this new setting we can extend the self-joining
\lambda
to
\nu\otimes\lambda\otimes\nu
on
(X\times Y)\times(X\times Y)
.

We fix a so-called “good sequence of partitions”
(\mathscr{Q}_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 1}
for
(X,\kappa)
, which is a sequence of finite partitions satisfying:

•

For all
\ell\geq 1
,
\mathscr{Q}_{\ell+1}
refines
\mathscr{Q}_{\ell}
,

•

\max_{Q\in\mathscr{Q}_{\ell}}\mathop{\mathrm{diam}}(Q)\xrightarrow[\ell\to\infty]{}0
,

•

\forall\ell\geq 1,\forall Q\in\mathscr{Q}_{\ell}
,
\kappa(\partial Q)=0
.

It is proved in
[
23
]
that such a sequence always exists and that it satisfies the additional property:
(\mathscr{Q}_{\ell}\times\mathscr{Q}_{\ell})
is also a good sequence of partitions for
(X\times X,\lambda)
. In particular, weak
∗
-convergence of a sequence
(\lambda_{n})
of probability measures on
X\times X
to
\lambda
is equivalent to

(78)

\forall Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathscr{Q},\quad\lambda_{n}(Q\times Q^{\prime})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\lambda(Q\times Q^{\prime}).

We also fix a sequence
(\varepsilon_{\ell})
of positive numbers decreasing to
0
, and a sequence
(h_{\ell})
of integers such that
\frac{1}{h_{\ell}}<\varepsilon_{\ell}
for all
\ell
. It is also explained in
[
23
]
that, for each
\ell
, we can find a Rokhlin tower
(B_{\ell},SB_{\ell},\ldots,S^{h_{\ell}-1}B_{\ell})
such that

•

\kappa\left(\bigsqcup_{0\leq j\leq h_{\ell}-1}S^{j}B\right)>1-\varepsilon_{\ell}
,

•

for all
j
,
\kappa\bigl(\partial(S^{j}B)\bigr)=0
.

Then, for each
\ell
, we construct the partition
\mathscr{P}_{\ell}
from
\mathscr{Q}_{\ell}
and the above Rokhlin tower, in the same way as
\mathscr{P}
is constructed from
\mathscr{Q}
and the Rokhlin tower
(B,\ldots,S^{h-1}B)
in the beginning of Section
8.2
. Since all atoms
P
of these partitions
\mathscr{P}_{\ell}
satisfy
\kappa(\partial P)=0
, (
61
) ensures that

\forall\ell,\forall P\in\mathscr{P}_{\ell},\quad\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N_{k}}\mathbbmss{1}_{P}(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u})\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}\kappa(P).

This enables us to define

k_{1}:=\min\Biggl\{K\geq 1:\forall k\geq K,\\
\eqref{eq:N_large}\text{ holds for }N\geq N_{k}-2h_{1},\ \varepsilon=\varepsilon_{1}\text{ and }\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}_{1},\\
\text{ and }\forall P\in\mathscr{P}_{1},\ \frac{4h_{1}}{N_{k}}+\left|\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N_{k}}\mathbbmss{1}_{P}(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u})-\kappa(P)\right|\leq\varepsilon_{1}\Biggr\},

and inductively, for all
\ell\geq 2
,

k_{\ell}:=\min\Biggl\{K\geq k_{\ell-1}+1:\forall k\geq K,\\
\eqref{eq:N_large}\text{ holds for }N\geq N_{k}-2h_{\ell},\ \varepsilon=\varepsilon_{\ell}\text{ and }\mathscr{P}=\mathscr{P}_{\ell},\\
\text{ and }\forall P\in\mathscr{P}_{\ell},\ \frac{4h_{\ell}}{N_{k}}+\left|\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N_{k}}\mathbbmss{1}_{P}(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u})-\kappa(P)\right|\leq\varepsilon_{\ell}\Biggr\}.

Consider an integer
k
with
k_{\ell}\leq k<k_{\ell+1}
for some
\ell\geq 1
. We want to use Lemma
8.4
to construct a permutation
\phi_{k}
of
\{1,\ldots,N_{k}\}
, and for this we have to precise with which points
x_{n}
we apply the lemma. With the goal of fulfilling Property (
71
), we set

x_{1}:=S^{i_{1}}\boldsymbol{u},\text{ where }i_{1}:=\min\left\{i\geq 1:S^{i}\boldsymbol{u}\notin\bigsqcup_{1\leq j\leq h_{\ell}-1}S^{j}B_{\ell}\right\}.

We have
i_{1}\leq h_{\ell}
. Then, we also consider

i_{2}:=\max\left\{i\leq N_{k}:S^{i}\boldsymbol{u}\notin\bigsqcup_{0\leq j\leq h_{\ell}-2}S^{j}B_{\ell}\right\}.

We also have
N_{k}-i_{2}\leq h_{\ell}-1
. Thus, setting
N:=i_{2}-i_{1}+1
, we have
N_{k}-2h_{\ell}\leq N\leq N_{k}
. Now, the points
x_{n}=S^{n+i_{1}-1}\boldsymbol{u}
,
1\leq n\leq N
, satisfy for each atom
P\in\mathscr{P}_{\ell}
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N}\mathbbmss{1}_{P}(x_{n})-\kappa(P)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N}\mathbbmss{1}_{P}(x_{n})-\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N_{k}}\mathbbmss{1}_{P}(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u})\right|+\left|\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N_{k}}\mathbbmss{1}_{P}(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u})-\kappa(P)\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{4h_{\ell}}{N_{k}}+\left|\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N_{k}}\mathbbmss{1}_{P}(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u})-\kappa(P)\right|\leq\varepsilon_{\ell}.

All assumptions of Lemma
8.4
are satisfied, so we get a permutation
\phi
of
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{2}\}
such that

\forall Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathscr{Q}_{\ell},\quad\left|\frac{1}{i_{2}-i_{1}+1}\sum_{i_{1}\leq n\leq i_{2}}\mathbbmss{1}_{Q\times Q^{\prime}}(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u},S^{\phi(n)}\boldsymbol{u})-\lambda(Q\times Q^{\prime})\right|\leq 8\varepsilon_{\ell},

and

(79)

\frac{1}{N}\Bigl|\Bigl\{n\in\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{2}\}:\phi(n+1)\neq\phi(n)+1\Bigr\}\Bigr|\leq 4\varepsilon_{\ell}+\frac{2}{h_{\ell}}+\frac{2}{N_{k}}.

Then, we can extend
\phi
to a permutation
\phi_{k}
of
\{1,\ldots,N_{k}\}
, for example by setting
\phi_{k}|_{\{1,\ldots,i_{1}-1\}\cup\{i_{2}+1,\ldots,N_{k}\}}:={\rm Id}
.

It is clear that the sequence
(\phi_{k})
constructed in this way satisfies for all
\ell\geq 1
,

\forall Q,Q^{\prime}\in\mathscr{Q}_{\ell},\quad\left|\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{1\leq n\leq N_{k}}\mathbbmss{1}_{Q\times Q^{\prime}}(S^{n}\boldsymbol{u},S^{\phi(n)}\boldsymbol{u})-\lambda(Q\times Q^{\prime})\right|\xrightarrow[k\to\infty]{}0.

Thanks to (
78
), this gives (
63
).
Moreover, (
79
) ensures that the sequence of permutations
(\phi_{k})
is locally orbital.
This concludes the proof of Proposition
8.1
.

8.4
Question

What could be an analog of Proposition
8.1
for the
logarithmic
Furstenberg systems? An important difficulty seems to be that, in this case, all points in the orbit of
\boldsymbol{u}
do not have the same weights, hence the permutation of indices changes the second marginal.

9
Cross-sections of ergodic components

The purpose of this section is to provide a proof of Proposition
2.12
. The arguments presented below were communicated to us by Tim Austin.

9.1
Measure theory

Consider a diagram of measurable spaces

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{Z.}

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
X
, and
\mu^{\prime}:=\phi_{\ast}\mu
be its image on
Y
. Let
{\cal A}
be the sigma-algebra of
Y
, and assume that
Z
is standard. Let
m
be Lebesgue measure restricted to
{\cal B}_{[0,1]}
.

Lemma 9.1
.

There is a measurable map
\pi
from
Y\times[0,1]
to
Z
that represents the joint distribution of
\phi
and
\psi
in the following sense:

\int_{X}(f\circ\phi)(g\circ\psi)\,d\mu=\int_{Y}f(y)\int_{0}^{1}g(\pi(y,t))\ dt\ d\mu^{\prime}(y)

for any bounded measurable functions
f
on
Y
and
g
on
Z
.

Moreover,
\pi
can be taken measurable with respect to
{\cal A}_{0}\times{\cal B}_{[0,1]}
, where
{\cal A}_{0}
is some countably generated sigma-subalgebra of
{\cal A}
.

This follows directly from two classical results in the measure theory of standard Borel spaces; we refer the reader to Kallenberg’s book
[
20
]
.

Proof.

First, since
Z
is standard, we may represent the conditional distribution of
\psi
given
\phi
using a probability kernel, say

Y\mapsto M(Z):y\mapsto\nu_{y}.

This is the disintegration theorem, as in
[
20
, Theorem 8.5]
.

Since
M(Z)
is standard, its sigma-algebra is countably generated. The pullback of this sigma-algebra to
Y
is a countably generated sigma-subalgebra of
{\cal A}
. Let this be
{\cal A}_{0}
.

Again since
Z
is standard, the kernel
\nu_{\bullet}
has a representation using an independent random variable distributed uniformly in
[0,1]
: see
[
20
, Lemma 4.22]
. This result gives a map
\pi:Y\times[0,1]\to Z
that is measurable with respect to
{\cal A}_{0}\times{\cal B}_{[0,1]}
and satisfies

\nu_{y}(C)=m\{t:\ \pi(y,t)\in C\}\quad\forall y\in Y,\ C\in{\cal B}_{Z}.

Putting these two representations together, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{X}(f\circ\phi)(g\circ\psi)\,d\mu

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}f(y)\int g\ d\nu_{y}\ d\mu^{\prime}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}f(y)\int_{0}^{1}g(\pi(y,t))\ dt\ d\mu^{\prime}(y),

as required.

9.2
Ergodic theory

Now let
T
be an automorphism of a standard probability space
(X,{\cal B}_{X},\mu)
. We recall that
\mathcal{I}_{T}
stands for the sub-sigma-algebra of
T
-invariant sets . Let

\mu=\int\mu_{x}\ d\mu(x)

be the ergodic decomposition of
(X,\mu,T)
: that is, the disintegration of
\mu
over
\mathcal{I}_{T}
.

Let
Z:=M^{e}(X,T)
. This is a Borel subset of
M(X)
, so the induced measurable structure on
Z
is still standard. The map
x\mapsto\mu_{x}
is
T
-invariant and measurable from
X
to
Z
.

Now let
(Y,{\cal A})
be another measurable space, not necessarily standard. Let
\phi:X\to Y
be a measurable map with the property that
\phi^{-1}{\cal A}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{T}
. (In case
{\cal A}
separates the points of
Y
, this implies that
\phi
is
T
-invariant, but we do not need this additional assumption.) In applications it could be that
Y
is the same set as
X
,
{\cal A}
is a proper sub-sigma-algebra of
\mathcal{I}_{T}
, and
\phi(x)=x
. However, the explanation below seems clearer if we give
Y
its own name. Let
\mu^{\prime}:=\phi_{\ast}\mu
be the image measure of
\mu
under
\phi
.

We are now ready to prove Proposition
2.12
.

Proof of Proposition
2.12
.
Begin by applying Lemma
9.1
to the maps
\phi:X\to Y
and
\mu_{\bullet}:X\to Z
. The result is a map from
Y\times[0,1]
to
Z
satisfying the equation promised in that lemma, and measurable with respect to
{\cal A}_{0}\times{\cal B}_{[0,1]}
for some countably generated sigma-subalgebra
{\cal A}_{0}
of
{\cal A}
. Since
Z
is itself a space of measures, this new map is actually a probability kernel, say
\theta_{(y,t)}
.

Let us consider the conclusion of Lemma
9.1
in case
f
is a bounded measurable function on
Y
and
g
is a function on
Z
of the form

g(\nu):=\int G\,d\nu

for some bounded Borel function
G
on
X
. Then that conclusion becomes

(80)

\int f(\phi(x))\Big(\int G\,d\mu_{x}\Big)\ d\mu(x)=\int_{Y}f(y)\int_{0}^{1}\Big(\int G\,d\theta_{(y,t)}\Big)\ dt\ d\mu^{\prime}(y).

We make use of (
80
) through two special cases:

1.

Taking
f=1
but allowing arbitrary
G
, equation (
80
) shows that

(81)

\mu=\int\mu_{x}\ d\mu(x)=\int_{Y}\int_{0}^{1}\theta_{(y,t)}\ dt\ d\mu^{\prime}(y).

2.

Take
f=\mathbbmss{1}_{A}
for some
A\in{\cal A}
, and take
G=\mathbbmss{1}_{B}
where
B
is either
\phi^{-1}A
or
X\setminus\phi^{-1}A
. Equation (
80
) becomes

(82)

\int_{\phi^{-1}A}\mu_{x}(B)\,d\mu(x)=\int_{A}\int_{0}^{1}\theta_{(y,t)}(B)\ dt\ d\mu^{\prime}(y).

Since
B
is
T
-invariant, the properties of the ergodic decomposition give

\mu_{x}(B)=\mathbbmss{1}_{B}(x)\quad\hbox{for}\ \mu\hbox{-a.a.}\ x,

so the left-hand side of (
82
) is either
\mu(\phi^{-1}A)=\mu^{\prime}(A)
(in case
B=\phi^{-1}A
) or
0
(in case
B=X\setminus\phi^{-1}A
). Allowing
A
to vary and looking at the right-hand side of (
82
), these outcomes are possible only if we have

(83)

\theta_{(y,t)}(\phi^{-1}A)=\mathbbmss{1}_{A}(y)\quad\hbox{for}\ (\mu^{\prime}\times m)\hbox{-a.a.}\ (y,t).

Equation (
83
) plays an important role below, but we need something slightly stronger. Since
{\cal A}_{0}
is countably generated, it contains a countable generating subalgebra of sets
{\cal{S}}
. Since
{\cal{S}}
is countable, there is a Borel set
F\subseteq[0,1]
with
m(F)=1
and such that (
83
) holds for
\mu^{\prime}
-a.a.
y
whenever
t\in F
and
A\in{\cal{S}}
: that is, the set of ‘good’ values of
t
does not depend on the choice of
A
from
{\cal{S}}
. This conclusion now extends from
{\cal{S}}
to
{\cal A}_{0}
. Indeed, let
t\in F
and let
Y_{t}\in{\cal A}_{0}
be the set of all
y\in Y
such that

\theta_{(y,t)}(\phi^{-1}A)=\mathbbmss{1}_{A}(y)\quad\forall A\in{\cal{S}}.

If
A\in{\cal A}_{0}
and
y\in Y_{t}\cap A
, then

(84)

A\supseteq\bigcap_{A^{\prime}\in{\cal{S}}:\ A^{\prime}\ni y}A^{\prime}

Indeed, let us say that two points
u,v
in
Y
are “the same according to
{\cal S}
” if every set in
{\cal S}
either contains both
u
and
v
, or neither of them. It is easy to see that if
u,v
are the same according to
{\cal S}
, then they are the same according to the sigma-algebra
{\cal A}_{0}
generated by
{\cal S}
(indeed: the collection of all sets according to which
u
and
v
are the same is a sigma-algebra containing
{\cal S}
).
Now, the set on the right is the set of all points in
Y
that are the same as
y
according to
{\cal S}
. By the observation above, this is the same as “all points in
Y
that are the same as
y
according to
{\cal A}_{0}
”. Since
A
is one particular member of
{\cal A}_{0}
, and
y
lies in
A
, all of these other points must also lie in
A
.

Since in (
84
) we deal with a countable intersection, we have

\theta_{(y,t)}(\phi^{-1}A)\geq\theta_{(y,t)}\Big(\bigcap_{A^{\prime}\in{\cal{S}}:\ A^{\prime}\ni y}A^{\prime}\Big)=1.

A symmetrical argument shows that
\theta_{(y,t)}(\phi^{-1}A)=0
if
y\in Y_{t}\setminus A
. Thus, for
t\in F
, we now know that

(85)

\theta_{(y,t)}(\phi^{-1}A)=\mathbbmss{1}_{A}(y)\quad\forall A\in{\cal A}_{0},\ \hbox{for}\ \mu^{\prime}\hbox{-a.a.}\ y.

For each
t\in F
, let

\nu_{t}:=\int_{Y}\theta_{(y,t)}\ d\mu^{\prime}(y).

For
t\in[0,1]\setminus F
, take
\nu_{t}
to equal
\nu_{s}
for some
s\in F
; the choice does not matter because the set of these
t
is negligible.

Now, we can show properties (a–c). The first two are simple: (a) holds because
\nu_{t}
is a mixture of
T
-invariant measures; and (b) follows from (
81
) and Fubini’s theorem.

We have
\mathcal{I}_{T}\supseteq\phi^{-1}\cal{A}
by assumption, so property (c) requires only the reverse inclusion modulo
\nu_{t}
. Suppose that
B\in\mathcal{I}_{T}
, and let

A:=\{y\in Y:\ \theta_{(y,t)}(B)=1\}.

Then
A\in{\cal A}_{0}
, by the measurability of
\theta
and because we are holding
t
fixed. Moreover, the value
\theta_{(y,t)}(B)
equals
0
or
1
for every
(y,t)
, since
\theta_{(y,t)}
is ergodic, and therefore

(86)

Y\setminus A=\{y\in Y:\ \theta_{(y,t)}(B)=0\}.

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\nu_{t}(B\setminus\phi^{-1}A)

\displaystyle=\int_{Y}\theta_{(y,t)}(B\setminus\phi^{-1}A)\ d\mu^{\prime}(y)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\theta_{(y,t)}(B\setminus\phi^{-1}A)\ d\mu^{\prime}(y)+\int_{Y\setminus A}\theta_{(y,t)}(B\setminus A)\ d\mu^{\prime}(y).

The first of these integrals is zero because (
85
) gives

\theta_{(y,t)}(\phi^{-1}A)=1\quad\hbox{for}\ \mu^{\prime}\hbox{-a.a.}\ y\in A,

and the second integral is zero because

\theta_{(y,t)}(B)=0\quad\hbox{for}\ \mu^{\prime}\hbox{-a.a.}\ y\in Y\setminus A.

Similarly,
\nu_{t}(\phi^{-1}A\setminus B)=0
, and so
B=\phi^{-1}A
modulo
\nu_{t}
.

10
Pinsker factor in ergodic components

Let
(X,\mu,T)
be a measure-preserving system, where
(X,\mu)
is a standard Borel probability space and
T:X\to X
is invertible and preserves
\mu
. We recall that
\mathcal{I}_{T}
denotes the factor sigma-algebra of
T
-invariant subsets. Recalling that
M^{e}(X,T)
stands for the set of ergodic
T
-invariant probability measures on
(X,{\cal B}_{X})
, we can view the ergodic decomposition of
\mu
as a measurable map
x\mapsto\mu_{x}
from
X
to
M^{e}(X,T)
such that, for all
f\in L^{1}(\mu)
,

(87)

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{T}]=\int_{X}f\,d\mu_{x}\quad\text{($\mu$-a.e.)}

We denote by
\Pi(X,\mu,T)
the Pinsker factor sigma-algebra of
(X,\mu,T)
, that is the sub-sigma-algebra of all Borel subsets
A
of
X
such that
h_{\mu}(\mathscr{P}_{A},T)=0
, where
\mathscr{P}_{A}
is the partition of
X
into
A
and
X\setminus A
. For each ergodic component
\mu_{x}
, we also have an associated Pinsker factor sigma-algebra
\Pi(X,\mu_{x},T)
. The following proposition clarifies the relationship between these Pinsker factors.

Proposition 10.1
.

There exists a countably generated sub-sigma-algebra
{\cal C}\subset{\cal B}_{X}
such that

•

\Pi(X,\mu,T)={\cal C}\mod\mu
;

•

for almost every ergodic component
\mu_{x}
,
\Pi(X,\mu_{x},T)={\cal C}\mod\mu_{x}
.

Moreover, for all
f\in L^{1}(\mu)
, we have
\mu
-almost surely

(88)

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\cal C}](x)={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{x}}[f\,|\,{\cal C}](x).

For a factor sub-sigma-algebra
{\cal A}\subset{\cal B}_{X}
, we can also consider the corresponding Pinsker factor sub-sigma-algebra
\Pi(X,{\cal A},\mu,T)
which obviously satisfies:

\Pi(X,{\cal A},\mu,T)=\Pi(X,\mu,T)\cap{\cal A}.

We recall the following classical result: if
\mathscr{P}
is a
finite
partition of
X
, and if

{\cal A}=\bigvee_{n\in{{\mathbbm{Z}}}}T^{-n}\mathscr{P}
,
then

(89)

\Pi(X,{\cal A},\mu,T)=\bigcap_{m\in{{\mathbbm{Z}}}^{-}}\bigvee_{n\leq m}T^{-n}\mathscr{P}\mod\mu.

We will also need two other facts, which we state as lemmas below.

Lemma 10.2
.

Let
{\cal A}
and
{\cal B}
be two factor sub-sigma-algebras, and assume that
{\cal B}\subset\Pi(X,\mu,T)
. Then

\Pi\bigl(X,{\cal A}\vee{\cal B},\mu,T\bigr)=\Pi(X,{\cal A},\mu,T)\vee\Pi(X,{\cal B},\mu,T)=\Pi(X,{\cal A},\mu,T)\vee{\cal B}\mod\mu.

(Note that the result is not true in general if we do not assume that one of the two factors has zero entropy: any zero entropy system can be seen as a factor of a joining of two Bernoulli shifts, cf.
[
34
]
.)

Proof.

The inclusion
\Pi(X,{\cal A},\mu,T)\vee\Pi(X,{\cal B},\mu,T)\subset\Pi\bigl(X,{\cal A}\vee{\cal B},\mu,T\bigr)
is clear, and does not require that one of the two factors be of zero entropy.
For the reverse inclusion, let us consider
f\in L^{2}(X,{\cal A},\mu)
,
g\in L^{2}(X,{\cal B},\mu)
, and let
h
be bounded and measurable with respect to
\Pi(X,{\cal A}\vee{\cal B},\mu,T)
. Since the factor generated by
g
and
h
has zero entropy, using Theorem
2.13
, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[fgh]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A},\mu,T)]\,gh\bigr],

and this shows that

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[fg\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}\vee{\cal B},\mu,T)\bigr]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A},\mu,T)]\,g\quad\mu\text{-a.e.}

In particular,
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[fg\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}\vee{\cal B},\mu,T)\bigr]
is measurable with respect to

\Pi(X,{\cal A},\mu,T)\vee\Pi(X,{\cal B},\mu,T).

This remains true if we replace
fg
by a finite linear combination of
f_{i}g_{i}
’s, where each
f_{i}\in L^{2}(X,{\cal A},\mu)
and each
g_{i}\in L^{2}(X,{\cal B},\mu)
. Then by the density of these linear combinations we get that
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[h\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}\vee{\cal B},\mu,T)\bigr]
is measurable with respect to
\Pi(X,{\cal A},\mu,T)\vee\Pi(X,{\cal B},\mu,T)
for any
h\in L^{2}(X,{\cal A}\vee{\cal B},\mu)
. Now, we conclude by taking
h
measurable with respect to
\Pi(X,{\cal A}\vee{\cal B},\mu,T)
.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
({\cal A}_{k})_{k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}
be an increasing sequence of factor sub-sigma-algebras, and let
{\cal A}:=\bigvee_{k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}{\cal A}_{k}
.
Then

\Pi(X,{\cal A},\mu,T)=\bigvee_{k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu,T)\mod\mu.

Proof.

Again, the inclusion
\bigvee_{k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu,T)\subset\Pi(X,{\cal A},\mu,T)
is clear.
For the converse, let us take
f\in L^{2}(X,\Pi(X,{\cal A},\mu,T),\mu)
. Since
f
is
{\cal A}
-measurable, the martingale theorem tells us that, in
L^{2}(\mu)
,

f=\lim_{k\to\infty}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{k}].

But
f
is measurable with respect to a zero-entropy factor, thus, in view of Theorem
2.13
, for any
k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}
and any
g\in L^{2}(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu)
, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[fg]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[f\,{{\mathbbm{E}}}[g\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu)]\bigr],

and it follows that

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\cal A}_{k}]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu)]\quad\mu\text{-a.e.}

Therefore,
f
is the
L^{2}
-limit of a sequence
(f_{k})
, where each
f_{k}
is
L^{2}(\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu))
-measurable.

Of course, the preceding results are also true if we replace
\mu
by any ergodic component
\mu_{x}
.

Remark 10.4
.

Lemmas
10.3
and
10.2
remain valid if we replace everywhere the Pinsker factor by the largest
{\cal F}
-factor for an arbitrary characteristic class
{\cal F}
. Indeed, their proofs only use the fact that ZE is a characteristic class, and that the Pinsker factor is the largest ZE-factor.

We will also need the following result about conditional expectations.

Lemma 10.5
.

For any bounded measurable function
\phi
and any finite partition
\mathscr{Q}
of
X
, we have for
\mu
-almost every
x\in X

(90)

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\left[\phi\,\left|\,\mathcal{I}_{T}\vee\mathscr{Q}\right.\right](x)={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{x}}\left[\phi\,\left|\,\mathscr{Q}\right.\right](x).

Proof.

Since
\mathscr{Q}
is a finite partition, the RHS is

(91)

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\text{ atom of }\mathscr{Q},\\
\mu_{x}(B)>0\end{subarray}}\mathbbmss{1}_{B}(x)\frac{1}{\mu_{x}(B)}\int_{B}\phi\,d\mu_{x}.

As
x\mapsto\mu_{x}
is
\mathcal{I}_{T}
-measurable, the above function of
x
is
\mathcal{I}_{T}\vee\mathscr{Q}
-measurable. To verify that it corresponds to the conditional expectation on the LHS of (
90
), we have to multiply it (i.e. (
91
)) by
\mathbbmss{1}_{C}\,h
for some atom
C
of
\mathscr{Q}
and some bounded
\mathcal{I}_{T}
-measurable function
h
, and integrate with respect to
\mu
. We get

\displaystyle\int_{X}\mathbbmss{1}_{C}(x)\,h(x)\,{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{x}}\left[\phi\,\left|\,\mathscr{Q}\right.\right](x)\,d\mu(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{X}\left(\mathbbmss{1}_{C}(x)\,h(x)\,\frac{1}{\mu_{x}(C)}\int_{C}\phi\,d\mu_{x}\right)\,d\mu(x)

But
h
and
x\mapsto\mu_{x}
are both
\mathcal{I}_{T}
-measurable, therefore in the last integral we can replace
\mathbbmss{1}_{C}(x)

by
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\mathbbmss{1}_{C}\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{T}](x)
, which is equal to
\mu_{x}(C)
for
\mu
-almost every
x
. After cancellation with the denominator, we are left with

\int_{X}\left(h(x)\int_{C}\phi\,d\mu_{x}\right)\,d\mu(x)
, which is equal to

\int_{X}h(x)\,{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\mathbbmss{1}_{C}\,\phi\,|\,\mathcal{I}_{T}](x)\,d\mu(x)=\int_{X}h(x)\,\mathbbmss{1}_{C}(x)\,\phi(x)\,d\mu(x).

This achieves the proof of the lemma.

Proof of Proposition
10.1
.

We fix a countable family
(A_{k})_{k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}
of Borel subsets of
X
that separates points, and we call
\mathscr{P}_{k}
be the finite partition of
X
generated by
A_{1},\ldots,A_{k}
. It follows that for any probability measure
\nu
on
X
, we have

{\cal B}_{X}=\bigvee_{k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}\mathscr{P}_{k}\mod\nu.

We define an increasing sequence
({\cal A}_{k})_{k\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}
of factor sub-sigma-algebras by setting

(92)

{\cal A}_{k}:=\mathcal{I}_{T}\vee\bigvee_{n\in{{\mathbbm{Z}}}}T^{-n}\mathscr{P}_{k}.

We also fix a countable set
\Phi=(\phi_{j})_{j\in{{\mathbbm{N}}}}
of bounded, Borel-measurable functions on
X
satisfying the following:
for any probability measure
\nu
on
X
,
\Phi
is dense in
L^{2}(X,\nu)
. There are several ways to get such a set: if
X
is compact, we can take a countable dense set in
C(X)
. We can also take
\Phi
as the set of all finite linear combinations with rational coefficients of
\mathbbmss{1}_{A_{i}}
where the
A_{i}
’s are atoms of a partition
\mathscr{P}_{k}
for some
k
.

We first establish the conclusion of the proposition for
\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu,T)
and
\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu_{x},T)
.
As
\Phi
is dense in
L^{2}(X,\mu)
, the Pinsker factor
\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu,T)
coincides modulo
\mu
with the sigma-algebra generated by all conditional expectations
of the form
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\phi\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu,T)]
(
\phi\in\Phi
).
From (
89
), (
92
) and Lemma
10.2
, we get

\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu,T)=\mathcal{I}_{T}\vee\bigcap_{m\in{{\mathbbm{Z}}}^{-}}\bigvee_{n\leq m}T^{-n}\mathscr{P}_{k}\mod\mu.

For integers
\ell\leq m\leq 0
, let us denote
\mathscr{Q}_{\ell,m}
the finite partition
\bigvee_{\ell\leq n\leq m}T^{-n}\mathscr{P}_{k}
.
By application of the reverse martingale theorem and the martingale theorem, we have

(93)

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[\phi\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu,T)\bigr]=\lim_{m\to-\infty}\lim_{\ell\to-\infty}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\left[\phi\ \left|\ \mathcal{I}_{T}\vee\mathscr{Q}_{\ell,m}\right.\right],

where all limits are pointwise, and exist for
\mu
-a.a.
x\in X
.

We can make the same analysis in the ergodic system
(X,\nu,T)
for any
T
-invariant ergodic measure
\nu
: the Pinsker factor
\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\nu,T)
coincides modulo
\nu
with the sigma-algebra generated by all conditional expectations
of the form
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\nu}[\phi\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\nu,T)]
when
\phi
runs over
\Phi
. Again, from (
89
) and Lemma
10.2
, we get

\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\nu,T)=\mathcal{I}_{T}\vee\bigcap_{m\in{{\mathbbm{Z}}}^{-}}\bigvee_{n\leq m}T^{-n}\mathscr{P}_{k}=\bigcap_{m\in{{\mathbbm{Z}}}^{-}}\bigvee_{n\leq m}T^{-n}\mathscr{P}_{k}\mod\nu.

(For the last equality, we used the ergodicity of
\nu
which ensures that
\mathcal{I}_{T}
is trivial under
\nu
.) For any
\phi\in\Phi
, we then have
\nu
-a.e.

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\nu}\bigl[\phi\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\nu,T)\bigr]=\lim_{m\to-\infty}\lim_{\ell\to-\infty}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\nu}\left[\phi\ \left|\ \mathscr{Q}_{\ell,m}\right.\right].

In particular, the above is true for
\nu=\mu_{y}
, for
\mu
-almost all
y
, and we can write this by making the variables explicit: for
\mu
-almost all
y
,
\mu_{y}
gives full measure to the set of
x\in X
satisfying

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{y}}\bigl[\phi\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu_{y},T)\bigr](x)=\lim_{m\to-\infty}\lim_{\ell\to-\infty}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{y}}\left[\phi\ \left|\ \mathscr{Q}_{\ell,m}\right.\right](x).

But we can also observe that, for
\mu
-almost all
y
,
\mu_{y}
gives full measure to the set of
x\in X
satisfying
\mu_{x}=\mu_{y}
. We get: for
\mu
-almost all
y
,
\mu_{y}(A)=1
, where

A:=\Bigl\{x\in X:{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{x}}\bigl[\phi\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu_{x},T)\bigr](x)=\lim_{m\to-\infty}\lim_{\ell\to-\infty}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{x}}\left[\phi\ \left|\ \mathscr{Q}_{\ell,m}\right.\right](x)\Bigr\}.

It follows that the above set
A
satisfies
\mu(A)=1
.
Now, take
x\in A
such that the conclusion of Lemma
10.5
is true at
x
for all partitions
\mathscr{Q}_{\ell,m}
, and for which (
93
) holds (the set of such
x
has
\mu
-measure 1). Then, we have

\displaystyle{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{x}}

\displaystyle\bigl[\phi\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu_{x},T)\bigr](x)

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to-\infty}\lim_{\ell\to-\infty}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{x}}\left[\phi\ \left|\ \mathscr{Q}_{\ell,m}\right.\right](x)\quad(\text{as $x\in A$})

\displaystyle=\lim_{m\to-\infty}\lim_{\ell\to-\infty}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\left[\phi\ \left|\ \mathcal{I}_{T}\vee\mathscr{Q}_{\ell,m}\right.\right](x)\quad(\text{by Lemma~\ref{lemma:c_exp}})

\displaystyle={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[\phi\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu,T)\bigr](x)\quad(\text{by~\eqref{eq:convpinsker}}).

Now, for each
\phi\in\Phi
, we can fix a Borel-measurable (defined everywhere) version
c_{k}^{\phi}
of the conditional expectation
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[\phi\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu,T)\bigr]
. The above equalities ensure that for
\mu
-almost every
x
, we have for all
\phi\in\Phi
,

(94)

c_{k}^{\phi}(x)={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{x}}\bigl[\phi\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu_{x},T)\bigr](x)={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}\bigl[\phi\,|\,\Pi(X,{\cal A}_{k},\mu,T)\bigr](x).

To conclude the proof for the factor
{\cal A}_{k}
, all that remains is to define
{\cal C}_{k}
as the sub-sigma-algebra generated by the countable family of functions
c_{k}^{\phi}
,
\phi\in\Phi
.

The general case follows from a straightforward application of Lemma
10.3
. We can obtain the required sub-sigma-algebra
{\cal C}
by setting

{\cal C}:=\bigvee_{k\geq 1}{\cal C}_{k}=\sigma\Bigl(\bigl(c_{k}^{\phi}\bigl)_{k\geq 1,\phi\in\Phi}\Bigr).

Now, it remains to prove (
88
). By Lemma
10.3
together with an application of the martingale convergence theorem, we can pass to the limit as
k\to\infty
in (
94
) to get the validity of (
88
) for
f\in\Phi
. Let us consider the case
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
: then there exists a sequence
(\phi_{\ell})
of elements of
\Phi
converging in
L^{1}
to
f
, and it can be arranged so that
\|\phi_{\ell}\|_{\infty}\leq\|f\|_{\infty}+1
for all
\ell
. Then, we also have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\phi_{\ell}\,|\,{\cal C}]\xrightarrow[\ell\to\infty]{}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\cal C}]\quad\text{ in }L^{1}(\mu).

Passing to a subsequence if necessary, we can also assume that the convergence of
\phi_{\ell}
to
f
and the convergence of
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[\phi_{\ell}\,|\,{\cal C}]
to
{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\cal C}]
holds pointwise,
\mu
-almost-surely. But since we already know that (
88
) is valid in
\Phi
, we get that for
\mu
-almost every
x
,

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{x}}[\phi_{\ell}\,|\,{\cal C}](x)\xrightarrow[\ell\to\infty]{}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\cal C}](x).

Then, for
\mu
-almost every
y\in X
, we have
\mu_{y}(B_{y})=1
, where
B_{y}
is the set of
x\in X
satisfying:

•

\mu_{x}=\mu_{y}
,

•

\phi_{\ell}(x)\xrightarrow[\ell\to\infty]{}f(x)
,

•

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{x}}[\phi_{\ell}\,|\,{\cal C}](x)\xrightarrow[\ell\to\infty]{}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\cal C}](x)
.

But, for any bounded
{\cal C}
-measurable function
g
, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{y}}[\phi_{\ell}g]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{y}}\Bigl[{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{y}}[\phi_{\ell}\,|\,{\cal C}]\,g\Bigr].

If
\mu_{y}(B_{y})=1
, we can pass to the limit as
\ell\to\infty
and we get by the dominated convergence theorem that

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{y}}[fg]={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{y}}\Bigl[{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\cal C}]\,g\Bigr],

which ensures that for
\mu_{y}
-almost every
x
, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{x}}[f\,|\,{\cal C}](x)={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{y}}[f\,|\,{\cal C}](x)={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\cal C}](x).

This proves the validity of (
88
) for
f\in L^{\infty}(\mu)
. For a general
f\in L^{1}(\mu)
, we can assume w.l.o.g. that
f
takes its values in
{{\mathbbm{R}}}_{+}
, and then
f
is the pointwise limit of the non-decreasing sequence
(f_{n})_{n\geq 1}
where for all
x
,
f_{n}(x):=\min\{n,f(x)\}
. Then, since each
f_{n}
satisfy (
88
), we get as an easy consequence of the monotone convergence theorem that, for
\mu
-almost every
x
, we have

{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{x}}[f\,|\,{\cal C}](x)=\lim_{n}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu_{x}}[f_{n}\,|\,{\cal C}](x)=\lim_{n}{{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f_{n}\,|\,{\cal C}](x)={{\mathbbm{E}}}_{\mu}[f\,|\,{\cal C}](x).

This concludes the proof.
